convex and contained in a ball of radius
R
), with the following
properties:

•

(Bounded Sensitivity)
For any two adjacent datasets

\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}
and any
\theta\in\Theta
,

\lvert S(\theta,\mathcal{Y})-S(\theta,\mathcal{Y}^{\prime})\rvert\leqslant 1
.

•

(Quasi-Convexity)
For any fixed dataset
\mathcal{Y}
,
any
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta
, and any
0\leqslant\lambda\leqslant 1
, we have

S\big(\lambda\theta+(1-\lambda)\theta^{\prime},\mathcal{Y}\big)\leqslant\max\!\big(S(\theta,\mathcal{Y}),\,S(\theta^{\prime},\mathcal{Y})\big).

•

(Efficiently Computable)
For any given
\theta\in\Theta

and dataset
\mathcal{Y}
, we can compute
S(\theta,\mathcal{Y})
up to
error
\gamma
in

\operatorname{poly}\!\big(n,d,\log\tfrac{R}{r},\log{}^{-1}\big)

time for any
\gamma>0
.

•

(Robust algorithm finds low-scoring point)
For a given
dataset
\mathcal{Y}
, let

T=\min_{\theta_{0}\in\Theta}S(\theta_{0},\mathcal{Y})
.
Then we can find some point
\theta
such that for all
\theta^{\prime}

within distance
r
of
\theta
,
S(\theta^{\prime},\mathcal{Y})\leqslant T+1
, in
time
\operatorname{poly}\!\big(n,d,\log\tfrac{R}{r}\big)
.

•

(Volume)
For any given dataset
\mathcal{Y}
and

\eta^{\prime}\geqslant\eta
, let
V_{\eta^{\prime}}(\mathcal{Y})
represent the

d
-dimensional Lebesgue volume of points
\theta\in\Theta\subset\mdmathbb{R}^{d}

with score at most
\eta^{\prime}n
. (Note that
V_{1}(\mathcal{Y})
is the
full volume of
\Theta
.)

Then, we have a pure
\varepsilon
-DP algorithm
\mathcal{M}
on datasets
of size
n
, that runs in

\operatorname{poly}\!\big(n,d,\log\tfrac{R}{r}\big)
time, with the
following property. For any dataset
\mathcal{Y}
, if there exists

\theta
with
S(\theta,\mathcal{Y})\leqslant\eta n
and if

n\geqslant\Omega\Biggl(\max_{\eta^{\prime}:\,\eta\leqslant\eta^{\prime}\leqslant 1}\frac{\log\bigl(\tfrac{V_{\eta^{\prime}}(\mathcal{Y})}{V_{\eta}(\mathcal{Y})}\bigr)+\log\bigl(\tfrac{1}{\beta\cdot\eta}\bigr)}{\varepsilon\cdot\eta^{\prime}}\Biggr)\,,

then
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{Y})
outputs some
\theta\in\Theta
of score
at most
2\eta n
with probability
1-\beta
.

Remark 4.20

(Applying Robustness to Privacy to Bayesian Estimation)
.

We note that the robustness to privacy lemmas and theorems in
[
HKMN23
]
are stated for fixed
\theta^{*}
that is independent from the data. However, upon close inspection, the same lemmas and theorems hold for estimation of any fixed functions of the input variables as well. More precisely, let
f
denote any fixed function and
\{x_{1}^{*},\ldots,x_{n}^{*}\}
denote the uncorrupted samples. Then the same lemmas and theorems hold if
\theta^{*}
is replaced with any fixed function of the input variables
f(x_{1}^{*},\ldots,x_{n}^{*})
.

For proving computational lower bounds for differential privacy, we also need the following theorem reduction in the other direction, from privacy to robustness.

Theorem 4.21

(Privacy to Robustness Reduction, Theorem 3.1 in
[
GH22
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}:\mathcal{X}^{*}\to\mathcal{O}
be an
\varepsilon
-private map from datasets
\mathcal{X}^{*}
to outputs
\mathcal{O}
.
For every dataset
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
, let
G_{X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}}\subseteq\mathcal{O}
be a set of good outputs.
Suppose that
\mathcal{M}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})\in G_{X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}}
with probability at least
1-\beta
for some

\beta=\beta(n)
. Then, for every
n\in\mdmathbb{N}
, on
n
-element datasets
\mathcal{M}
is robust to adversarial
corruption of any
\eta(n)
-fraction of inputs, where

\eta(n)=O\biggl(\frac{\log(1/\beta)}{\varepsilon n}\biggr),

meaning that for every
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
and
X_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,X_{n}^{\prime}
differing on only
\eta n
elements,

\mathcal{M}(X_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,X_{n}^{\prime})\in G_{X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}}
with probability at least
1-\beta^{\Omega(1)}
.

4.5
Sparse spectral norm of matrices

For the analysis of algorithm for Bayesian linear regression, we use the sparse spectral norm of matrices.

Definition 4.22

(Sparse spectral norm of matrices)
.

Let
X\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{d\times n}
.
For an index set
S\subseteq[n]
, let
X_{S}\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{d\times|S|}
denote the submatrix formed by the
columns indexed by
S
. For
k\in[n]
define the
k
-sparse spectral norm

\|X\|_{\mathrm{sp}}(k)\;\coloneqq\;\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq[n]\\
|S|\leqslant k\end{subarray}}\ \|X_{S}\|_{\mathsf{op}}\,.

5
Robust and Private Bayesian Mean Estimation

In this section we prove our main results for Bayesian Gaussian mean estimation.
The model is as follows:

Definition 5.1

(Bayesian mean estimation under Gaussian prior)
.

A parameter
\mu
is drawn from a prior
\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
, and then
n
samples
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
are drawn i.i.d. from
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\textup{Id}_{d})
. Given these samples, our goal is to privately estimate the posterior mean
\mu_{\mathrm{post}}:=\E[\mu|x]
to
\ell_{2}
norm.

Our main positive result for this problem is
Theorem

1.1
, restated here for convenience:

Theorem

(Restatement of
Theorem

1.1
)
.

Given
\mu
drawn from prior
\pi=\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
, if the data
x
consists of
n\geqslant\tilde{\Omega}(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\log(\sqrt{\Tr(\Sigma)}/\alpha))
i.i.d. samples from
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\textup{Id})
, there is

1.

A computationally inefficient
\varepsilon
-DP estimator
\hat{\mu}(x)
such that with probability at least
1-\beta
,

\lVert\hat{\mu}(x)-\E[\mu\mid x]\rVert_{2}\leqslant\tilde{O}\biggl(\frac{\tr(\Lambda)+\lVert\Lambda\rVert_{\sf op}\log(1/\beta)}{\varepsilon n}\biggr)\coloneqq\tilde{O}(\alpha_{\rm stat})

(18)

2.

A
polynomial-time

\varepsilon
-DP estimator
\hat{\mu}(x)
such that with probability at least
1-\beta
,

\lVert\hat{\mu}(x)-\E[\mu\mid x]\rVert_{2}\leqslant\tilde{O}\biggl(\frac{\lVert\Lambda\rVert_{4/3}+\lVert\Lambda\rVert_{\sf op}\log^{3/4}(1/\beta)}{\varepsilon^{1/2}n^{3/4}}+\alpha_{\rm stat}\biggr)\coloneqq\tilde{O}(\alpha_{\rm comp}+\alpha_{\rm stat})\,.

(19)

where
\Lambda\coloneqq(\textup{Id}+\frac{1}{n}\Sigma^{-1})^{-1}
and
\lVert\Lambda\rVert_{4/3}
denotes that Schatten
4/3
-norm of
\Lambda
. Here the
\tilde{O}
conceals factors of
\log 1/\alpha
,
\log\log 1/\beta
, and
\log d
. When
\Sigma=\textup{Id}
, we do not lose
\log d
factors.

We will also show that the gap between the statistical and computational rates is fundamental, under the low-degree hardness framework:

Theorem 5.2

(Information-computation gap for Bayesian posterior mean estimation)
.

Consider the case of an isotropic prior
\mu\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}\textup{Id}_{d})
, and suppose that
\beta<\frac{1}{12}
,
d>2
, and
\alpha\leqslant\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}}{63}\sigma
. There is a distinguishing problem
\Pi
which is degree-
n^{o(1)}
hard such that, given a polynomial-time algorithm for private posterior mean estimation under prior
\textup{Id}_{d}
with

n=o\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\min(d,\log\frac{1}{\beta})}{\alpha^{4/3}\varepsilon^{2/3}}}}\right)

samples, there would be a polynomial-time distinguishing algorithm for
\Pi
.

5.1
Reformulation as private empirical mean estimation

To prove these results, we first give a straightforward reduction to the task of private
empirical mean
estimation.

Suppose without loss of generality that
\Sigma
is invertible (otherwise, we can simply change basis to exclude its kernel). By a standard calculation, there is a simple linear correspondence between the posterior mean and the empirical mean
\bar{x}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}x_{i}
:

\mu_{\mathrm{post}}=\Bigl(\textup{Id}+\frac{1}{n}\Sigma^{-1}\Bigr)^{-1}\bar{x}=:\Lambda\bar{x}\,.

Obtaining an estimate
\hat{\mu}
of the posterior mean in
L_{2}
norm thus corresponds to obtaining an estimate
\hat{x}
of the empirical mean in a certain Mahalanobis norm:

\|\hat{\mu}-\mu_{\mathrm{post}}\|_{2}=\|\Lambda(\hat{x}-\bar{x})\|=\|\hat{x}-\bar{x}\|_{\Lambda^{-2}}

Furthermore, note that after applying (known) linear transformation
\Lambda
to the dataset, this is equivalent to estimating the empirical mean of a dataset of samples from
\mathcal{N}(0,\Lambda^{2})
in
L_{2}
norm.

The remainder of this section will thus focus on this latter problem of empirical mean estimation for Gaussians of covariance
\Lambda^{2}
.
Theorem

1.1
follows directly (see
Section

A.1
for details) from the following upper bound:

Theorem 5.3

(Upper bounds for private empirical mean estimation)
.

Fix a public parameter
R>1
. For all
\mu\in\mdmathbb R^{d}
, given
n
i.i.d. samples from
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\Lambda^{2})
with empirical mean
\|\bar{x}\|_{2}
, if
\|\bar{x}\|_{2}\leqslant R
,

1.

There is a (computationally inefficient)
\varepsilon
-private estimator which uses at most

n=\tilde{O}\biggl(\frac{\mathrm{tr}(\Lambda)+\|\Lambda\|_{\mathrm{op}}\log(1/\beta)}{\alpha\varepsilon}+\frac{d\log(R/\alpha)}{\varepsilon}\biggr)\,,

then the estimator will output
\hat{x}
such that
\|\hat{x}-\bar{x}\|_{2}\leqslant\alpha
with probability at least
1-\beta
.

2.

There is a polynomial-time
\varepsilon
-private estimator which uses at most

n=\tilde{O}\biggl(\frac{\tr(\Lambda^{4/3})+\|\Lambda^{4/3}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\log(1/\beta)}{\alpha^{4/3}\varepsilon^{2/3}}+\frac{\tr(\Lambda)+\|\Lambda\|_{\mathrm{op}}\log(1/\beta)}{\alpha\varepsilon}+\frac{d\log(R/\alpha)}{\varepsilon}\biggr)\,,

then the same conclusion holds.

Here, the
\tilde{O}
hides log factors of
\alpha,n,d
. If
\Lambda
is proportional to
\textup{Id}_{d}
, then it only hides log factors of
\alpha,n
.

A simple bucketing argument on the eigenvalues of
\Lambda
shows that to prove these bounds for general
\Lambda
, it suffices to prove them in the special case where
\Lambda
is proportional to
\textup{Id}_{d}
, at the loss of a logarithmic factor in
d
(see
Section

A.2
for details).

The statistical rate in
Theorem

5.3
is optimal up to
\log 1/\alpha
factors, as improving this would violate existing lower bounds for private parameter mean estimation. More precisely, the last term in the rate is necessary by a standard packing argument, the
\tr(\Lambda)
term is necessary by
[
DJY+24
, Theorem 1.3]
, and the
\lVert\Lambda\rVert_{\sf op}\log(1/\beta)
term is necessary by the univariate lower bound of
[
KV17
, Theorem 6.2]
applied to the top eigenspace of
\Lambda
.

Additionally, we provide low-degree evidence that the computational rate in
Theorem

5.3
is optimal.

Theorem 5.4

(Information-computation gap for private empirical mean estimation)
.

Let
\beta<\frac{1}{3}
and
d>1
. There is a distinguishing problem
\Pi
(see
Section

5.4.1
) which is degree-
d^{o(1)}
hard such that if there were a polynomial-time
\varepsilon
-DP algorithm for empirical mean estimation for isotropic Gaussian data which achieves error
\alpha
with probability
1-\beta
using

n=o\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\min(d,\log\frac{1}{\beta})}{\alpha^{4/3}\varepsilon^{2/3}}}}\right)

samples, then there would be an efficient distinguishing algorithm for
\Pi
.

Although interesting in its own right, the above is a worst-case lower bound that does not suffice to prove
Theorem

5.2
, which holds even in the average case over
\mu\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\textup{Id}_{d})
. We show an average-case variant of this lower bound that holds for sufficiently small
\alpha
and
\beta
. A full proof is contained in

Section

A.1
.

5.2
Statistical rate for private isotropic empirical mean estimation

In this section, we prove the statistical bound in
Theorem

5.3
in the case where
\Lambda
is isotropic. Below, we first prove a statistical upper bound for
robust
empirical mean estimation for isotropic Gaussians, and then apply the privacy-to-robustness reduction of
[
HKMN23
]
.

Theorem 5.5

(Statistical rate for robust empirical mean estimation)
.

Suppose
0\leqslant\eta<1/2
is bounded away from
1/2
. There exists a (computationally inefficient)
\eta
-robust estimator that, for all
\mu\in\mdmathbb R^{d}
, given
n
i.i.d. samples from
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\textup{Id}_{d})
with empirical mean
\bar{x}
, outputs
\hat{x}
such that
\|\hat{x}-\bar{x}\|_{2}\leqslant\alpha
, with probability at least
1-\beta
, for

\alpha=O\biggl(\eta\sqrt{\log(1/\eta)}+\sqrt{\eta}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}\biggr)\,.

(20)

Note that this implies that to achieve error
O(\eta\sqrt{\log(1/\eta)})
in robustly estimating the empirical mean, it suffices to use
\tilde{O}(d/\eta)
samples, rather than the usual “parametric” rate of
\tilde{O}(d/\eta^{2})
. Additionally, observe that the error, as expected, tends to zero as
\eta\to 0
.

The proof of
Theorem

5.5
is a basic resilience-type argument
[
SCV17
]
:

Proof.

We will construct our estimator in the following way: let
x_{1},...,x_{n}
be the uncorrupted input samples with mean
\bar{x}
, and let
\tilde{x}_{1},...,\tilde{x}_{n}
be the corrupted input samples. Our estimator will search over vector-valued variables
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}
satisfying the following constraints:

•

|\{i:\tilde{x}_{i}\neq y_{i}\}|\leqslant\eta n

•

For all subsets
S\subseteq[n]
of size at most
2\eta n
:

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\frac{1}{|S|}\sum_{i\in S}(y_{i}-\bar{y})}}\right\|_{2}\leqslant C\sqrt{\frac{d+\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{\eta n}+\log\frac{1}{\eta}}

for sufficiently large absolute constant
C>0
, where
\overline{y}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}y_{i}
.
If the above conditions are infeasible, the estimator will output
\bot
. However, by standard concentration and a union bound over all
O(1/\eta)^{\eta n}
subsets
S
of size at most
2\eta n
, the constraints are satisfied by the ground truth
(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
.
Next, let
(y_{1},...,y_{n})
and
(y_{1}^{\prime},...,y_{n}^{\prime})
be two assignments which both satisfy the above constraints, and let
S=\{i:y_{i}\neq y_{i}^{\prime}\}
(where we note
|S|\leqslant 2\eta n
). Define
\overline{y}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}y^{\prime}_{i}
. We will prove that
\|\bar{y}-\bar{y}^{\prime}\|_{2}\leqslant O(\alpha)
for
\alpha
given in Eq. (
20
), which will imply that any assignment satisfying the above set of constraints will achieve the bound claimed in the theorem. Then

\displaystyle\|\bar{y}-\bar{y}^{\prime}\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\Biggr\|\sum_{i\in S}(y_{i}-y^{\prime}_{i})\Biggl\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\Biggl\|\sum_{i\in S}(y_{i}-\bar{y})-\sum_{i\in S}(y_{i}^{\prime}-\bar{y}^{\prime})+2\eta n(\bar{y}-\bar{y}^{\prime})\Biggr\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2C\eta\sqrt{\frac{d+\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{\eta n}+\log\frac{1}{\eta}}+2\eta\cdot\|\bar{y}-\bar{y}^{\prime}\|_{2}\,.

After rearranging and using the assumption
\eta
is bounded away from
1/2
, we conclude that
\|\bar{y}-\bar{y}^{\prime}\|_{2}\leqslant O(\alpha)
as desired.
∎

We now prove the statistical rate for private isotropic empirical mean estimation (first part of
Theorem

5.3
when
\Lambda=\textup{Id}
) by combining
Theorem

5.5
and
Section

4.4
.

Proof of first part of
Theorem

5.3
.

Let
\eta^{*}\coloneqq 1/3
, and let
\alpha(\eta)
be the error term in
Theorem

5.5
for
\eta<\eta^{*}
for robust empirical mean estimation, and for larger
\eta
let
\alpha(\eta)=R
.
Let
\alpha
be the target accuracy of the private algorithm, which we assume to be smaller than a sufficiently small constant, and let
\eta_{0}
be such that
\alpha(\eta_{0})=\alpha
.
Now from
Section

4.4
, there is a computationally inefficient
\varepsilon
-DP algorithm that takes
n
samples from
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\textup{Id})
and outputs
\hat{x}
such that
\lVert\hat{x}-\bar{x}\rVert_{2}\leqslant 2\alpha(\eta_{0})=2\alpha
with probability
1-2\beta
, as long as

n\geqslant\max_{\eta_{0}\leqslant\eta^{\prime}\leqslant 1}\frac{d\cdot\log\frac{2\alpha(\eta^{\prime})}{\alpha(\eta_{0})}+\log(1/\beta)}{\eta^{\prime}\varepsilon}\,.

(21)

This maximum is at most the sum of the maximums over
\eta_{0}\leqslant\eta^{\prime}\leqslant\eta^{*}
and
\eta^{*}\leqslant\eta^{\prime}<1
, that is,

\frac{d\log\frac{2\alpha(\eta^{*})}{\alpha}+\log(1/\beta)}{\eta_{0}\varepsilon}+\frac{d\log\frac{2R}{\alpha}+\log(1/\beta)}{\varepsilon}\,.

Note that because
\eta_{0}
is such that
\alpha=O(\eta_{0}\sqrt{\log 1/\eta_{0}}+\sqrt{\frac{\eta_{0}d}{n}})
, we must have
\eta_{0}=\tilde{\Omega}(\min\{\alpha,\alpha^{2}n/d\})
. Substituting this into Eq. (
21
) yields the claimed bound. ∎

5.3
Computational rate for private isotropic empirical mean estimation

Here we will prove the computational bound in Theorem
5.3
in the case where
\Lambda
is isotropic. We will first develop an efficient sum-of-squares algorithm for robust empirical mean estimation, and then apply the privacy-to-robustness reduction.

5.3.1
Efficient robust empirical mean estimation

Theorem 5.6
.

Let
\eta>0
be smaller than a sufficiently small constant. There is an efficient
\eta
-robust estimator such that, for all
\mu\in\mdmathbb R^{d}
, given
n
i.i.d. samples from
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\textup{Id}_{d})
such that the uncorrupted samples have empirical mean
\bar{x}
, the estimator will output an
\hat{x}
such that
\|\hat{x}-\bar{x}\|_{2}\leqslant\alpha
except with probability at most
\beta
for

\alpha\leqslant O\Biggr(\eta\sqrt{\log 1/\eta}+\sqrt{\eta\sqrt{\frac{d+\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{n}}}\Bigg)\,.

Proof.

The proof follows from a careful analysis of the algorithm in
[
KMZ22
]
.
Let
\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
be values to be determined later.
Let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
denote the observed samples, an
\eta
fraction of which have been corrupted. Let
x^{\circ}_{1},\ldots,x^{\circ}_{n}
denote the original, uncorrupted samples, which we do not have access to and which have empirical mean
\bar{x}
.
We consider the following set of polynomial constraints in the indeterminates
w_{1},\dots,w_{n}
(indicating our guesses for which points are uncorrupted), and
x_{1}^{\prime},\dots,x_{n}^{\prime}
:

\mathcal{A}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} &\forall i\in[n]:&w_{i}&=w_{i}^{2}\\
&\forall i\in[n]:&w_{i}x_{i}&=w_{i}x_{i}^{\prime}\\
&&\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}&\geqslant(1-\eta)n\\
&\forall v\in\mdmathbb{S}^{d-1}:&\E_{i}\langle x_{i}^{\prime}-\mu^{\prime},v\rangle^{2}&\leqslant(1+\alpha_{0})\end{aligned}}}\right\}

where
\mu^{\prime}=\E_{i}x_{i}^{\prime}
.
Then the algorithm is as follows:

Algorithm 1

(Robust algorithm for estimating the empirical mean)
.

Input:

\eta
-corrupted samples
x_{1},\ldots x_{n}\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{d}

Output:
a polynomial time estimate
\hat{x}\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{d}
for the empirical mean

1.

Solve the degree-
O(1)
SoS relaxation of the constraint system
\mathcal{A}(x^{\prime},w;x)
, and obtain a pseudo-expectation operator
\tilde{\E}
.

2.

Set
\hat{x}\leftarrow\tilde{\E}[\mu^{\prime}]
.

3.

Return

\hat{x}
.

Suppose we know that for all vectors
v\in\mdmathbb{S}^{d-1}
and
b\in[0,1]^{n}
such that
\E_{i}b_{i}\geqslant 1-\eta
, the bounds

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\E_{i}b_{i}\langle x_{i}^{\circ}-\bar{x},v\rangle}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leqslant\alpha_{1}

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\E_{i}b_{i}\langle x_{i}^{\circ}-\bar{x},v\rangle^{2}-1}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leqslant\alpha_{2}

hold.
Then tracking the error terms in the proof of
[
KMZ22
, Lemma 4.1]
gives us that if
\tilde{\mdmathbb{E}}
is a degree-
O(1)
pseudoexpectation that satisfies
\mathcal{A}
, then

\lVert\tilde{\mdmathbb{E}}\mu^{\prime}-\bar{x}\rVert_{2}\leqslant O(\alpha_{1}+\sqrt{\eta\cdot(\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{2})+\eta^{2}})\,.

We include a proof of this statement in
Section

A.4
(see
Section

A.4
).
Now it suffices to set
\{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\}
. Applying
Section

5.3.1
, since
n\geqslant d+\log(1/\beta)
, we can take

\displaystyle\alpha_{0}=\alpha_{2}

\displaystyle=O\Bigl(\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}+\eta\log(1/\eta)\Bigr)\,,

\displaystyle\text{ and }\alpha_{1}

\displaystyle=O\Bigl(\sqrt{\frac{\eta(d+\log(1/\beta))}{n}}+\eta\sqrt{\log(1/\eta)}\Bigr)\,.

Substituting these choices of
\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
, finishes the proof.
For feasibility, note that pseudoexpectations generalize expectations which generalize masses on single points. In order to verify feasibility it suffices to take
\tilde{\mdmathbb{E}}
to be the pseudoexpectation that assigns each
x_{i}^{\prime}
to
x_{i}^{\circ}
and sets each
w_{i}
equal to
1
if that sample is uncorrupted and to
0
otherwise.
∎

Lemma 5.7

(Stability)
.

Let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
be i.i.d. samples from
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\textup{Id}_{d})
and
\bar{x}
be their empirical mean. Then with probability
1-\beta
, for all vectors
v\in\mdmathbb{S}^{d-1}
and
b\in[0,1]^{n}
such that
\E_{i}b_{i}\geqslant 1-\eta
, we have

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\E_{i}b_{i}\langle x_{i}-\bar{x},v\rangle}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leqslant O\Bigl(\sqrt{\frac{\eta(d+\log(1/\beta))}{n}}+\eta\sqrt{\log(1/\eta)}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\E_{i}b_{i}\langle x_{i}-\bar{x},v\rangle^{2}-1}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leqslant O\Bigl(\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}+\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}+\eta\log(1/\eta)\Bigr)

We prove the bound in
Section

A.3
.

5.3.2
Robustness-to-privacy reduction

In this section we apply the robustness to privacy reduction and prove the efficient isotropic case of
Theorem

5.3
. Our goal is to apply
Theorem

4.19
. We follow the reduction in Section 5 of
[
HKMN23
]
closely. The program we used in the proof of
Theorem

5.6
is the same as the program used by
[
HKMN23
]
, the only difference being the accuracy guarantee we aim to obtain from the program. Their focus is on estimating the parameter mean of the distribution, while our focus is estimating the empirical mean of the uncorrupted samples. Despite this distinction, we can still instantiate the same reduction by using the same score function. Most of the properties required by
Theorem

4.19
— bounded sensitivity, quasi-convexity, efficient computability, efficient computability, and efficient finding of low-scoring points — follow automatically. It suffices for us to verify the accuracy guarantees and the volume ratios.

We now define the score function. First we need to define what it means for a candidate point to be a certifiable mean:

Definition 5.8

(
(\alpha,\tau,T)
-Certifiable Mean)
.

Let
x
be a set of inputs, we call a candidate point
\tilde{x}
an
(\alpha,\tau,T)
-certifiable mean
for the this set of inputs if there exists a linear functional
\mathcal{L}
that takes as inputs polynomials of degree
O(1)
in indeterminates
(x^{\prime},w,M)
with the following properties:
\mathcal{L}
satisfies

\lVert\mathcal{L}\mu^{\prime}-\tilde{x}\rVert_{2}\leqslant\alpha
and
\lVert\mathcal{L}\mu^{\prime}\rVert_{2}\leqslant 2R+\tau T
where
\mu^{\prime}=\E_{i}x_{i}^{\prime}
,
\lVert\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{L})\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}\leqslant R^{\prime}+n\tau
, for
R^{\prime}=\poly(n,d,R)
sufficiently large, where
\mathcal{R}
maps
\mathcal{L}
to its matrix representation.
Moreover,
\mathcal{L}
must

\tau
-approximately (as in
Section

4.3.3
) satisfy the following system of polynomial inequalities up to degree
6
.

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} &\forall i\in[n]:&w_{i}&=w_{i}^{2}\\
&\forall i\in[n]:&w_{i}x_{i}&=w_{i}x_{i}^{\prime}\\
&&\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}&\geqslant n-T\\
&&\E_{i}(x_{i}^{\prime}-\mu^{\prime}){(x_{i}^{\prime}-\mu^{\prime})}{}^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}+M{M}{}^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}&=(1+\alpha_{0})I\end{aligned}}}\right\}

We view that last equality as
k^{2}
many equalities.
Moreover, we choose
\eta
to be such that
\alpha=\alpha(\eta)=\Theta(\eta\sqrt{\log 1/\eta}+\sqrt{\eta\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}})
(as in
Theorem

5.6
), and we choose
\alpha_{0}=\alpha_{0}(\eta)
to be as in the proof of
Theorem

5.6
, as a function of
\eta
and consequently
\alpha
,
\alpha_{0}(\eta)=\Theta(\eta\log(1/\eta)+\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}})
.

Given the above definition the score function is defined as follows:

Definition 5.9

(Score Function)
.

Let
\alpha,\tau>0
.
Let
\mdmathbb{B}_{2}^{d}(2R+n\tau+\alpha)
denote the
\ell_{2}
ball of radius
2R+n\tau+\alpha
in
d
dimensions.
Given
x
be a set of inputs, and
\tilde{x}\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{d}
, we define the
score function

\mathcal{S}
over
\mdmathbb{B}_{2}^{d}(2R+n\tau+\alpha)
as

\mathcal{S}(\tilde{x};x,\alpha,\tau)=\min\{T\geqslant 0:\tilde{x}\text{ is an }(\alpha,\tau,T)\text{-certifiable mean with respect to }x\}\,.

Next we bound the volume ratios.

Lemma 5.10

(Volume of High and Low Scoring Points)
.

Assume
R=\Omega(\alpha)
is sufficiently large.
Let
\eta\geqslant 0
be at most a sufficiently small constant
\eta^{*}
.
Assume the set of input points
x
satisfy the properties of
Section

5.3.1
. Then:

1.

Every point in a ball of radius
\alpha(\eta)
around
\bar{x}
has score at most
\eta n

2.

Every point of score at most
\eta^{*}n
is contained in a ball of radius
O(\alpha(\eta^{*}))
.

Proof.

Note that since the set of the samples satisfy the properties of
Section

5.3.1
. The original program is feasible: consider the solution given by the point mass that assigns each
x_{i}^{\prime}
to the uncorrupted values. Therefore it suffices to take
\mathcal{L}
to be the pseudoexpectation corresponding to that assignment. Then the closeness constraint on
\mathcal{L}
, namely
\lVert\mathcal{L}\tilde{\mu}-\mu\rVert_{2}\leqslant\alpha
, will be satisfied, and since
R\geqslant\alpha
, the constraint
\lVert\mathcal{L}\mu\rVert_{2}\leqslant 2R+\tau T
, is satisfied. Therefore, every point in radius
\alpha(\eta)
must have score at most
\eta n
.

For the second part, note that since
\mathcal{L}
satisfies the constraint system, then from the guarantees of the robust algorithm we must have that
\lVert\mathcal{L}\mu^{\prime}-\bar{x}\rVert_{2}\leqslant O(\alpha(\eta^{*}))
. Additionally, by the closeness constraint on
\mathcal{L}
we know that
\lVert\mathcal{L}\mu^{\prime}-\tilde{\mu}\rVert_{2}\leqslant\alpha(\eta)\leqslant\alpha(\eta^{*})
. Triangle inequality gives us the desired result.
∎

As mentioned earlier, the rest of the requirements of
Theorem

4.19
are satisfied as the score function is the same as the one in
[
HKMN23
]
. Now we may prove the computationally efficient version of
Theorem

5.3
in the case where the prior is isotropic.

Proof of second part of
Theorem

5.3
.

Let
\eta
be such that
\alpha=\alpha(\eta)
, where
\alpha(\eta)
is as in
Theorem

5.6
.
From
Theorem

4.19
we know that it suffices for
n
to be such that

n\geqslant\Omega\Bigl(\max_{\eta^{\prime}:\eta\leqslant\eta^{\prime}\leqslant 1}\frac{\log V_{\eta}^{\prime}/V_{\eta}+\log(1/\beta\eta)}{\varepsilon\eta^{\prime}}\Bigr)\,.

We divide above into two parts,
[\eta,\eta^{*}]\cup[\eta^{*},1]
.
Now we apply
Section

5.3.2
.
From the first part it suffices to have

n\geqslant\Omega\Bigl(\frac{d\log(\alpha(\eta^{*})/\alpha(\eta))+\log(1/\beta\eta)}{\varepsilon\eta}\Bigr)\,.

From the second part it suffices to have

n\geqslant\Omega\Bigl(\frac{d\log(R/\alpha(\eta^{*}))+\log(1/\beta\eta)}{\varepsilon}\Bigr)\,.

Plugging in the definition of
\eta
and
\alpha(\eta^{*})
as a function of
\alpha,d,n,\beta
, it suffices to take
n
such that

n\geqslant\tilde{\Omega}\Bigl(\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{\alpha^{4/3}\varepsilon^{2/3}}+\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{\alpha\varepsilon}+\frac{d\log(R/\alpha)+\log(1/\beta)}{\varepsilon}\Bigr)\,,

hiding logarithmic factors in
\alpha
.
∎

5.4
Information-computation gap for Bayesian mean estimation

We might hope for a computationally efficient sum-of-squares algorithm that even matches the statistically optimal rate of
Theorem

5.5
for robust empirical mean estimation, so that we can efficiently apply the reduction of
[
HKMN23
]
to attain a statistically optimal rate for private empirical mean estimation. In this section, we will prove that this is not possible. We will first show a computational lower bound for the robust problem using the low-degree framework, and then a computational lower bound for the private problem via the privacy-to-robustness reduction proposed in
[
GH22
]
.

5.4.1
Hardness of robust empirical mean estimation

For simplicity, assume
\eta n
is an integer. To prove an information-computation gap for the robust problem, we will exhibit an efficient reduction from the following problem:

Definition 5.11

(
(\eta,\delta)
-Gaussian mixtures distinguishing problem)
.

Given
\eta,\delta>0
and
n,d\in\mdmathbb N
, consider the following two distributions:

•

Null distribution:
\mathcal{N}(0,\textup{Id}_{d})

•

Planted distribution: Sample
v
uniformly from the unit sphere. Then the planted distribution is
(1-\eta)\mathcal{N}(-\eta\delta v,\textup{Id}_{d})+\eta\mathcal{N}((1-\eta)\delta v,\textup{Id}_{d})
.

The problem is to distinguish between the two distributions to constant advantage, given
n
samples.

This is closely related to a distinguishing problem considered in
[
DHPT25
]
, and in
Section

5.4.1
at the end of this section, we verify that this problem is computationally hard for
\delta=\Theta(\alpha/\eta)
under the low-degree framework.

We now proceed to reduce from this problem to obtain a hardness result for empirical mean estimation.

Theorem 5.12

(Worst-case hardness of empirical mean estimation)
.

Let
\eta\in[0,1/3)
. Consider any polynomial-time estimator which uses
n\leqslant o(d\eta^{2}/\alpha^{4})
samples from
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\textup{Id}_{d})
to perform
\eta
-robust empirical mean estimation to error
\alpha
. If any such estimator succeeds with worst-case probability
\frac{2}{3}
for all
\|\mu\|\leqslant 21\alpha
, then there is a polynomial-time algorithm for the
(\eta,\Theta(\alpha/\eta))
-Gaussian mixtures distinguishing problem.

The same reduction will give us the following average-case lower-bound, which will be crucial in proving the hardness of private Bayesian mean estimation as the latter is inherently an average-case problem over the draw of
\mu
from the prior:

Lemma 5.13

(Average-case hardness of robust empirical mean estimation)
.

Let
\eta\in[0,\frac{1}{3})
and
\mu\sim 21\alpha v\cdot\text{Bern}(1/2)
where
v\sim\mdmathbb S^{d-1}
uniformly. Consider any polynomial-time estimator which uses
n=o\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d\eta^{2}}{\alpha^{4}}}}\right)
samples from
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\textup{Id}_{d})
to perform
\eta
-robust empirical mean estimation to error
\alpha
. If any such estimator succeeds with average-case probability
\frac{5}{6}
over the draw of
\mu
as described, then there is a polynomial-time algorithm for the
(\eta,\Theta(\alpha/\eta))
-Gaussian mixtures distinguishing problem.

Both of these results are immediate from the following reduction:

Lemma 5.14

(Estimation implies distinguishability)
.

Assume there is a polynomial-time
\eta
-robust empirical mean estimator that takes in
n
samples from
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\textup{Id})
where
\mu
is generated as either

•

\mu=0

•

\mu=21\alpha v
where
v\sim\mdmathbb S^{d-1}
uniformly.

and in either case outputs an estimate of the empirical mean to
\ell_{2}
accuracy
\alpha
with probability
1-\beta>2/3
. Then there is a polynomial-time algorithm that solves the
(\eta,\delta)
-Gaussian mixtures distinguishability problem from
Section

5.4.1
with
n
samples to constant advantage, for
\delta=21\alpha/\eta
.

Proof.

Our algorithm will be as follows: run the robust empirical mean estimator on the
n
samples to get an estimate
\hat{\mu}
, and let
\tilde{x}
be the mean of the input samples. We will guess “null” if
\|\hat{\mu}-\tilde{x}\|\leqslant\alpha
and guess “planted” otherwise. The idea is that, if we are drawing from the null distribution, clearly

\|\hat{\mu}-\tilde{x}\|\leqslant\alpha

with probability
1-\beta>2/3
(by the accuracy guarantee in the
\eta=0
case). However, if we are drawing from the planted distribution, we can view the “planting” process as an
\eta
-corruption of clean samples drawn from
\mathcal{N}(-\eta\delta v,\textup{Id}_{d})
; denote the empirical mean of these “clean samples” by
\bar{x}
. Furthermore, letting
X_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(-\eta\delta v,\textup{Id}_{d})
,
Y_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}((1-\eta)\delta v,\textup{Id}_{d})
, and
Z_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(\delta v,2\textup{Id}_{d})
, we see that
\tilde{x}-\bar{x}
is distributed as

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{\eta n}[Y_{i}-X_{i}]=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{\eta n}Z_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(\eta\delta v,\frac{2\eta}{n}\textup{Id}_{d})

It is not hard to find that

\sup_{\sigma>0}\mathop{\mdmathbb P}_{X\sim\mathcal{N}(v,\sigma^{2}\textup{Id}_{d})}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\|X\|_{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{10}}}\right]\leqslant\frac{1}{9^{d}\sqrt{\pi d}}<\frac{1}{10}

(from upper bounding the PDF of the Gaussian on the ball and the ball’s volume, plugging in the
\sigma
from first-order optimality, and then using Stirling’s approximation), so in particular we can rescale to get

\mdmathbb P\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\|\tilde{x}-\bar{x}\|_{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{10}\eta\delta}}\right]<\frac{1}{10}

Thus in the second case, a reverse triangle inequality between the above and the algorithm’s accuracy guarantee suffices to give

\displaystyle\|\hat{\mu}-\tilde{x}\|

\displaystyle\geqslant\|\bar{x}-\tilde{x}\|-\|\hat{\mu}-\bar{x}\|

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{10}\eta\delta-\alpha

\displaystyle>\alpha

except with probability
\frac{1}{3}+\frac{1}{10}<\frac{1}{2}
by a union bound, giving our distinguisher constant advantage.
∎

Note that an empirical mean estimator that beats the lower bound stated in
Theorem

5.12
would clearly meet the criteria for the estimator used in
Section

5.4.1
. Additionally, an estimator that beats the average-case lower bound in
Section

5.4.1
must succeed with probability
2/3
in each of the two cases, and thus can also be reduced to the testing problem by
Section

5.4.1
.

It remains to verify low-degree hardness of the
(\eta,\delta)
-Gaussian mixtures distinguishing problem:

Lemma 5.15
.

The degree-
D
advantage for the
(\eta,\delta)
-Gaussian mixtures distinguishability problem for
\delta=O(\frac{\alpha}{\eta})
given
n
samples is at most
O(1)
if
n\eta^{2}\geqslant 1
and
n\leqslant\Omega\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d\eta^{2}}{\alpha^{4}\mathrm{poly}(D)}}}\right)
.

Proof.

We will apply
Section

4.2
, setting
\kappa:=\eta^{2}
,
\tau:=\frac{1}{\delta^{4}}
, and
j_{*}:=2
therein.
The first condition in
Section

4.2
holds by construction, and for the second condition we need to show that for all
j\geqslant j_{*}
,

\displaystyle\bigl|\mathop{\mdmathbb E}_{X\sim A}h_{j}(X)\bigr|^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant j^{j}\kappa\tau^{-j/j^{*}}

Using
4.7
,

\displaystyle\bigl|\mathop{\mdmathbb E}_{X\sim A}h_{j}(X)\bigr|

\displaystyle=\bigl|(1-\eta)\mathop{\mdmathbb E}_{X\sim\mathcal{N}(-\eta\delta,1)}[h_{j}(X)]+\eta\mathop{\mdmathbb E}_{X\sim\mathcal{N}(\delta-\eta\delta,1)}[h_{j}(X)]\bigr|

\displaystyle\leqslant\bigl|(1-\eta)\cdot(-\eta\delta)^{j}/\sqrt{j!}\bigr|+\bigl|\eta\cdot(\delta-\eta\delta)^{j}/\sqrt{j!}\bigr|

and squaring both sides gives

\displaystyle\bigl|\mathop{\mdmathbb E}_{X\sim A}h_{j}(X)\bigr|^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\bigl|(1-\eta)\cdot(-\eta\delta)^{j}/\sqrt{j!}\bigr|^{2}+2\bigl|\eta\cdot(\delta-\eta\delta)^{j}/\sqrt{j!}\bigr|^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\bigl|(-\kappa^{1/2}\tau^{-1/4})^{j}/\sqrt{j!}\bigr|^{2}+2\kappa\bigl|\tau^{-j/4}/\sqrt{j!}\bigr|^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\kappa^{j}\tau^{-j/2}/j!+2\kappa\tau^{-j/2}/j!

\displaystyle\leqslant 4\kappa\tau^{-j/j^{*}}/j!

\displaystyle\leqslant j^{j}\kappa\tau^{-j/j^{*}}

as desired. This yields a degree-
D
lower bound for sample complexity

n\geqslant d^{j^{*}/2}\frac{\tau}{\kappa}\cdot\frac{1}{\poly(D)}=\Omega\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d\eta^{2}}{\alpha^{4}}}}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{\poly(D)}

as claimed.
∎

5.4.2
Hardness of private empirical mean estimation

We can use the privacy-to-robustness reduction of
[
GH22
]
along with
Theorem

5.12
to prove the computational lower bound of
Theorem

5.4
:

Proof of
Theorem

5.4
.

Let
p,q>0
to be set later, and suppose that we could perform private empirical mean estimation with
n_{\sf priv}
isotropic samples, where

n_{\sf priv}=o\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\min(d,\log\frac{1}{\beta})}{\alpha^{p}\varepsilon^{q}}}}\right)

Without loss of generality, say
\log 1/\beta=d
(otherwise we could decrease the dimension or increase
\beta
, which should make the problem easier). By the privacy-to-robustness reduction from
[
GH22
]
, restated as
Theorem

4.21
above, there is an
\eta
-robust empirical mean estimator with sample complexity
n_{\sf rob}=o\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d}{\alpha^{p}\varepsilon^{q}}}}\right)
for corruption fraction
\eta=\frac{d}{\varepsilon n_{\sf rob}}
.

Solving for
\varepsilon
and reorganizing variables, there is an
\eta
-robust empirical mean estimator with sample complexity

n_{\sf rob}=o\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d}{\alpha^{p}}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\eta n_{\sf rob}}{d}}}\right)^{q}}}\right)=o\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d^{1-q}\eta^{q}}{\alpha^{p}}n_{\sf rob}^{q}}}\right)=o\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d\eta^{q/(1-q)}}{\alpha^{p/(1-q)}}}}\right)

For
p=4/3
and
q=2/3
, this would contradict the computational hardness result of
Theorem

5.12
for robust empirical mean estimation.
∎

We can apply the same reduction to the average-case robust lower bound stated in
Section

5.4.1
to get an average-case private lower bound:

Corollary 5.16
.

Let
\beta<1/6
and assume
\mu=21\alpha v\cdot\mathrm{Ber}(1/2)
where
v\sim\mdmathbb S^{d-1}
. If there is a polynomial-time
\varepsilon
-DP estimator of the empirical mean that succeeds to error
\alpha
and confidence
\beta
given
n
samples from
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\textup{Id}_{d})
and has sample complexity

o\Bigg(\frac{\min\big(d,\log\frac{1}{\beta}\big)}{\alpha^{4/3}\varepsilon^{2/3}}\Bigg)\,,

then there is a polynomial-time algorithm for the
(\eta,\delta)
-Gaussian mixture distinguishing problem for
\eta=\Theta(\alpha^{4/3}/\varepsilon^{1/3})
and
\delta=\Theta(\varepsilon^{1/3}/\alpha^{1/3})
with
o(\frac{d}{\alpha^{4/3}\varepsilon^{2/3}})
samples, which is low-degree-hard.

Finally, we complete the proof of
Theorem

5.2
, which establishes an information-computation gap for the original question of private
Bayesian
mean estimation. The proof requires adapting the average-case lower bound of
Section

5.4.2
so that the distribution over
\mu
matches the Gaussian prior in the setting of
Theorem

5.2
.

Proof of
Theorem

5.2
.

We construct a coupling of
(X,Y)
with the following properties:

•

X
has marginal distribution
\mathcal{D}_{1}:=\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I)
.

•

Y
has marginal distribution
\mathcal{D}_{2}
that is
1/6
-close in TV distance to the prior.

•

(X,Y)
are jointly distributed as follows: first
X
is drawn according to
\mathcal{D}_{1}
. Then,
Y
is set to be equal to
X+21\alpha v
where
v\sim\mdmathbb S^{d-1}
uniformly and independently and
\alpha
is chosen appropriately.

Under this construction, by standard bounds relating TV to parameter distance for Gaussians, there is an absolute constant
c>0
such that the second property is satisfied provided
\alpha\leqslant c\sigma
.

The third property combined with
Section

5.4.2
shows that no
\varepsilon
-private estimator of the empirical mean using

o\Bigg(\frac{\min(d,\log\frac{1}{\beta})}{\alpha^{4/3}\varepsilon^{2/3}}\Bigg)

(22)

samples can be
\alpha
-accurate with probability more than
5/6
when
\mu
is drawn from
\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{D}_{1}+\mathcal{D}_{2})
. Indeed, suppose to the contrary that some efficient estimator achieves success probability
5/6
. Then by averaging, there must be some
x
such that conditioned on
X=x
, it succeeds with probability
5/6
over
\mu=x+21\alpha\nu\cdot\mathrm{Ber}(1/2)
. The guarantee in
Section

5.4.2
is clearly translation-invariant, so the existence of this estimator implies an efficient algorithm for the
(\eta,\delta)
-Gaussian mixture distinguishing problem, contradicting low-degree hardness.

The TV distance between
\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{D}_{1}+\mathcal{D}_{2})
and
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is at most
1/12
, so by triangle inequality, no
\varepsilon
-private estimator of the empirical mean with sample complexity given by Eq. (
22
) can be
\alpha
-accurate with probability more than
\frac{11}{12}
when
\mu
is drawn from the prior. Therefore we require
\beta<\frac{1}{12}
, and the connection between Bayesian mean estimation and empirical mean estimation elucidated in
Section

5.1
yields the desired lower bound for the former.
∎

5.5
Application to private Gaussian mean estimation under continual observation

As an application of our algorithm for private Bayesian mean estimation with Gaussian prior, we consider an online model of mean estimation under continual observation.:

Definition 5.17

(Private online mean estimation)
.

Let
\mu\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{d}
be an unknown parameter with
|\mu|\leqslant R
. Suppose we see a stream of
k
batches each of size
n
, where the samples are independently sampled from
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\textup{Id})
. After each successive batch, we must report an estimate of
\mu
given what we have seen so far, and the overall mechanism must be differentially private with respect to all
nk
samples encountered in the stream.

If the streaming algorithm can safely keep non-private state hidden from the public, the solution becomes simple: take

\varepsilon_{i}=\frac{\varepsilon}{i\log k}

and after each iteration publish an
\varepsilon_{i}
-private empirical mean estimate of the samples you have seen so far. By a simple calculation, if
n\geqslant\frac{d\log\frac{R}{\alpha}}{\varepsilon}
then with probability
1-\beta
, for all
i\in[k]

\|\hat{\mu}_{i}-\mu\|_{2}\leqslant\tilde{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sqrt{\frac{d+\log\frac{k}{\beta}}{n}}+\frac{d+\log\frac{k}{\beta}}{\varepsilon n}\cdot\log k}}\right)

However, the stronger notion of
pan-privacy
is often studied in the the streaming setting. In this model, in addition to seeing all of the outputs, the adversary can view the hidden state kept by the algorithm at
m
times (between processing of consecutive batches) of her choice. The commonly-studied problem is to estimate the empirical mean when the batch size is
1
and
x_{i}
are chosen arbitrarily from some bounded domain; in this setting, pan-privacy against
m
intrusions can be shown to require accuracy loss that degrades with
\sqrt{m}

[
DNPR10
]
. In contrast, we will show that in a stochastic setting—when
x_{i}
are drawn i.i.d. from a Gaussian rather than chosen arbitrarily, and for sufficiently large batch sizes—pan-privacy can be assured against
any
number of intrusions, since no hidden state is kept whatsoever between batches.

Although this task is frequentist as there is no prior on
\mu
, we will use our private Bayesian mean estimation algorithm through the following natural strategy.
We will start with the uniform improper prior over
\mdmathbb R^{d}
so that after each batch, conditioned on all samples seen thus far the posterior mean is simply the maximum likelihood estimate (MLE). At the start of every new batch, we have an estimate for the posterior conditioned on everything up to and including the previous batch, and we use this posterior as a prior upon which we perform a private Bayesian update using the samples in the new batch. Composition of the privacy-utility guarantee from Section
5
across batches yields the following guarantee for private online mean estimation:

Theorem 5.18
.

Let
\mu\in\mdmathbb R^{d}
be a mean parameter, unknown to the algorithm, satisfying
\|\mu\|\leqslant R
. Given a steam of
k
batches of i.i.d. samples
x_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,\textup{Id}_{d})
each of size
n>\frac{d\log\frac{R}{\alpha}}{\varepsilon}
, there is a computationally inefficient (resp. computationally efficient)
\varepsilon
-DP mechanism that outputs an estimate of
\mu
after every batch such that, with probability
1-\beta
, for all
t\in[k]
, the
t
-th estimate has error at most

\tilde{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sqrt{\frac{d+\log\frac{k}{\beta}}{nt}}+\frac{d+\log\frac{k}{\beta}}{\varepsilon n}}}\right)\,,\ \ \ \mathrm{resp.}\ \ \ \tilde{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sqrt{\frac{d+\log\frac{k}{\beta}}{nt}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d+\log\frac{k}{\beta}}{\varepsilon^{2/3}n}}}\right)^{3/4}+\frac{d+\log\frac{k}{\beta}}{\varepsilon n}}}\right)\,.

Proof.

In order to get a frequentist guarantee, we will start with the uniform improper prior (so after each iteration, the true posterior mean we are trying to estimate is just the maximum likelihood estimator). After each batch, we will use our previous posterior as a prior, and do a posterior mean estimate using the batch data. Assume as always that
n\geqslant\tilde{\Omega}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d\log\frac{R}{\alpha}}{\varepsilon}}}\right)
. We note that the posterior variance at step
t
is independent of the samples, and is defined by

\sigma_{t+1}^{2}=\bigg(n+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\bigg)^{-1}

which (subject to
\sigma_{0}^{-2}=0
) has solution

\sigma_{t}^{-2}=nt

The posterior mean update can be written as

\displaystyle\mu_{t+1}

\displaystyle=\mu_{t}+\frac{1}{1+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}n}}(\bar{x}_{t}-\mu_{t})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1+\sigma_{t}^{2}n}\mu_{t}+\frac{1}{1+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}n}}\bar{x}_{t}

Let
E_{t}
be an upper bound on the error of the posterior mean (or equivalently in this case, maximum likelihood) estimate at time
t
, and suppose that we can estimate the empirical mean of
n
samples in
d
dimensions with
\varepsilon
-privacy and
\beta
-confidence to error
C
.
Then we note that

\displaystyle E_{t+1}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{1+\sigma_{t}^{2}n}E_{t}+\frac{1}{1+\frac{1}{\sigma_{t}^{2}n}}\cdot C

\displaystyle=\frac{t}{1+t}E_{t}+\frac{1}{1+t}\cdot C

which implies that
E_{t}\leqslant C
for all
t\geqslant 1
.
Hence, using Theorem
5.3
, we can inefficiently (resp. computationally efficiently) estimate the MLE to error

\alpha\leqslant\tilde{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d+\log\frac{k}{\beta}}{\varepsilon n}}}\right)\,,\ \ \ \mathrm{resp.}\ \ \ \alpha\leqslant\tilde{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d+\log\frac{k}{\beta}}{\varepsilon n}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d+\log\frac{k}{\beta}}{\varepsilon^{2/3}n}}}\right)^{3/4}}}\right)\,.

The theorem then follows by the convergence of the MLE to the parameter mean in the Gaussian model.
∎

6
Robust and Private Bayesian Linear Regression

In this section, we prove our main results for Bayesian linear regression. The model is as follows:

Definition 6.1

(Bayesian regression under Gaussian prior)
.

A parameter
{w^{*}}\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{d}
is drawn from a prior
\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
, and then data is generated as follows. The columns of design matrix
{X^{\circ}}\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{d\times n}
are sampled i.i.d. from the distribution
\mathcal{N}(0,\textup{Id}_{d})
, and the response vector
{y^{\circ}}\sim\mathcal{N}({X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}{w^{*}},\textup{Id}_{n})
. Each sample is given by
({X^{\circ}}(\cdot,i),{y^{\circ}}(i))
, where
{X^{\circ}}(\cdot,i)
denotes the
i
-th column of the design matrix
{X^{\circ}}
. Given these samples, our goal is to privately estimate the posterior mean
w_{\rm post}\coloneqq\E[{w^{*}}\mid{X^{\circ}},{y^{\circ}}]
to
\ell_{2}
norm.

We will also consider the setting in which an
\eta
-fraction of the samples have been arbitrarily corrupted. In this case, privacy is defined with respect to the corrupted samples, but the posterior mean that we wish to estimate is with respect to the uncorrupted samples, even though the algorithm only gets access to the corrupted ones.

Our main positive results for this problem are algorithms in the special case where
\Sigma=\sigma^{2}\textup{Id}_{d}
. Specifically, we will prove the following slight strengthenings of
Theorem

1.4
.

Theorem 6.2

(Statistical rate for private, robust Bayesian linear regression)
.

Let
\Sigma=\sigma^{2}\textup{Id}_{d}
for known parameter
\sigma>0
.
Let
\beta\in[0,1],\varepsilon\leqslant O(1)
.
Suppose
n\geqslant\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{d\log(\sigma\sqrt{d})/\varepsilon,(d+\log(1/\beta))/\eta}}\right\}
.
Then there is a computationally inefficient algorithm that, given
\eta
-corrupted samples
(X_{\mathrm{input}},y_{\mathrm{input}})
generated as in
Section

6
, outputs an
\varepsilon
-DP estimator
\hat{w}
such that with probability at least
1-\beta
,

\lVert\hat{w}-\E[{w^{*}}\mid{X^{\circ}},{y^{\circ}}]\rVert_{2}\leqslant\tilde{O}(\eta+\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n\varepsilon})\,,

where
({X^{\circ}},{y^{\circ}})
are the uncorrupted samples.

Theorem 6.3

(Computational rate for private, robust Bayesian linear regression)
.

Let
\Sigma=\sigma^{2}\textup{Id}_{d}
for known parameter
\sigma>0
.
Let
\beta\in[0,1]
,
\varepsilon\leqslant O(1)
.
Suppose
n\geqslant\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{d\log(\sigma\sqrt{d})/\varepsilon,(d+\log(1/\beta))/\eta}}\right\}
.
There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given
\eta
-corrupted samples
(X,y)
generated as in
Section

6
, outputs an
\varepsilon
-DP estimator
\hat{w}
such that with probability at least
1-\beta
,

\lVert\hat{w}-\E[{w^{*}}\mid{X^{\circ}},{y^{\circ}}]\rVert_{2}\leqslant\tilde{O}\biggl(\sqrt{\eta\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}}+\eta+\frac{\Bigl(d+\log(1/\beta)\Bigr)^{3/4}}{n^{3/4}\varepsilon^{1/2}}+\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n\varepsilon}\biggr)\,,

where
({X^{\circ}},{y^{\circ}})
are the uncorrupted samples.

We note that the Bayes-optimal estimator achieves
\mathrm{MSE}(\hat{w})^{1/2}
, as defined in Eq. (
8
), of the order
(1+\frac{1}{n\sigma^{2}})^{-1}\sqrt{d/n}
.
It follows as a corollary that, under the condition that
\sqrt{d/n}\ll\eta+d/n\varepsilon
and
n\geqslant\Omega(d\log(\sigma\sqrt{d})/\varepsilon)
, one achieves the Bayesian optimal error rate asymptotically.

There are three different regimes for
\sigma
:

•

Overly strong prior
: When
\sigma\ll 1/\sqrt{n}
, private and robust estimation in this setting is trivial. The prior is sufficiently strong that one can ignore the samples and still asymptotically achieve the Bayes-optimal MSE simply by outputting the mean of the prior.

•

Overly weak prior
: When
\sigma\gg 1/\sqrt{n}
, private and robust Bayesian estimation reduces to private and robust
frequentist
estimation. The prior is sufficiently weak that the Bayes-optimal MSE is achievable asymptotically by ordinary least squares, though proving that this can be done optimally under privacy and robustness still takes some work.

•

Critical prior
: The most interesting regime is when
\sigma=\Theta(1/\sqrt{n})
, in which case one needs to combine information from the prior with information from the samples to compete with the Bayes estimator.

In this section we will focus on computationally efficient algorithms, deferring our proof of the statistical rate in
Theorem

6.2
to
Section

C.2.1
. In
Section

6.1
, we consider the critical prior regime, and in
Section

6.2
, we consider the weak prior regime.

Additionally, we provide low-degree evidence that the computational rate is optimal.

Theorem 6.4

(Information-computation gap for private Bayesian linear regression)
.

Suppose
\varepsilon\leqslant O(1)
and
\alpha\leqslant\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\eta d^{1/4}/D,\eta^{2}}}\right\}
.
There is a distinguishing problem
\Pi
(see
Section

6.3
) which is degree-
D
hard such that if there were a polynomial-time
\varepsilon
-DP algorithm for Bayesian linear regression with standard Gaussian design and
\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}\textup{Id})
prior that estimates the posterior mean to error
\alpha
with probability
1-\beta
using

n=o\biggl(\frac{d^{1/3}\log^{2/3}(1/\beta)}{\alpha^{4/3}\varepsilon^{2/3}\cdot D^{1/3}}\biggr)

samples, then there would be an efficient distinguishing algorithm for
\Pi
.

Note that when the failure probability
\beta
is exponentially small in
d
, this matches the rate achieved by
Theorem

6.3
, up to log factors. In fact, even for general
\beta
, we obtain a
bona fide
information-computation gap (
Remark

6.28
).

While the proof of the above lower bound also draws upon non-Gaussian component analysis like the one for Bayesian mean estimation, the former is more involved as the embedding of non-Gaussian component analysis into Bayesian linear regression is more delicate.
We defer the details to
Section

6.3
and
Section

6.4
.

6.1
Computational rate for private Bayesian linear regression with critical prior

Our main guarantees in the critical prior regime are as follows:

Theorem 6.5

(Computational rate under critical prior)
.

Let
\Sigma=\sigma^{2}\textup{Id}_{d}
for known parameter
\sigma=\Theta(1/\sqrt{n})
. There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given
\eta
-corrupted samples
(X,y)
generated as in
Section

6
, outputs an
\varepsilon
-DP estimator
\hat{w}
such that with probability at least
1-\beta
,

\lVert\hat{w}-\E[{w^{*}}\mid{X^{\circ}},{y^{\circ}}]\rVert_{2}\leqslant O(\alpha)\,,

whenever
\alpha\geqslant\eta\log(1/\eta)
,
\varepsilon\leqslant O(1)
and

n\geqslant\tilde{\Omega}\Bigl(\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{\alpha^{4/3}\varepsilon^{2/3}}+\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{\alpha\varepsilon}+\frac{d\log(\sigma\sqrt{d})}{\varepsilon}+\frac{(d+\log(1/\beta))\eta^{2}}{\alpha^{4}}\Bigr)\,.

We first give robust, non-private algorithms for estimating the posterior mean, and then apply the privacy-to-robustness reduction.

6.1.1
Efficient robust regression with critical prior

Here we give a robust, non-private algorithm for estimating the posterior mean when
\sigma=\Theta(\sqrt{1/n})
.

We will design a sum-of-squares estimator for
(\Sigma^{-1}+n\textup{Id}_{d})^{-1}{X^{\circ}}{y^{\circ}}
and then show that this is close to
w_{\mathrm{out}}
by concentration.
As discussed in
Section

2
, the key difference compared to our algorithm for Bayesian mean estimation is enforcing the existence of a short-flat decomposition for our estimate
y
for the true responses
{y^{\circ}}
.

Let
X_{\mathrm{input}},y_{\mathrm{input}}
denote the observed data, an
\eta
fraction of which have been corrupted. Let
{X^{\circ}},{y^{\circ}}
denote the original, uncorrupted data, which we do not have access to. We consider the following set of polynomial constraints in the indeterminates
\xi=(\xi(1),\ldots,\xi(n))
(indicating our guesses for which points are corrupted), and
X,y
. First, we have the constraints indicating that the
\xi(i)
’s try to select for corrupted points:

\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{corr}}(X,y,\xi;X_{\mathrm{input}},y_{\mathrm{input}})=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} \,&\xi\odot\xi=\xi,\quad\|\xi\|_{1}\leqslant\eta n,\\
&(1-\xi(i))\,X(i,\cdot)=(1-\xi(i))\,X_{\mathrm{input}}(i,\cdot),\ \forall i\in[n],\\
&(1-\xi(i))\,y(i)=(1-\xi(i))\,y_{\mathrm{input}}(i),\ \forall i\in[n]\end{aligned}}}\right\}\,.

(23)

Next, we have the resilience constraints that enforce that our estimates
X
for the uncorrupted covariates have bounded covariance, and that our estimates
y
for the uncorrupted responses admit a short-flat decomposition into a vector of indeterminates
z_{1}
with small
\ell_{2}
norm and a vector
z_{2}
with small
\ell_{\infty}
norm:

\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{res}}(X,y)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\frac{1}{n}XX^{\intercal}-\textup{Id}}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant\chi\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}},\\
&y=z_{1}+z_{2}\\
&\|z_{1}\|^{2}_{2}\leqslant\chi\eta n\log(1/\eta)+\chi\log(1/\beta),\\
&\eta n\|z_{2}\|_{\infty}^{2}\leqslant\chi\eta n\log(1/\eta)+\chi\log(1/\beta)\end{aligned}}}\right\}\,.

(24)

Here
\chi>0
is some fixed universal constant.

Finally, we have a constraint defining a variable that corresponds to our estimate for the posterior mean:

\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{post}}(X,y,w_{\mathrm{out}})=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{w_{\mathrm{out}}=(1/\sigma^{2}+n)^{-1}Xy}}\right\}\,.

(25)

With these constraints in place, we present our algorithm in
Algorithm

2
below.

Algorithm 2

(Robust algorithm for approximating posterior mean estimator)
.

Input:

\eta
-corrupted design matrix
X_{\mathrm{input}}\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{n\times m}
and responses
y_{\mathrm{input}}\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{n}

Output:
a polynomial time estimate
\hat{w}\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{d}
for the posterior mean

1.

Solve the degree-
O(1)
SoS relaxation of the constraints

\mathcal{A}(X,y,\xi,w;X_{\mathrm{input}},y_{\mathrm{input}})=\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{corr}}(X,y,\xi;X_{\mathrm{input}},y_{\mathrm{input}})\cup\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{res}}(X,y,w)\cup\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{post}}(X,y,w_{\mathrm{out}}),

obtaining a pseudo-expectation operator
\tilde{\E}
.

2.

Set
\hat{w}\leftarrow\tilde{\E}[w_{\mathrm{out}}]
.

3.

return

\hat{w}
.

First, we verify feasibility of the program
\mathcal{A}
. The proof ingredients for this are given in
Section

C.1
.

Lemma 6.6
.

The program constraints
\mathcal{A}(X,y,\xi,w;X_{\mathrm{input}},y_{\mathrm{input}})
are satisfied by the clean samples with probability at least
1-\beta
.

Proof.

The corruption constraints
\mathcal{A}_{\rm corr}
are satisfied by definition.
For the resilience constraints
\mathcal{A}_{\rm res}
, by
Theorem

C.1
, with probability at least
1-\beta
, the bound on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\frac{1}{n}{X^{\circ}}{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}-\textup{Id}}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}
holds.
On the other hand, the vector
y
is i.i.d Gaussian with variance bounded by
2
.
We can let
z_{1}
represent the
\eta n
entries in
y
with largest absolute values, and
z_{2}
the remaining entries.
By
Theorem

C.3
, we have

\lVert z_{2}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\leqslant\chi\log(1/\eta)+\frac{\chi\log(1/\beta)}{\eta n}\,.

By
Theorem

C.2
, we have

\lVert z_{1}\rVert^{2}_{2}\leqslant\chi\eta n\log(1/\eta)+\chi\log(1/\beta)\,.

Therefore the resilience constraint is satisfied with probability at least
1-\beta
.
∎

Next, we show that our algorithm outputs an accurate estimate of
(1/\sigma^{2}+n)^{-1}{X^{\circ}}{y^{\circ}}
:

Lemma 6.7
.

Consider the setting of
Theorem

6.5
.
Then with probability at least
1-\beta
,
Algorithm

2
outputs
\hat{w}\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{d}
such that

\|\hat{w}-(1/\sigma^{2}+n)^{-1}{X^{\circ}}{y^{\circ}}\|^{2}_{2}\leqslant O\Bigl(\eta^{2}\log^{2}(1/\eta)+\eta\log(1/\eta)\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}\Bigr)\,.

Proof.

Let
r\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{0,1}}\right\}^{n}
be the true indicator for the corrupted samples.
Then in the support of
(1-\xi)\odot(1-r)
, the program variables
X,y
are identical to the ground truth samples, i.e

\displaystyle X((1-\xi)\odot(1-r))

\displaystyle={X^{\circ}}((1-\xi)\odot(1-r))

\displaystyle(1-\xi)\odot(1-r)\odot y

\displaystyle=(1-\xi)\odot(1-r)\odot{y^{\circ}}\,.

We have

Xy-{X^{\circ}}{y^{\circ}}=X(y\odot v)-{X^{\circ}}({y^{\circ}}\odot v)\,.

where
v=1-(1-r)\odot(1-\xi)
indicates the points which are truly corrupted and/or were marked by
\xi
as corrupted.

Now we bound the two terms on the right-hand side separately.
For every unit vector
u
, using Cauchy-Schwarz, we have

\langle X(y\odot v),u\rangle^{2}\leqslant\lVert Xu\odot v\rVert^{2}\lVert y\odot v\rVert^{2}\,.

Now by the constraint
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{res}}(X,y,\xi)
, we have

\lVert y\odot v\rVert^{2}\lesssim\lVert z_{1}\odot v\rVert^{2}+\lVert z_{2}\odot v\rVert^{2}\leqslant\lVert z_{1}\rVert^{2}+\eta n\lVert z_{2}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\lesssim\eta n\log(1/\eta)+\log(1/\beta)\,.

Moreover, similar to the setting of empirical mean estimation where we invoked the isotropic estimation lemma (
Section

A.4
),
we have

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert Xu\odot v}}\right\rVert^{2}=\sum_{i}v_{i}\langle X(\cdot,i),u\rangle^{2}\leqslant\eta n\log(1/\eta)+\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}n\,.

We can bound
\langle{X^{\circ}}({y^{\circ}}\odot v),u\rangle^{2}
similarly, as the ground truth is a feasible solution. As a result, for every direction
u
, we have a degree-
O(1)
sum-of-squares proof that

\langle Xy-{X^{\circ}}{y^{\circ}},u\rangle^{2}\lesssim(\eta n\log(1/\eta)+\log(1/\beta))\cdot\Bigl(\eta n\log(1/\eta)+\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}n\Bigr)\,.

Taking pseudo-expectation of
w_{\mathrm{out}}=(1/\sigma^{2}+n)^{-1}Xy
, we get an estimator with the claimed error rate:

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle\leqslant O\Bigl(\eta\log(1/\eta)+\sqrt{\eta\log(1/\eta)\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}}\Bigr)\,.\qed

Finally, we show that our estimator is close to the posterior mean by concentration.

Lemma 6.8
.

Consider the setting of
Theorem

6.5
.
With probability at least
1-\beta
, we have

\lVert\E[{w^{*}}\mid{X^{\circ}},{y^{\circ}}]-(\Sigma^{-1}+n\textup{Id}_{n})^{-1}{X^{\circ}}{y^{\circ}}\rVert\lesssim\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}\,.

Proof.

The posterior mean of our regression model is given by

\E[{w^{*}}\mid{X^{\circ}},{y^{\circ}}]=\Bigl({X^{\circ}}{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}+\Sigma^{-1}\Bigr)^{-1}{X^{\circ}}{y^{\circ}}\,.

By
Theorem

C.1
, with probability
1-\beta
we have

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\frac{1}{n}{X^{\circ}}{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}-\textup{Id}_{n}}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}

Therefore, as a result we have

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert n(\Sigma^{-1}+n\textup{Id}_{n})^{-1}-n(\Sigma^{-1}+{X^{\circ}}{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal})^{-1}}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}\,.

Since with probability at least
1-\beta
we have
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\frac{1}{n}{X^{\circ}}{y^{\circ}}}}\right\rVert\leqslant O(\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}})
, the claim thus follows.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

6.5
.

Combining
Section

6.1.1
and
Section

6.1.1
with triangle inequality, we know that with probability at least
1-\beta
, we have

\lVert\hat{w}-(1/\sigma^{2}+{X^{\circ}}{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal})^{-1}{X^{\circ}}{y^{\circ}}\rVert^{2}\leqslant O\Bigl(\eta^{2}\log^{2}(1/\eta)+\eta\log(1/\eta)\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}\Bigr)\,.

Converting to sample complexity bound, we have the claim.
∎

6.1.2
Robustness-to-privacy reduction

In this section we apply the robustness to privacy reduction to prove
Theorem

6.5
. We will privatize our
Algorithm

2
.
We make the following definition for certifiable posterior mean for regression.

Definition 6.9

((
\alpha,\tau,T
)-certifiable posterior mean for regression)
.

Fix
R>0
and
\alpha,\tau>0
. For an input
(X_{\mathrm{in}},y_{\mathrm{in}})
, a vector
\widetilde{w}\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{d}

is
(\alpha,\tau,T)
-certifiable
if there exists a degree-
O(1)
linear functional
L
(a pseudoexpectation) over indeterminates
(X,y,\xi,w,w_{\mathrm{out}},M)
such that:

1.

Closeness/boundedness:

\lVert L[w_{\mathrm{out}}]-\widetilde{w}\rVert_{2}\leqslant\alpha
and
\lVert L[w_{\mathrm{out}}]\rVert_{2}\leqslant 2R+\tau T
.

2.

Approximate feasibility (degree-6):

L

\tau
-approximately satisfies the union of constraint families

\mathcal{A}\;=\;\underbrace{\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{corr}}}_{\text{corruption}}\ \cup\ \underbrace{\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{res}}}_{\text{design/residual stability}}\ \cup\ \underbrace{\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{post}}}_{w_{\mathrm{out}}=(1/\sigma^{2}+n)^{-1}Xy}\,,

concretely:

•

\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{corr}}
:
\xi\odot\xi=\xi
,
\|\xi\|_{1}\leqslant\eta n
and
(1-\xi(i))X(i,\cdot)=(1-\xi(i))X_{\mathrm{in}}(i,\cdot)
,
(1-\xi(i))y(i)=(1-\xi(i))y_{\mathrm{in}}(i)
for all
i
.

•

\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{res}}
:

\big\|\frac{1}{n}XX^{\intercal}-I\big\|_{\mathsf{op}}\lesssim\sqrt{(d+\log(1/\beta))/n}
and a decomposition
y=z_{1}+z_{2}
with

\|z_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim\eta n\log(1/\eta)+\log(1/\beta)
and
\|z_{2}\|_{\infty}^{2}\lesssim\log(1/\eta)+\log(1/\beta)/(\eta n)
.

•

\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{post}}
:
w_{\mathrm{out}}=(1/\sigma^{2}+n)^{-1}Xy
.

We choose
R=\Theta(\sigma\sqrt{d})
and take the slack inside
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{res}}
to be

\alpha_{0}(\eta)\;\asymp\;\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}\ +\ \eta\log 1/\eta\,.

Definition 6.10

(Regression score)
.

For
\widetilde{w}\in\mdmathbb{B}_{2}^{d}(\,2R+n\tau+\alpha\,)
, define

\mathcal{S}(\widetilde{w};X_{\mathrm{in}},y_{\mathrm{in}},\alpha,\tau)\;\coloneqq\;\min\bigl\{T\geqslant 0:\ \widetilde{w}\ \text{is $(\alpha,\tau,T)$-certifiable for }(X_{\mathrm{in}},y_{\mathrm{in}})\bigr\}.

Now we bound the volume ratio for different score values.

Lemma 6.11

(Low-score sets localize near
w_{\mathrm{out}}
)
.

There exists a universal
\eta_{\star}>0
such that, on the stability event from the resilience constraints (which holds with prob.
1-\beta
), the following hold simultaneously. Let

\alpha(\eta)^{2}\ \lesssim\ \eta^{2}\log^{2}1/\eta+\ \eta\log(1/\eta)\cdot\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}\,.

1.

Completeness (inner ball).
If
\lVert\widetilde{w}-w_{\mathrm{out}}\rVert_{2}\leqslant\alpha(\eta)
then
\mathcal{S}(\widetilde{w};\cdot)\leqslant\eta n
.

2.

Soundness (outer ball).
If
\mathcal{S}(\widetilde{w};\cdot)\leqslant\eta_{\star}n
then
\lVert\widetilde{w}-w_{\mathrm{out}}\rVert_{2}\lesssim\alpha(\eta_{\star})
.

Proof.

For (1), use the witness that puts point mass on the uncorrupted assignment consistent with
(X_{\mathrm{in}},y_{\mathrm{in}})
; the constraints are feasible and
L[w_{\mathrm{out}}]=w_{\mathrm{out}}
.
For (2), combine the SoS identifiability for regression under
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{res}}
with the closeness constraint of
Section

6.1.2
.
∎

We need to prove that our score function has value bounded by
n
for all candidate points
\tilde{w}
.

Lemma 6.12

(Well-definedness of the score function)
.

For any
\tilde{w}\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{d}
, the value of score function is bounded by
n
.

Proof.

For feasibility, we note that we can take
y=\mathbf{1}
, and the rest of the proof is similar to
[
ABMT25
, Lemma 6.15]
.
In particular, when the norm of
\tilde{w}
is bounded by
2R+\tau T
, we can set
X_{i}=\Bigl(\frac{1}{n\sigma^{2}}+1\Bigr)^{-1}\tilde{w}
.
Otherwise we project
\tilde{w}
to the ball of radius
2R+\tau T-\phi
for some
\phi
, and sample from the distribution
N(\bar{w},\textup{Id})
, where
\bar{w}
is the projection.
∎

Lemma 6.13
.

The score function defined in
Section

6.1.2
is quasi-convex.

Proof.

Most of the constraints are similar to the program for empirical mean estimation, except for the resilience constraints on
y
.
However, by the property of pseudo-distribution, it is easy to see that these convex constraints admit quasi-convexity.
∎

Now we conclude the proof of the
Theorem

6.5
with utility analysis.

Proof of
Theorem

6.5
.

Apply the robustness-to-privacy reduction to the score
\mathcal{S}
on the domain
\mdmathbb{B}_{2}^{d}(\,2R+n\tau+\alpha\,)
.
Let
V_{\eta}
denote the volume of
\{\widetilde{w}:\ \mathcal{S}(\widetilde{w})\leqslant\eta n\}
inside this domain.
By
Section

6.1.2
:

•

Local regime

\eta\leqslant\eta^{\prime}\leqslant\eta_{\star}
: Low-score sets are sandwiched between
\ell_{2}
-balls around
w_{\mathrm{out}}
with radii
\alpha(\eta)
and
\alpha(\eta^{\prime})
, hence

\log\!\frac{V_{\eta^{\prime}}}{V_{\eta}}\ \lesssim\ d\cdot\log\!\frac{\alpha(\eta^{\prime})}{\alpha(\eta)}.

•

Global regime

\eta_{\star}\leqslant\eta^{\prime}\leqslant 1
: The score-
\leqslant\eta_{\star}n
set lies in an
O(\alpha(\eta_{\star}))
-ball while the ambient radius is
O(R+n\tau)
, so

\log\!\frac{V_{\eta^{\prime}}}{V_{\eta}}\ \lesssim\ d\cdot\log\!\frac{R+n\tau}{\alpha(\eta_{\star})}.

Finally it is easy to verify that the score function is quasi-convex and can be efficiently evaluated. The reduction in
Theorem

4.19
then yields a sufficient condition

n\ \gtrsim\ \max_{\eta\leqslant\eta^{\prime}\leqslant\eta_{\star}}\frac{d\log(\alpha(\eta^{\prime})/\alpha(\eta))+\log(1/\beta_{\eta})}{\varepsilon\eta^{\prime}}\;+\;\max_{\eta_{\star}\leqslant\eta^{\prime}\leqslant 1}\frac{d\log\!\big((R+n\tau)/\alpha(\eta_{\star})\big)+\log(1/\beta_{\eta})}{\varepsilon}.

Optimizing over
\eta
using
\alpha(\eta)^{2}\asymp\eta\cdot\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}
in the small-
\eta
regime yields the two “local” terms

\tfrac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{\alpha^{4/3}\varepsilon^{2/3}}
and
\tfrac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{\alpha\varepsilon}
.
For the global term, take
R=\Theta(\sigma\sqrt{d})
so that the contribution is
\tfrac{d\log(\sigma\sqrt{d})}{\varepsilon}
, up to log factors.
∎

Remark 6.14

(On
\sigma
and transferring from
w_{\mathrm{out}}
)
.

The
\tfrac{d\log(\sigma\sqrt{d})}{\varepsilon}
term is the regression analogue of the global-volume term in mean estimation and arises purely from the ratio between the ambient ball and the localized score set.
In the regime
\sigma=\Omega(n^{-1/2})
,
R=\Theta(\sigma\sqrt{d})
matches the typical scale of
\|w_{\mathrm{out}}\|_{2}
.
Standard concentration shows
w_{\mathrm{out}}=(\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}+n)^{-1}Xy
is within
\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{(d+\log(1/\beta))/n})
of the true Bayesian posterior mean, which is absorbed into
\alpha
for the stated range.

6.2
Computational rate for private Bayesian linear regression with weak prior

Our main guarantee in the weak prior regime is as follows:

Theorem 6.15
.

Let
\Sigma=\sigma^{2}\textup{Id}_{d}
for known parameter
\sigma=\omega(1/\sqrt{n})
. Let
\beta\in[0,1]
,
\varepsilon\leqslant O(1)
. There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given
\eta
-corrupted samples
(X,y)
generated as in
Section

6
, outputs an
\varepsilon
-DP estimator
\hat{w}
such that with probability at least
1-\beta
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\hat{w}-\E[{w^{*}}\mid{X^{\circ}},{y^{\circ}}]}}\right\rVert^{2}\leqslant\alpha^{2}\,,

where
({X^{\circ}},{y^{\circ}})
are the uncorrupted samples, under the conditions that
\alpha\geqslant\eta\log(1/\eta)
and

n\geqslant\tilde{\Omega}\,\Bigl(\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{\alpha^{4/3}\varepsilon^{2/3}}+\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{\alpha\varepsilon}+\frac{d\log(\sigma\sqrt{d})}{\varepsilon}+\frac{d\eta^{2}}{\alpha^{4}}\Bigr)\,.

The argument is more involved than in the critical prior setting, and we defer some of the details to
Appendix

C
.

In the regime of
\sigma=\omega(1/\sqrt{n})
, the prior is sufficiently weak that the Bayes-optimal error is asymptotically achieved by the OLS estimator.
We design a
multi-stage
algorithm.
In the first stage, we obtain a rough estimator with error rate
O(1)
.
In the second and the third stage, we refine the estimator to the target level of accuracy.

6.2.1
Overview of multi-stage algorithm

Here we overview our approach in the weak prior regime. For starters, without any
a priori
upper bound on the variance of the true regressor
w
, the strategy in the critical prior regime cannot be directly applied as we have no control over the magnitudes of the labels
y
. The first step is thus to “re-center” the labels by learning a
coarse estimate
of
w
(
Section

6.2.2
). This can be done again with the short-flat decomposition constraint, but this time imposed not upon the label vector
\hat{y}
, but on the
residual vector

\hat{y}-\hat{X}^{\intercal}\hat{w}
. An argument similar to the above can be used to show that this will yield an estimate
w_{\rm init}
of the true
w
which is accurate to within
O(1)
error in
L_{2}
.

After the coarse estimation stage, intuitively we can now “subtract out” our coarse estimate
w_{\rm init}
from
w
to reduce the problem to something resembling the proportional regime. This runs into several obstacles.

First, we do not want to directly subtract out
\tilde{X}w_{\rm init}
from
\tilde{y}
because of the corruptions in
\tilde{X}
, so instead we indirectly fold our coarse estimate into a new sum-of-squares program and search for short-flat decompositions of both
\hat{y}-\hat{X}^{\intercal}\hat{w}
, where
\hat{w}
is the SoS variable for the regressor, as well as
X^{\intercal}(\hat{w}-w_{\rm init})
.

Second, it turns out that even this is only enough to refine the
O(1)
error achieved by
w_{\rm init}
to error
\tilde{O}(\sqrt{d/n})
in
L_{2}
for estimating the posterior mean.
This is because the spectral norm of
\frac{1}{n}XX^{\intercal}-\textup{Id}
is only bounded by
O(\sqrt{d/n})
with high probability.
Therefore, naively one can only show that
\frac{1}{n}Xy
is within
L_{2}
distance
\sqrt{d/n}
to the least square estimator
(XX^{\intercal})^{-1}Xy
.
Consequently, given the vector
w_{1}
achieving this latter error bound, the above program which looks for short-flat decompositions of both labels and residuals needs to be re-run, with
w_{\rm init}
replaced by
w_{1}
.
Particularly, because of the refined error bound from
w_{1}
, one can now show that the vectors
v_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(n+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}})^{-1}X(y-X^{\intercal}w_{1})
and
v_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(XX^{\intercal}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}})^{-1}X(y-X^{\intercal}w_{1})
are close in the sense that with high probability

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert v_{1}-v_{2}}}\right\rVert\\
\leqslant O\Bigl(\sqrt{\eta\log 1/\eta}+\sqrt{d/n}\Bigr)\cdot\Bigl(\sqrt{d/n}\Bigr)\,.

This will enable us to prove the desired error bound of
\tilde{O}(\eta+\sqrt{\eta\sqrt{d/n}})
. We defer the details of these latter two SoS programs to
Section

6.2.3
.

Finally, we note that because we are working in a Bayesian setting, the three SoS programs that are used in sequence to obtain the final posterior mean estimate are necessarily run on the
same dataset
. This might appear to be problematic as the latter two SoS programs are adaptively constructed: some of the constraints therein depend on estimates
w_{\rm init},w_{1}
computed in preceding SoS programs, and this data reuse could potentially break feasibility. As we will see (
Section

6.2.3
), this turns out not to be an issue, essentially because we can use a bound on the “sparse singular values” of the design matrix
X
to argue that regardless of
w_{\rm init},w_{1}
, as long as they are
O(1)
-close to
w
, the vectors
X^{\intercal}(w-w_{\rm init})
and
X^{\intercal}(w-w_{1})
admit a short-flat decomposition.

6.2.2
Stage 1: Rough estimation

In the first stage, we will use a sum-of-squares program that bears resemblance to the one used in the critical prior regime, with the following key differences.

First, the corruption constraints
\mathcal{A}_{\rm corr}
are the same as before (
Eq.

23
). The resilience constraints are similar, except instead of insisting on a short-flat decomposition for the labels
y
, we insist on a short-flat decomposition for the residual
y-X^{\intercal}w
:

\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{res}}^{\prime}(X,y,w)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\frac{1}{n}XX^{\intercal}-\textup{Id}}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant\chi\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}},\\
&y-X^{\intercal}w=z_{1}+z_{2},\lVert z_{1}+z_{2}\rVert^{2}\leqslant 2n\\
&\|z_{1}\|^{2}\leqslant\eta n\log(1/\eta)+\log(1/\beta),\\
&\|z_{2}\|_{\infty}^{2}\leqslant\chi\log(1/\eta)+\log(1/\beta)/\eta n\end{aligned}}}\right\}\,.

(26)

Formally, we have the following guarantee showing that the true parameter
{w^{*}}
can be recovered to within
constant error
:

Lemma 6.16

(Rough estimation under weak prior)
.

Let
\Sigma=\sigma^{2}\textup{Id}_{d}
for known parameter
\sigma=\omega(1/\sqrt{n})
. There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given
\eta
-corrupted samples
(X,y)
generated as in
Section

6
, outputs
\hat{w}
such that with probability at least
1-\beta
, we have
\lVert\hat{w}-{w^{*}}\rVert_{2}\leqslant O(1)
provided that
n\geqslant\Omega((d+\log(1/\beta))/\eta)
.

The algorithm will be to solve the degree-
O(1)
SoS relaxation of the program
\mathcal{A}_{\rm corr}\cup\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{res}}^{\prime}
and naively round the resulting pseudo-distribution (see
Algorithm

6
). We defer the details of the SoS identifiability proof to
Section

C.3
.

6.2.3
Stage 2: Refinement

In this next stage, we will run two SoS programs in sequence. The first will take as a parameter, call it
w_{\text{init}}
, the estimate from the previous stage. This will result in a new estimate
\hat{w}_{1}
which achieves error
O(\sqrt{\eta\log 1/\eta}+\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}})
in estimating the true parameter
{w^{*}}
. We will pass
\hat{w}_{1}
into a final SoS program which will then refine it to produce an estimate
\hat{w}_{2}
with the desired level of error for approximating the posterior mean.

As in Stage 1, we use the same corruption constraints
\mathcal{A}_{\rm corr}
from
Eq.

23
. For the resilience constraints, for the first program, in addition to insisting on a short-flat decomposition for the residual
y-X^{\intercal}w
, we also insist on one for the difference between the labels
X^{\intercal}w_{\text{init}}
predicted by the initialization
w_{\text{init}}
versus under the labels
X^{\intercal}w
predicted by the SoS variable
w
:

\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{res}}^{\prime\prime}(X,y;w_{\text{init}})=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\frac{1}{n}XX^{\intercal}-\textup{Id}}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant\chi\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}},\\
&y=X^{\intercal}w+z,z=z_{1}+z_{2},\lVert z_{1}+z_{2}\rVert^{2}\leqslant 2n\\
&\|z_{1}\|^{2}\leqslant\eta n\log(1/\eta)+\log(1/\beta),\\
&\|z_{2}\|_{\infty}^{2}\leqslant\chi\log(1/\eta)+\log(1/\beta)/\eta n\\
&X^{\intercal}(w-w_{\text{init}})=b_{1}+b_{2},\\
&\|b_{1}\|^{2}\leqslant\eta n\log(1/\eta),\|b_{2}\|_{\infty}^{2}\leqslant\chi\log(1/\eta)\end{aligned}}}\right\}.

(27)

Similarly, for the second program, we replace
w_{\text{init}}
with
\hat{w}_{1}
to get
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{res}}^{\prime\prime}(X,y;\hat{w}_{1})
.

For the first program, we add a constraint for the definition of the least square estimator:

\mathcal{A}_{\text{LS}}(X,y,w,w_{\text{LS}})=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{w_{\text{LS}}=\frac{1}{n}Xy,\lVert w-w_{\text{LS}}\rVert^{2}\leqslant\frac{\chi(d+\log(1/\beta))}{n}}}\right\}\,,

(28)

and for the second program, we will use the constraint for the definition of the (simplified) posterior mean from
Eq.

25
.

Formally, we have the following guarantee showing that the posterior mean can be estimated to within the desired level of error:

Lemma 6.17
.

Let
\Sigma=\sigma^{2}\textup{Id}_{d}
for known parameter
\sigma=\omega(1/\sqrt{n})
. There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given
\eta
-corrupted samples
(X,y)
generated as in
Section

6
, and given rough estimate
w_{\text{init}}
satisfying
\lVert w_{\text{init}}-{w^{*}}\rVert\leqslant O(1)
, outputs
\hat{w}
such that with probability at least
1-\beta
, we have

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\hat{w}-\E[{w^{*}}\mid{X^{\circ}},{y^{\circ}}]}}\right\rVert^{2}\leqslant O\Bigl(\eta\log(1/\eta)\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}+\eta^{2}\log^{2}(1/\eta)\Bigr)

provided
n\geqslant(d+\log(1/\beta))/\eta
.

The full algorithm combining Stages 1 and 2 is given in
Algorithm

3
below:

Algorithm 3

(Robust algorithm for approximating posterior mean estimator)
.

Input:

\eta
corrupted matrix
X_{\mathrm{input}}\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{n\times m},y_{\mathrm{input}}\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{n}

Output:
a polynomial time estimator
\hat{w}\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{d}

1.

Run
Algorithm

6
, for obtaining estimator
w_{\text{init}}
.

2.

Solve the degree-
O(1)
SoS relaxation of the constraints
\mathcal{A}(X,y,\xi,w;X_{\mathrm{input}},y_{\mathrm{input}},w_{\text{init}})=\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{corr}}(X,y,\xi;X_{\mathrm{input}},y_{\mathrm{input}})\cup\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{res}}^{\prime\prime}(X,y,w;w_{\text{init}})\cup\mathcal{A}_{\text{LS}}(X,y,w_{\text{LS}})
, where
t=O(1)
,
obtaining a pseudo-expectation operator
\tilde{\E}
.

3.

Round the pseudo-distribution for
\hat{w}_{1}=\tilde{\E}[w]
.

4.

Solve the degree-
O(1)
SoS relaxation of the constraints
\mathcal{A}(X,y,\xi,w;X_{\mathrm{input}},y_{\mathrm{input}},\hat{w}_{1})=\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{corr}}(X,y,\xi;X_{\mathrm{input}},y_{\mathrm{input}})\cup\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{res}}^{\prime\prime}(X,y,w;\hat{w}_{1})
, where
t=O(1)
,
obtaining a pseudo-expectation operator
\tilde{\E}
.

5.

Round the pseudo-distribution for
\hat{w}_{2}=\tilde{\E}[(n+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}})^{-1}X(y-X\hat{w}_{1})]
and return
(n+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}})^{-1}\hat{w}_{1}+\hat{w}_{2}
.

First, we verify feasibility of the relevant programs.

Lemma 6.18
.

The program constraints in Steps
2
and
4
of
Algorithm

3
are satisfied by the ground truth with probability at least
1-3\beta
.

Proof.

The corruption constraints
\mathcal{A}_{\rm corr}
are satisfied by definition.
For the resilience constraints, by
Theorem

C.1
, with probability at least
1-\beta
, the bound on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\frac{1}{n}{X^{\circ}}{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}-\textup{Id}}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}
holds.
On the other hand, the vector
{y^{\circ}}-({X^{\circ}})^{\intercal}w
is i.i.d Gaussian with variance bounded by
1
.
We can let
z_{1}
represent the
\eta n
entries in
y
with largest absolute values.
By
Theorem

C.3
, we have

\lVert z_{2}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\leqslant\chi\log(1/\eta)+\frac{\chi\log(1/\beta)}{\eta n}\,.

By
Theorem

C.2
