, we have

\lVert z_{1}\rVert^{2}\leqslant\chi\eta n\log(1/\eta)+\chi\log(1/\beta)\,.

Fix
u\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=w^{*}-w_{\rm init}
and let
a\coloneqq{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}({w^{*}}-w_{\text{init}})\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{n}
.
Let
S\subseteq[n]
be the indices of the
k=\eta n
largest values of
|a_{i}|
(breaking ties arbitrarily),
and define

b_{1}\coloneqq a\odot\mathbf{1}_{S},\qquad b_{2}\coloneqq a\odot\mathbf{1}_{S^{c}}.

Then
a=b_{1}+b_{2}
by construction.

By
Section

C.1
, there exists a universal
constant
C>0
such that, with probability at least
1-\beta
over
X
,
we have
\|X\|_{\mathrm{sp}}(\eta n)\leqslant L
where
L=C(\sqrt{d}\;+\;\sqrt{\eta n\log\frac{e}{\eta}}\;+\;\sqrt{\log\frac{1}{\beta}})
.
Under the condition that
n\geqslant\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{\eta}
, we have
L\lesssim\sqrt{\eta n\log(1/\eta)}
.

For the bound of
b_{1}
, note

\|b_{1}\|_{2}=\|a_{S}\|_{2}=\|X_{S}^{\intercal}u\|_{2}\leqslant\|X_{S}\|_{\mathsf{op}}\,\|u\|_{2}\leqslant\|X\|_{\mathrm{sp}}(k)\,\|u\|_{2}\leqslant L\|u\|_{2},

which implies
\|b_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant L^{2}\|u\|_{2}^{2}
.

For the bound of
b_{2}
, let
|a|_{(1)}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant|a|_{(n)}
be the order statistics of

\{|a_{i}|\}_{i=1}^{n}
. By definition of
S
, for every
i\notin S
we have
|b_{2}(i)|=|a_{i}|\leqslant|a|_{(k+1)}\leqslant|a|_{(k)}
.
Moreover,

|a|_{(k)}^{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{k}\sum_{j=1}^{k}|a|_{(j)}^{2}=\frac{1}{k}\|a_{S}\|_{2}^{2}=\frac{1}{\eta n}\|b_{1}\|_{2}^{2}.

Therefore,

\|b_{2}\|_{\infty}^{2}=\max_{i\notin S}|a_{i}|^{2}\leqslant|a|_{(k)}^{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{k}\|b_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant\frac{L^{2}}{\eta n}\|u\|_{2}^{2},

where we used the bound of
b_{1}
in the last step.
Therefore, the program constraints are satisfied.
∎

Next we show that
\hat{w}_{1}
produced by first program in Stage 2 achieves error
O(\sqrt{\eta\log 1/\eta}+\sqrt{\frac{d+\log 1/\beta}{n}})
:

Lemma 6.19
.

Consider the program in Step
3
from
Algorithm

3
. There is a degree-
O(1)
sum-of-squares proof that

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(X^{(1)},y^{(1)},\xi^{(1)},w^{(1)};X_{\mathrm{input}},y_{\mathrm{input}})\,,\ \mathcal{A}(X^{(2)},y^{(2)},\xi^{(2)},w^{(2)};X_{\mathrm{input}},y_{\mathrm{input}})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.00003pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.00003pt\raisebox{-1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.00003pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\hskip-8.00003pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.00003pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}$}}}\lVert w^{(1)}-w^{(2)}\rVert^{2}\leqslant O\Bigl(\eta\log(1/\eta)+\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}\Bigr)\,.

By standard rounding, this implies that

\lVert\hat{w}_{1}-w^{*}\rVert_{2}\leqslant O\Bigl(\sqrt{\eta\log 1/\eta}+\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}\Bigr)\,.

(29)

Proof.

For
j\in\{1,2\}
, define
y^{(j)}_{1}=y^{(j)}-(X^{(j)})^{\intercal}w_{\text{init}}
and
w^{(j)}_{1}=w^{(j)}-w_{\text{init}}
.
Then we have
\lVert w^{(j)}_{1}\rVert^{2}\leqslant O(1)
.
We aim to give sum-of-squares proof that

\lVert w_{\text{LS}}^{(1)}-w_{\text{LS}}^{(2)}\rVert^{2}\leqslant O\Bigl(\eta\log(1/\eta)+\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}\Bigr)\,.

Let
v=1-(1-\xi^{(1)})\odot(1-\xi^{(2)})
, noting that

\displaystyle X^{(1)}(1-v)

\displaystyle=X^{(2)}(1-v)

\displaystyle(1-v)\odot y_{1}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=(1-v)\odot y_{1}^{(2)}\,.

We perform the split

X^{(1)}y_{1}^{(1)}-X^{(2)}y_{1}^{(2)}=X^{(1)}(y_{1}^{(1)}\odot v)-X^{(2)}(y_{1}^{(2)}\odot v)

and bound these two terms separately, using the spectral norm bound on
X
.
In particular, we have

\lVert X^{(1)}(y_{1}^{(1)}\odot v)\rVert^{2}=\lVert X^{(1)}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\cdot\lVert y_{1}^{(1)}\odot v\rVert^{2}\leqslant\chi n\cdot\lVert y_{1}^{(1)}\odot v\rVert^{2}\,.

By the short-flat decomposition for
y^{(1)}
,

\lVert y_{1}^{(1)}\odot v\rVert^{2}\leqslant\chi^{2}\eta n\log(1/\eta)+\log(1/\beta)\,.

As a result, we have

\lVert X^{(j)}(y_{1}^{(j)}\odot v)\rVert^{2}\leqslant O(\eta n^{2}\log(1/\eta)+\log(1/\beta)n)

for
j=1,2
, and thus
\lVert w_{\text{LS}}^{(1)}-w_{\text{LS}}^{(2)}\rVert^{2}\leqslant\eta\log(1/\eta)+\log(1/\beta)/{n}
.
On the other hand, we have the program constraints that
\lVert w^{(j)}-w^{(j)}_{\text{LS}}\rVert^{2}\leqslant O(\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n})
, from which the bound on
\lVert w^{(1)}-w^{(2)}\rVert
follows by triangle inequality.
∎

Finally, we show that the last program in
Algorithm

3
results in a sufficiently accurate approximation to the posterior mean.

Proof of
Section

6.2.3
.

Let
y^{\prime}=y-X^{\intercal}\hat{w}_{1}
, and
w^{\prime}=w-\hat{w}_{1}
.
Let
{y^{\circ}}^{\prime}={y^{\circ}}-{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}\hat{w}_{1}
and
{w^{*}}^{\prime}={w^{*}}-\hat{w}_{1}
.
By the guarantee on
\hat{w}_{1}
from
Section

6.2.3
, we have
\lVert w^{\prime}\rVert^{2}\leqslant O\Bigl(\eta\log(1/\eta)+\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}\Bigr)
.

Now we give sum-of-squares proof that for any unit vector
u

\langle Xy^{\prime}-{X^{\circ}}{y^{\circ}}^{\prime},u\rangle^{2}\leqslant O\Bigl(\eta^{2}\log(1/\eta)+\eta\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}\Bigr)\,.

Let
r
be the indicator for the set of corrupted nodes. Let
v=1-(1-\xi)\odot(1-r)
, noting that

\displaystyle X((1-v)\odot y)

\displaystyle={X^{\circ}}((1-v)\odot{y^{\circ}}^{\prime})

\displaystyle(1-v)\odot y

\displaystyle=(1-v)\odot{y^{\circ}}^{\prime}\,.

As before, we perform the split

Xy^{\prime}-{X^{\circ}}{y^{\circ}}^{\prime}=X(y^{\prime}\odot v)-{X^{\circ}}({y^{\circ}}^{\prime}\odot v)

and bound these two terms separately, using the spectral norm bound on
X
. In particular, for any unit vector
u
,

\langle X(y^{\prime}\odot v),u\rangle^{2}\leqslant\lVert Xu\odot v\rVert^{2}\lVert y^{\prime}\odot v\rVert^{2}\,.

Now similar to our proofs for empirical mean estimation, we have a sum-of-squares proof that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\lVert Xu\odot v\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant O\Bigl(\eta\log(1/\eta)+\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\lVert y^{\prime}\odot v\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant(\eta n\log(1/\eta)+\log(1/\beta))\,.

Combining the above, we have a sum-of-squares proof that

\frac{1}{n}\langle X(y^{\prime}\odot v),u\rangle^{2}\leqslant O\Bigl(\eta\log(1/\eta)+\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}\Bigr)\cdot(\eta n\log(1/\eta)+\log(1/\beta))\,,

(30)

as well as the same bound for
\frac{1}{n}\langle{X^{\circ}}({y^{\circ}}^{\prime}\odot v),u\rangle^{2}
.
Therefore, by
Section

6.2.3
, for any pseudo-distribution
\tilde{\E}
satisfying the constraints and for any unit vector
u
, we have that
\tilde{\E}\Bigl[\frac{1}{n}\langle Xy^{\prime}-{X^{\circ}}{y^{\circ}}^{\prime},u\rangle^{2}\Bigr]
is bounded by the right-hand side of
Eq.

30
.
Therefore by rounding the pseudo-distribution, we conclude that

\Bigl\|\frac{1}{n}\Bigl(\tilde{\E}[Xy^{\prime}]-{X^{\circ}}{y^{\circ}}^{\prime}\Bigr)\Bigr\|^{2}\leqslant O\Bigl(\eta\log(1/\eta)+\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}\Bigr)\cdot(\eta\log(1/\eta)+\log(1/\beta)/n)\,.

(31)

Now we show that
(n+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}})^{-1}{X^{\circ}}{y^{\circ}}^{\prime}+\Bigl(1+\frac{1}{n\sigma^{2}}\Bigr)^{-1}\hat{w}_{1}
is close to the true posterior mean
({X^{\circ}}{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\textup{Id})^{-1}{X^{\circ}}{y^{\circ}}
.
By definition, we have
{y^{\circ}}^{\prime}={y^{\circ}}-{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}\hat{w}_{1}
.
Now we have

\displaystyle(n+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}})^{-1}{X^{\circ}}{y^{\circ}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\Bigl({X^{\circ}}{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\textup{Id}\Bigr)^{-1}{X^{\circ}}({y^{\circ}}-{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}\hat{w}_{1})+\Bigl((n+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}})^{-1}-\Bigl({X^{\circ}}{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\textup{Id}\Bigr)^{-1}\Bigr){X^{\circ}}({y^{\circ}}-{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}\hat{w}_{1})

\displaystyle=\Bigl({X^{\circ}}{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\textup{Id}\Bigr)^{-1}{X^{\circ}}{y^{\circ}}-\hat{w}_{1}+\Bigl({X^{\circ}}{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\textup{Id}\Bigr)^{-1}\frac{\hat{w}_{1}}{\sigma^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\Bigl((n+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}})^{-1}-\Bigl({X^{\circ}}{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\textup{Id}\Bigr)^{-1}\Bigr){X^{\circ}}({y^{\circ}}-{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}\hat{w}_{1})\,.

First,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\Bigl({X^{\circ}}{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\textup{Id}\Bigr)^{-1}\frac{\hat{w}_{1}}{\sigma^{2}}-\frac{\hat{w}_{1}}{n\sigma^{2}+1}}}\right\rVert\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\Bigl({X^{\circ}}{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\textup{Id}\Bigr)^{-1}-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n\sigma^{2}+1}}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\frac{\lVert\hat{w}_{1}\rVert}{\sigma^{2}}

Moreover, we have

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\Bigl({X^{\circ}}{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\textup{Id}\Bigr)^{-1}-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n\sigma^{2}+1}\textup{Id}}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant\frac{1}{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\frac{1}{n}{X^{\circ}}{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}-\textup{Id}}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant\frac{1}{n}\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}\,.

Combining, we obtain

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\Bigl({X^{\circ}}{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\textup{Id}\Bigr)^{-1}\frac{\hat{w}_{1}}{\sigma^{2}}-\frac{\hat{w}_{1}}{n\sigma^{2}+1}}}\right\rVert\leqslant\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}\cdot\frac{\lVert\hat{w}_{1}\rVert}{n\sigma^{2}}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}\cdot\frac{1}{n\sigma}\lesssim\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}\,.

Finally we want to bound the norm of
\Bigl((n+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}})^{-1}-\Bigl({X^{\circ}}{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\textup{Id}\Bigr)^{-1}\Bigr){X^{\circ}}({y^{\circ}}-{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}\hat{w}_{1})
.
In particular, with probability at least
1-\beta
, for some universal constant
C
, we have

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert(n+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}})^{-1}\textup{Id}-\Bigl({X^{\circ}}{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\textup{Id}\Bigr)^{-1}}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant\frac{1}{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\frac{1}{n}{X^{\circ}}{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}-\textup{Id}}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant\frac{1}{n}\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}\,.

On the other hand, we have

\lVert\frac{1}{n}{X^{\circ}}({y^{\circ}}-{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}\hat{w}_{1})\rVert=\lVert\frac{1}{n}{X^{\circ}}{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}({w^{*}}-\hat{w}_{1})\rVert+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\frac{1}{n}{X^{\circ}}z_{0}}}\right\rVert\leqslant O\Bigl(\sqrt{\eta\log 1/\eta}+\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}\Bigr)\,.

Therefore, we have

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\Bigl((n+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}})^{-1}\textup{Id}-\Bigl({X^{\circ}}{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\textup{Id}\Bigr)^{-1}\Bigr){X^{\circ}}({y^{\circ}}-{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}\hat{w}_{1})}}\right\rVert\\
\leqslant O\Bigl(\sqrt{\eta\log 1/\eta}+\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}\Bigr)\cdot\Bigl(\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}\Bigr)\,.

Putting everything together, we get

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert(n+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}})^{-1}{X^{\circ}}{y^{\circ}}^{\prime}+\Bigl(1+\frac{1}{n\sigma^{2}}\Bigr)^{-1}\hat{w}_{1}-({X^{\circ}}{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\textup{Id})^{-1}{X^{\circ}}{y^{\circ}}}}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\Bigl((n+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}})^{-1}\textup{Id}-\Bigl({X^{\circ}}{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\textup{Id}\Bigr)^{-1}\Bigr){X^{\circ}}({y^{\circ}}-{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}\hat{w}_{1})}}\right\rVert+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\Bigl({X^{\circ}}{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\textup{Id}\Bigr)^{-1}\frac{\hat{w}_{1}}{\sigma^{2}}-\frac{\hat{w}_{1}}{n\sigma^{2}+1}}}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leqslant O\Bigl(\sqrt{\eta\log 1/\eta}+\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}\Bigr)\cdot\Bigl(\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}\Bigr)\,.

Combining with
Eq.

31
, we conclude the proof.
∎

6.2.4
Putting everything together

To obtain a private algorithm, it remains to plug the above robustness guarantees into the robustness-to-privacy reduction. We implement this in
Section

C.4
to prove Theorem
6.15
. Finally, by combining this with the guarantee in the critical prior case, we immediately deduce
Theorem

6.3
.

Proof of
Theorem

6.3
.

When
\sigma=o(1/\sqrt{n})
, the claimed error rate is achieved by sampling from the prior distribution.
When
\sigma=\Omega(1/\sqrt{n})
, we can combine
Theorem

6.5
,
Theorem

6.15
and
Eq.

14
.
In particular, by
Theorem

6.5
,
Theorem

6.15
, when
n\geqslant d\log(\sigma\sqrt{d})/\varepsilon
with probability
1-\beta
, we can achieve error rate
\alpha=O(\eta^{\prime}+\sqrt{\eta^{\prime}\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}})
where
\eta^{\prime}=\eta+\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n\varepsilon}
.
∎

6.3
Hardness of robust Bayesian regression

We now give a computational lower bound for robust posterior mean estimation in the regime

\eta\log(1/\eta)\lesssim\alpha\leqslant\eta d^{1/4-o(1)}.

As in the rest of this subsection, it is convenient to work first with the simplified posterior statistic

w_{\rm simp}(X,y)\coloneqq\Bigl(n+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\Bigr)^{-1}Xy.

In the critical-prior regime
\sigma^{2}=\Theta(1/n)
,
Section

6.1.1
implies that

w_{\rm simp}(X^{\circ},y^{\circ})
differs from the exact posterior mean by

O((d+\log(1/\beta))/n)
.

Definition 6.20

(Distinguishing mixtures of linear regressions)
.

Fix
\eta\in(0,1/2)
.
Let
K
be some sufficiently large constant.
Fix a signal parameter
\delta>0
, and define

s\coloneqq\sqrt{1+K^{2}\alpha^{2}},\qquad a\coloneqq-\frac{K\alpha}{\eta s^{2}}.

We consider the following one-sample hypothesis testing problem on

(x_{i},y_{i})\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mdmathbb{R}}
.

•

Null distribution
Q
:

x_{i}=g_{i},y_{i}=sz_{i},

where
g_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\textup{Id}_{d})
,
z_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, and
g_{i},z_{i}
are independent.

•

Planted
P
:

first draw

u\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\mdmathbb S^{d-1}),B\sim\mathrm{Ber}(q).

Conditional on
(u,B)
, draw

g_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\textup{Id}_{d}),z_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1),

independently, and set

y_{i}\coloneqq K\alpha\langle g_{i},u\rangle+z_{i}.

If
B=0
, let

x_{i}\coloneqq g_{i}.

If
B=1
, let

x_{i}\coloneqq g_{i}+ay_{i}u\,.

We record some basic properties of the distinguishing problem:

Lemma 6.21
.

Assume

\alpha^{2}\lesssim\eta.

Then the following hold.

1.

Under
P
, conditional on
u
, the observed sample is a
q
-corruption of a clean
Gaussian-design regression sample with regressor
w^{\star}=K\alpha u
.

2.

Under both
Q
and
P
, the marginal law of
y_{i}
is exactly
\mathcal{N}(0,s^{2})
.

3.

Under
Q
,

\E_{Q}[x_{i}y_{i}]=0,

while under
P
, conditional on
u
,

\E_{P}[x_{i}y_{i}\mid u]=0.

4.

The null law
Q
is a
\eta
-corruption of a clean Gaussian-design regression sample with
regressor
w^{\star}=0
.

We show that a robust estimator for the exact posterior mean can be used to solve the distinguishing problem.

Lemma 6.22

(Reduction from distinguishing to robust posterior mean estimation)
.

Assume
\sigma^{2}=\Theta(1/n)
, and define

w_{\rm simp}(X,y)\coloneqq\Bigl(n+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\Bigr)^{-1}Xy.

Consider the testing problem in
Section

6.3
.
Assume that

n\geqslant C_{1}\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{\alpha}\;+\;C_{2}\frac{\eta(d+\log(1/\beta))}{\alpha^{2}}\;+\;C_{3}\frac{\log(1/\beta)}{\eta}

(32)

for sufficiently large universal constants
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}
.

Suppose there exists a polynomial-time estimator
\mathcal{A}
with the following guarantee:
for every
\eta
-corruption
(X,y)
of a clean sample
(X^{\circ},y^{\circ})
from

Section

6
with
\|{w^{*}}\|_{2}\leqslant\alpha
, with
probability at least
9/10
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\mathcal{A}(X,y)-\E[{w^{*}}\mid X^{\circ},y^{\circ}]}}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\frac{\alpha}{20}.

Then there is a polynomial-time test that distinguishes
Q
from
P
in

Section

6.3
with probability at least

1-O(\beta)-\exp(-\Omega(\eta n))
.

In particular, we give the following test:
we compute

w_{\rm obs}\coloneqq w_{\rm simp}(X,y),\widehat{w}\coloneqq\mathcal{A}(X,y)
,
and output
planted
if

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\widehat{w}-w_{\rm obs}}}\right\rVert_{2}\geqslant 2\alpha,

and
null
otherwise.

Finally, we give a lemma showing that the hypothesis testing problem is low-degree hard in the regime that we are interested in.

Lemma 6.23
.

Assume
\alpha\leqslant\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\eta d^{1/4}/D,\eta^{2}}}\right\}
and
D\leqslant d^{10^{-4}}
.
Consider the testing problem in
Section

6.3
, and let

\mathrm{Adv}_{\leqslant D}
denote the degree-
D
advantage.
Then there exists a universal constant
c>0
such that whenever

n\leqslant c\,\frac{d\eta^{2}}{D^{2}\alpha^{4}},

we have

\mathrm{Adv}_{\leqslant D}=O(1).

From these two results, we can conclude an information-computation gap for robust posterior mean estimation in regression.

Theorem 6.24

(Information-computation gap for robust posterior mean estimation in regression)
.

There exist absolute constants
c_{0},C_{0}>0
such that the following holds for all sufficiently large

d
.
Assume
\alpha\leqslant\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\eta d^{1/4}/D,\eta^{2}}}\right\}
.
Suppose

C_{0}\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{\alpha}\;+\;C_{0}\frac{\eta(d+\log(1/\beta))}{\alpha^{2}}\;+\;C_{0}\frac{\log(1/\beta)}{\eta}\;\leqslant\;n\;\leqslant\;c_{0}\frac{d\eta^{2}}{D^{2}\alpha^{4}}\,.

Assuming the low-degree conjecture in the hypothesis testing problem

Section

6.3
.

Then any polynomial-time estimator that robustly estimates the exact posterior mean to error

\alpha/20
with probability at least
9/10
on every
\eta
-corruption of clean samples from

Section

6
with
\|{w^{*}}\|_{2}\leqslant\alpha
yields a
polynomial-time distinguisher for the testing problem.

Proof.

This follows from combining
Section

6.3
and
Section

6.3
.
∎

6.3.1
Proof of
Section

6.3

Proof of
Section

6.3
.

Let
\delta=K\alpha
.
For (i), define the clean sample

x_{i}^{\circ}\coloneqq g_{i},\qquad y_{i}^{\circ}\coloneqq\delta\langle g_{i},u\rangle+z_{i}.

Then
(X^{\circ},Y^{\circ})
is exactly one clean sample from

Section

6
with regressor
w^{\star}=\delta u
.
Under
P
, if
B=0
we keep the sample unchanged, and if
B=1
we replace
x_{i}^{\circ}
by

x_{i}^{\circ}+ay_{i}^{\circ}u
while leaving
y_{i}^{\circ}
unchanged. This proves (i).

For (ii), under
Q
the claim is immediate. Under
P
, conditional on
u
, in both branches
we have

y_{i}=\delta\langle g_{i},u\rangle+z_{i}.

Since
\langle g_{i},u\rangle\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
and is independent of
z_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
,
it follows that
y_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1+\alpha^{2})=\mathcal{N}(0,s^{2})
.

For (iii), under
Q
we have independence and centering, so
\E_{Q}[x_{i}y_{i}]=0
.
Under
P
, conditional on
u
, in the clean branch
B=0
,

\E[x_{i}y_{i}\mid u,B=0]=\E\bigl[x_{i}(\delta\langle G,u\rangle+Z)\mid u\bigr]=\delta u.

In the outlier branch
B=1
,

\E[x_{i}y_{i}\mid u,B=1]=\E[(g_{i}+auy_{i})y_{i}\mid u]=\E[g_{i}y_{i}\mid u]+a\E[y_{i}^{2}\mid u]u=\delta u+as^{2}u.

Averaging over
B\sim\mathrm{Ber}(q)
,

\E_{P}[x_{i}y_{i}\mid u]=(1-q)\delta u+q(\delta+as^{2})u=\bigl(\delta+qas^{2}\bigr)u=0,

because
a=-\delta/(qs^{2})
.

For (iv), let
\varphi_{\sigma}
denote the density of
\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
, and define

r_{0}(y)\coloneqq\frac{\varphi_{s}(y)-(1-\eta)\varphi_{1}(y)}{\eta}.

Since

\frac{\varphi_{s}(y)}{\varphi_{1}(y)}=\frac{1}{s}\exp\Bigl(\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2(1+\alpha^{2})}y^{2}\Bigr),

the ratio is minimized at
y=0
, so

\inf_{y}\frac{\varphi_{s}(y)}{\varphi_{1}(y)}=\frac{1}{s}.

Also,

1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+t}}\leqslant\frac{t}{2}\qquad(t\geqslant 0),

hence

1-\frac{1}{s}\leqslant\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}\leqslant\eta.

Therefore

\varphi_{s}(y)\geqslant(1-\eta)\varphi_{1}(y)\qquad\text{for all }y,

so
r_{0}(y)\geqslant 0
. Moreover,

\int_{\mdmathbb{R}}r_{0}(y)\,dy=\frac{1-(1-\eta)}{\eta}=1,

so
r_{0}
is a valid density.

Now start from a clean
w^{\star}=0
sample

x_{i}^{\circ}=g_{i},
and
y_{i}^{\circ}=z_{i}
,
with
g_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\textup{Id}_{d})
,
z_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
independent. Keep
x_{i}^{\circ}
unchanged.
With probability
1-\eta
, keep
y_{i}^{\circ}
; with probability
\eta
, replace it by an independent draw

R\sim r_{0}
. The observed response then has density

(1-\eta)\varphi_{1}+\eta r_{0}=\varphi_{s},

so
y_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,s^{2})
, and therefore the resulting observed pair
(x_{i},y_{i})
has exactly law
Q
.
∎

6.3.2
Reducing hypothesis testing to robust posterior mean estimation.

In this section, we give the proof of
Section

6.3

Proof of
Section

6.3
.

Let

\lambda\coloneqq\Bigl(n+\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\Bigr)^{-1},\qquad\nu\coloneqq n\lambda=\frac{n\sigma^{2}}{1+n\sigma^{2}}.

Since
\sigma^{2}=\Theta(1/n)
, we have
\nu=\Theta(1)
.

We first consider the case under null distribution.
By
Section

6.3
,
Q
can be realized as a
\eta
-corruption of a clean

w^{\star}=0
sample
(X^{\circ},y^{\circ})
, where
X^{\circ}_{i}=G_{i}
and
y^{\circ}_{i}=Z_{i}
.
Let
C_{i}\sim\mathrm{Ber}(\eta)
denote the indicators of the corrupted responses.
A Chernoff bound then yields

\ProbOp\Bigl[\sum_{i=1}^{n}C_{i}\leqslant\eta n\Bigr]\geqslant 1-\exp(-\Omega(\eta n)).

On this event, the robust guarantee for
\mathcal{A}
applies. Also, by
Section

6.1.1
and the first term
in (
32
),

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\E[{w^{*}}\mid X^{\circ},y^{\circ}]-w_{\rm simp}(X^{\circ},y^{\circ})}}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\frac{\alpha}{20}

except with probability at most
\beta
. Hence, except with probability
O(\beta)
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\widehat{w}-w_{\rm simp}(X^{\circ},y^{\circ})}}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\frac{\alpha}{10}.

(33)

Now compare the observed and clean simplified statistics. Since only the responses are modified,

w_{\rm obs}-w_{\rm simp}(X^{\circ},y^{\circ})=\lambda\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{\circ}\Delta_{i},\qquad\Delta_{i}\coloneqq C_{i}(R_{i}-Z_{i}).

The replacement density
r_{0}
has variance

\mathrm{Var}(R_{i})=\frac{s^{2}-(1-\eta)}{\eta}=1+\frac{K^{2}\alpha^{2}}{\eta}\leqslant O(1),

so
\Delta_{i}
are centered and subgaussian with variance proxy
O(\eta)
. Thus

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Delta_{i}^{2}\leqslant C\eta n

with probability at least
1-\exp(-\Omega(\eta n))
. Conditional on the
\Delta_{i}
’s, the vector

\sum_{i}X_{i}^{\circ}\Delta_{i}
is Gaussian with covariance
(\sum_{i}\Delta_{i}^{2})\textup{Id}_{d}
. Therefore,
with probability at least
1-\beta-\exp(-\Omega(\eta n))
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert w_{\rm obs}-w_{\rm simp}(X^{\circ},y^{\circ})}}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant C\lambda\sqrt{\eta n(d+\log(1/\beta))}\leqslant C\sqrt{\frac{\eta(d+\log(1/\beta))}{n}}\leqslant\alpha,

where the last step uses the second term in (
32
).
Combining with (
33
),

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\widehat{w}-w_{\rm obs}}}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\frac{11}{10}\alpha<2\alpha

except with probability
O(\beta)+\exp(-\Omega(\eta n))
. Thus the test outputs
null
.

We then consider the planted distribution.
By construction, the planted law is realized by first drawing a clean sample

X_{i}^{\circ}=G_{i},\qquad y_{i}^{\circ}=\alpha\langle G_{i},u\rangle+Z_{i}

with regressor
w^{\star}=\alpha u
, then drawing i.i.d. indicators
B_{i}\sim\mathrm{Ber}(q)
, and finally
setting

(X_{i},y_{i})=\begin{cases}(X_{i}^{\circ},y_{i}^{\circ}),&B_{i}=0,\\
(X_{i}^{\circ}+ay_{i}^{\circ}u,\ y_{i}^{\circ}),&B_{i}=1.\end{cases}

Again

\ProbOp\Bigl[\sum_{i=1}^{n}B_{i}\leqslant\eta n\Bigr]\geqslant 1-\exp(-\Omega(\eta n)).

On this event the robust guarantee for
\mathcal{A}
applies, and by
Section

6.1.1
together with the first
term in (
32
),

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\widehat{w}-w_{\rm simp}(X^{\circ},y^{\circ})}}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant\frac{\alpha}{10}

(34)

except with probability
O(\beta)
.

Now compare the observed and clean simplified statistics. Since only
X
changes,

w_{\rm obs}-w_{\rm simp}(X^{\circ},y^{\circ})=\lambda\sum_{i=1}^{n}B_{i}\,a\,(y_{i}^{\circ})^{2}\,u.

Taking expectations conditional on
u
,

\E[w_{\rm obs}-w_{\rm simp}(X^{\circ},y^{\circ})\mid u]=\lambda n\eta as^{2}u=-\nu K\alpha\,u.

Also,
y_{i}^{\circ}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,s^{2})
, so Bernstein’s inequality gives

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}B_{i}\bigl((y_{i}^{\circ})^{2}-s^{2}\bigr)}}\right|\leqslant Cs^{2}\sqrt{\eta n\log(1/\beta)}

with probability at least
1-\beta-\exp(-\Omega(\eta n))
. Therefore,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\Bigl(w_{\rm obs}-w_{\rm simp}(X^{\circ},y^{\circ})\Bigr)+\nu\alpha u}}\right\rVert_{2}\leqslant C\lambda|a|s^{2}\sqrt{\eta n\log(1/\beta)}=C\nu K\alpha\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\beta)}{\eta n}}.

By the third term in (
32
), the right-hand side is at most

\nu K\alpha/4
.
Hence, except with probability
\beta+\exp(-\Omega(\eta n))
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert w_{\rm obs}-w_{\rm simp}(X^{\circ},y^{\circ})}}\right\rVert_{2}\geqslant\frac{3\nu K}{4}\alpha.

Combining with (
34
),

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\widehat{w}-w_{\rm obs}}}\right\rVert_{2}\geqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert w_{\rm obs}-w_{\rm simp}(X^{\circ},y^{\circ})}}\right\rVert_{2}-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\widehat{w}-w_{\rm simp}(X^{\circ},y^{\circ})}}\right\rVert_{2}\geqslant\frac{3\nu K}{4}\alpha-\frac{\alpha}{10}.

Since
\nu=\Theta(1)
, choosing
K
sufficiently large gives

\frac{3\nu K}{4}\alpha-\frac{\alpha}{10}\geqslant 2\alpha.

Thus the test outputs
planted
except with probability
O(\beta)+\exp(-\Omega(\eta n))
.
∎

6.3.3
Proof of
Section

6.3

It is easier to prove low-degree lower bounds when the null distribution is standard Gaussian.
We define the fixed linear map

T:{\mdmathbb{R}}^{d+1}\to{\mdmathbb{R}}^{d+1},\qquad T(x,y)\coloneqq(x,y/s).

Since
T
is an invertible linear map and preserves total polynomial degree, the degree-
D

advantage is unchanged by applying
T
samplewise. We will henceforth work with the data transformed by
T
, for which the likelihood ratio admits the following expansion.

Lemma 6.25
.

Let
\theta\coloneqq\frac{K\alpha}{s}
.
Under
T
, the null becomes standard Gaussian on
{\mdmathbb{R}}^{d+1}
:

T_{\#}Q=\mathcal{N}(0,\textup{Id}_{d+1}).

Under the planted law, conditional on
u
, if we write

t\coloneqq\langle x,u\rangle,y\coloneqq Y/s,

then the one-sample likelihood ratio with respect to the whitened null has the expansion

\ell_{u}(x,y)=1+\sum_{k\geqslant 1}h_{k}(\langle x,u\rangle)\,{}_{k}(y),

(35)

where

{}_{k}(y)=\theta^{k}\Bigl((1-q)h_{k}(y)+\eta\,h_{k}\Bigl(\Bigl(1-\frac{1}{\eta}\Bigr)y\Bigr)\Bigr).

(36)

In particular,

{}_{1}(y)\equiv 0.

Proof.

Under the null,
X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\textup{Id}_{d})
and
Y/s\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
independently, so

T_{\#}Q=\mathcal{N}(0,\textup{Id}_{d+1})
.
Let
\delta=K\alpha
.

Now condition on the planted direction
u
, and let
g\coloneqq\langle G,u\rangle
.
Then

y=\frac{Y}{s}=\frac{\delta g+Z}{s}=\theta g+\sqrt{1-\theta^{2}}\,\xi,

where
\xi\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. Hence
y\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.

In the clean branch
B=0
, we have
x=G
, so
t=g
. Therefore

t\mid y,\,B=0\sim\mathcal{N}(\theta y,\,1-\theta^{2}).

In the outlier branch
B=1
, we have

x=G+aYu=G+as\,y\,u,

so

t=g+by,\qquad b\coloneqq as=-\frac{\delta}{\eta s}=-\frac{\theta}{\eta}.

Hence

t\mid y,\,B=1\sim\mathcal{N}\Bigl(\theta\Bigl(1-\frac{1}{\eta}\Bigr)y,\,1-\theta^{2}\Bigr).

By Fact 4.7, if
T\sim\mathcal{N}(\rho x,1-\rho^{2})
, then

\E[h_{k}(T)]=\rho^{k}h_{k}(x).

Applying this with
\rho=\theta
in the two branches gives

\E[h_{k}(t)\mid y,B=0]=\theta^{k}h_{k}(y),\qquad\E[h_{k}(t)\mid y,B=1]=\theta^{k}h_{k}\Bigl(\Bigl(1-\frac{1}{\eta}\Bigr)y\Bigr).

Averaging over
B\sim\mathrm{Ber}(\eta)
yields (
36
), we immediately have

\displaystyle{}_{1}(y)

\displaystyle=\theta\Bigl((1-\eta)h_{1}(y)+\eta h_{1}\Bigl(\Bigl(1-\frac{1}{\eta}\Bigr)y\Bigr)\Bigr)=0\,.\qed

(37)

In preparation for the proof of
Section

6.3
, we prove the following two helper lemmas.

Lemma 6.26
.

Let
Y\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, let
|c|\geqslant 1
, and let
k\geqslant 0
. Then

\E[h_{k}(cY)^{2}]\leqslant 2(4c^{2})^{k}.

Proof.

By Mehler’s identity, for every
|r|<1
,

\sum_{k\geqslant 0}r^{k}h_{k}(x)^{2}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-r^{2}}}\exp\Bigl(\frac{rx^{2}}{1+r}\Bigr).

Substituting
x=cY
and taking expectation over
Y\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
gives

\sum_{k\geqslant 0}r^{k}\E[h_{k}(cY)^{2}]=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-r^{2}}}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\frac{2rc^{2}}{1+r}}}.

Set

r=\frac{1}{4c^{2}}.

Since
|c|\geqslant 1
, we have
0<r<1
, and

1+r-2rc^{2}=1+\frac{1}{4c^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\geqslant\frac{1}{2},\qquad 1-r\geqslant\frac{3}{4}.

Hence the right-hand side is at most
2
. Since all coefficients are nonnegative,

r^{k}\E[h_{k}(cY)^{2}]\leqslant 2,

and therefore

\E[h_{k}(cY)^{2}]\leqslant 2r^{-k}=2(4c^{2})^{k}.\qed

Lemma 6.27
.

Let

a_{k}\coloneqq\|{}_{k}\|_{L_{2}(\mathcal{N}(0,1))}^{2}.

Then

a_{1}=0,

and for every
k\geqslant 2
,

a_{k}\leqslant\eta^{2}\Bigl(\frac{C\theta^{2}}{\eta^{2}}\Bigr)^{k}

for a universal constant
C>0
.

Proof.

The identity
a_{1}=0
follows from
{}_{1}\equiv 0
.
For
k\geqslant 2
, by (
36
),

a_{k}\leqslant 2\theta^{2k}\Bigl((1-\eta)^{2}\|h_{k}\|_{2}^{2}+\eta^{2}\,\E\Bigl[h_{k}\Bigl(\Bigl(1-\frac{1}{\eta}\Bigr)Y\Bigr)^{2}\Bigr]\Bigr).

Since
\|h_{k}\|_{2}=1
and
|1-1/\eta|\geqslant 1
,
Section

6.3.3
gives

a_{k}\leqslant 2\theta^{2k}\Bigl(1+2\eta^{2}\Bigl(4\Bigl(1-\frac{1}{\eta}\Bigr)^{2}\Bigr)^{k}\Bigr).

Because we have
|1-1/\eta|\leqslant 1/\eta
, so

a_{k}\leqslant\eta^{2}\Bigl(\frac{C\theta^{2}}{\eta^{2}}\Bigr)^{k}

after adjusting the constant
C
.
∎

Proof of
Section

6.3
.

By
Section

6.3.3
, it suffices to analyze the problem after rescaling via
T
.
For
k\geqslant 2
, define

\phi_{u,k}(x,y)\coloneqq h_{k}(\langle x,u\rangle){}_{k}(y).

Then the one-sample likelihood ratio is

\ell_{u}(x,y)=1+\sum_{k\geqslant 2}\phi_{u,k}(x,y).

Let
u,v\in\mdmathbb S^{d-1}
, and let
\gamma\coloneqq\langle u,v\rangle
.
Under the whitened null,
x
and
y
are independent standard Gaussians, so by orthogonality,

\langle\phi_{u,k},\phi_{v,\ell}\rangle_{L_{2}(Q)}=\mathbf{1}_{\{k=\ell\}}\,{}^{k}a_{k}.

(38)

Also, for independent
u,v\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\mdmathbb S^{d-1})
,

\E[|\gamma|^{m}]\leqslant\Bigl(\frac{Cm}{d}\Bigr)^{m/2}

(39)

for all integers
m\geqslant 1
.
Let

K\coloneqq\lfloor D/2\rfloor.

Since each
\phi_{u,k}
has total degree
2k
, the
n
-sample degree-
D
projection keeps
exactly those terms for which the sum of the chosen hidden degrees is at most
K
. Expanding the

n
-sample likelihood ratio and using (
38
), we obtain

\mathrm{Adv}_{\leqslant D}^{2}\leqslant 1+\sum_{\ell\geqslant 1}\binom{n}{\ell}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},\dots,k_{\ell}\geqslant 2\\
k_{1}+\cdots+k_{\ell}\leqslant K\end{subarray}}\Bigl(\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}a_{k_{j}}\Bigr)\E\bigl[|\gamma|^{k_{1}+\cdots+k_{\ell}}\bigr].

(40)

Set

S\coloneqq n\alpha^{4}/d\eta^{2}.

Since
\alpha^{2}\leqslant\eta
, we have
s=\Theta(1)
and hence
\theta=\Theta(\alpha)
. Therefore

Section

6.3.3
implies

a_{k}\leqslant\eta^{2}\Bigl(\frac{C\delta^{2}}{\eta^{2}}\Bigr)^{k}\qquad(k\geqslant 2).

(41)

We split the sum in (
40
) into the
\ell=1
part and the
\ell\geqslant 2
part.

We first bound the contribution from terms where
\ell=1
.
For
m\geqslant 2
, the
\ell=1
term is

na_{m}\E[|\gamma|^{m}].

For
m=2
, using (
41
) and (
39
),

na_{2}\E[{}^{2}]\leqslant C\frac{n\alpha^{4}}{d\eta^{2}}=CS.

Now fix
m\geqslant 4
. Using (
41
) and (
39
),

\displaystyle na_{m}\E[|\gamma|^{m}]

\displaystyle\leqslant n\eta^{2}\Bigl(\frac{C\delta^{2}}{\eta^{2}}\Bigr)^{m}\Bigl(\frac{Cm}{d}\Bigr)^{m/2}

\displaystyle=S\cdot\Bigl(\frac{\delta}{\eta}\Bigr)^{2m-4}\cdot(Cm)^{m/2}\,d^{1-m/2}.

When
m\leqslant K\leqslant d^{10^{-4}}
, we have
(\delta/\eta)^{4}\cdot m/d\leqslant O(1)
.
Therefore

na_{m}\E[|\gamma|^{m}]\leqslant S\cdot\Bigl(\Bigl(\frac{\delta}{q}\Bigr)^{4}\cdot\frac{m}{d}\Bigr)^{m/2-1}\leqslant S\cdot d^{-0.001}\,.

for every
m\geqslant 4
. Summing over
m=4,\dots,K
, we conclude that the entire
\ell=1

contribution is
O(S)
.

For the contribution from terms where
\ell\geqslant 2
.
Fix
m\in\{4,\dots,K\}
. The number of compositions of
m
into
\ell
parts each at least
2
is

\binom{m-\ell-1}{\ell-1}\leqslant 2^{m}.

Using this, together with (
41
) and (
39
), the
contribution of all terms with total hidden degree
m
and
\ell\geqslant 2
is at most

\displaystyle\E[|\gamma|^{m}]\sum_{\ell=2}^{\lfloor m/2\rfloor}\binom{n}{\ell}\binom{m-\ell-1}{\ell-1}\eta^{2\ell}\Bigl(\frac{C\delta^{2}}{\eta^{2}}\Bigr)^{m}

\displaystyle\leqslant\Bigl(\frac{Cm}{d}\Bigr)^{m/2}\cdot 2^{m}\sum_{\ell=2}^{\lfloor m/2\rfloor}(n\eta^{2})^{\ell}\Bigl(\frac{C\delta^{2}}{\eta^{2}}\Bigr)^{m}.

Since
n\eta^{2}\geqslant 1
in the regime of interest, and
\ell\leqslant m/2
,

\sum_{\ell=2}^{\lfloor m/2\rfloor}(n\eta^{2})^{\ell}\leqslant m(n\eta^{2})^{m/2}.

Hence the total
\ell\geqslant 2
contribution is bounded by

m(Cm)^{m}\Bigl(\frac{n\delta^{4}}{d\eta^{2}}\Bigr)^{m/2}=m(Cm)^{m}S^{m/2}.

If
S\leqslant c/K^{2}
for a sufficiently small universal constant
c>0
, then

m(Cm)^{m}S^{m/2}\leqslant 2^{-m}\qquad\text{for all }m\leqslant K.

Therefore the sum over all
m\geqslant 4
and
\ell\geqslant 2
is
O(1)
.
Combining the
\ell=1
and
\ell\geqslant 2
estimates, we obtain

\mathrm{Adv}_{\leqslant D}^{2}\leqslant 1+O(S)+O(1).

If

n\leqslant c\,\frac{d\eta^{2}}{D^{2}\delta^{4}},

then
S\leqslant c/D^{2}
, and hence
\mathrm{Adv}_{\leqslant D}=O(1)
.
∎

6.4
Hardness of private Bayesian regression

Now we can apply the privacy-to-robustness reduction of
[
GH22
]
to obtain the claimed information-computation gap for private Bayesian regression,
Theorem

6.4
.

Theorem

(Restatement of
Theorem

6.4
)
.

Suppose
\varepsilon\leqslant O(1)
and
\alpha\leqslant\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\eta d^{1/4}/D,\eta^{2}}}\right\}
.
There is a distinguishing problem
\Pi
(see
Section

6.3
) which is degree-
D
hard such that if there were a polynomial-time
\varepsilon
-DP algorithm for Bayesian linear regression with standard Gaussian design and
\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}\textup{Id})
prior that estimates the posterior mean to error
\alpha
with probability
1-\beta
using

n=o\biggl(\frac{d^{1/3}\log^{2/3}(1/\beta)}{\alpha^{4/3}\varepsilon^{2/3}\cdot D^{1/3}}\biggr)

samples, then there would be an efficient distinguishing algorithm for
\Pi
.

Remark 6.28
.

Within the low-degree framework, we give a computational threshold for error rate
\alpha_{\mathrm{comp}}=\Bigl(\frac{d\log^{2}(1/\beta)}{n^{3}\varepsilon^{2}}\Bigr)^{1/4}
.
In comparison, by the black-box privacy reduction of
[
GH22
]
combined with our exponential-time robust algorithm, we know that exponential time algorithms can achieve error rate
\alpha\leqslant\tilde{O}\Bigl(\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n\varepsilon}\Bigr)
.
Therefore, the error achievable by exponential time algorithm is given by
\alpha_{\mathrm{IT}}=\tilde{O}(d/n\varepsilon)
.
As a result, even for constant failure probability
0.001
, there is a separation between
\alpha_{\mathrm{comp}}
and
\alpha_{\mathrm{IT}}
when

\varepsilon^{2}\geqslant d^{3}/n
.

Proof of
Theorem

6.4
.

We argue by contradiction and apply the privacy-to-robustness reduction from

Theorem

4.21
.

Let
\mu_{\mathrm{post}}\coloneqq\E[{w^{*}}\mid{X^{\circ}},{y^{\circ}}]
denote the posterior mean for Bayesian linear regression under the stated model.
Assume there exists a polynomial-time
\varepsilon
-DP algorithm
\mathcal{A}
which, given
n
i.i.d. samples,
outputs an estimate
\widehat{\theta}
satisfying

\ProbOp\bigl[\ \|\widehat{\theta}-\mu_{\mathrm{post}}\|\leqslant O(\alpha)\ \bigr]\;\geqslant\;1-\beta.

Applying
Theorem

4.21
to the target estimator
\mu_{\mathrm{post}}
, we obtain a
polynomial-time
\eta
-robust algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{rob}}
that achieves error
\alpha
with probability
at least
1-\beta
against an
\eta
-fraction of corruptions, where

\eta\;=\;O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\log(1/\beta)/{n\varepsilon}}}\right).

By
Section

6.3
, if there exists an efficient

\eta
-robust regression algorithm with error
\alpha
using

n\;=\;\tilde{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d\,\eta^{2}}{\alpha^{4}D}}}\right)

samples, then one can efficiently solve the distinguishing problem
\Pi

from
Section

6.3
in as many samples. But
Section

6.3
implies that no degree-
D
estimator can do this in that many samples. Hence, for any such efficient

\varepsilon
-DP algorithm
\mathcal{A}
, it must be that

n=\tilde{\Omega}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d\,\eta^{2}}{\alpha^{4}D}}}\right)
.

Substituting
\eta=O(\log(1/\beta)/(n\varepsilon))
and rearranging gives
n=\tilde{\Omega}(\frac{d}{\alpha^{4}D}\cdot\frac{\log^{2}(1/\beta)}{n^{2}\varepsilon^{2}})
, from which
the claimed lower bound follows.
∎

Appendix A
Deferred Proofs From
Section

5

A.1
From empirical mean to posterior mean

In this section we fill in the details for how to deduce
Theorem

1.1
(private posterior mean estimation) from
Theorem

5.3
(private empirical mean estimation).

Proof of the Inefficient Upper Bound in
Theorem

1.1
.

We will apply the inefficient algorithm in
Theorem

5.3
with

R=\sqrt{\Bigr(\|\Sigma\|_{\text{op}}+\frac{1}{n}\Bigl)\Bigl(d+\log\frac{1}{\beta}\Bigr)}

so that the antecedent
\|\bar{x}\|_{2}\leqslant R
holds with probability
1-O(\beta)
. Then if we require

n\geqslant\frac{d\log\frac{R}{\alpha}}{\varepsilon}=\tilde{\Theta}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d\log\frac{\|\Sigma\|_{\text{op}}}{\alpha}}{\varepsilon}}}\right)

and apply the rescaling reduction, we get the desired error

\alpha\leqslant\tilde{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\text{tr}(\Lambda)+\|\Lambda\|_{\text{op}}\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{\varepsilon n}}}\right)=\tilde{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\text{tr}\bigl(I+\frac{1}{n}\Sigma^{-1}\bigr)^{-1}+\bigl\|\bigl(I+\frac{1}{n}\Sigma^{-1}\bigr)^{-1}\bigr\|_{\text{op}}\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{\varepsilon n}}}\right)\qed

Proof of the Efficient Upper Bound in
Theorem

1.1
.

Along the same lines as the previous proof, we apply the efficient algorithm in
Theorem

5.3
with the same
R
. Then assuming
n\geqslant\tilde{\Theta}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d\log\|\Sigma\|_{\text{op}}}{\varepsilon}}}\right)
we have

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle\leqslant\tilde{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\text{tr}(\Lambda^{4/3})+\|\Lambda^{4/3}\|_{\text{op}}\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{n\varepsilon^{2/3}}}}\right)^{3/4}+\frac{\text{tr}(\Lambda)+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\Lambda}}\right\|_{\text{op}}\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{\varepsilon n}}}\right)

as desired.
∎

A.2
From isotropic to anisotropic via bucketing

Here we will deduce
Theorem

5.3
from the special case of isotropic prior that was proven in Section
5
.

Proof of the Inefficient Variant of
Theorem

5.3
.

Our plan is to break
\Lambda^{2}
apart by eigenvalue into buckets of for
\sigma^{2}_{i}=2^{-i}\cdot\|\Lambda^{2}\|_{\text{op}}
, and run the isotropic estimator of
Theorem

5.3
with privacy parameter
\frac{\varepsilon}{M+1}
on the first
M+1
buckets and then on all of the remaining buckets output
0
. Formally, let
\alpha_{i}
be the error on bucket
\sigma^{2}_{i}
,
\Lambda^{2}_{i}
be the covariance matrix restricted to bucket
i
, and
d_{i}
be the size of bucket
i
.

\alpha\leqslant\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{M}\alpha_{i}+2^{-M/2}\cdot d\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\Lambda^{2}}}\right\|_{\text{op}}\cdot\frac{d+\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{\varepsilon n}

Let’s ignore the second term for now (and set
M
large enough later to make it negligible). The first term, using a QM-AM inequality, is at most (up to constant factors)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{M}\alpha_{i}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{M}\sigma_{i}(M+1)\cdot\frac{d_{i}+\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{\varepsilon n}

\displaystyle\leqslant(M+1)\sum_{i=1}^{M}\sigma_{i}\cdot\frac{d_{i}+\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{\varepsilon n}

\displaystyle\leqslant(M+1)\cdot\frac{\text{tr}(\Lambda)+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\Lambda}}\right\|_{\text{op}}\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{\varepsilon n}

where we used the inequalities
\sum_{i=0}^{M}\sigma_{i}^{2}\leqslant 2\|\Lambda^{2}\|_{\text{op}}
and
\sum_{i=0}^{M}\sigma_{i}\leqslant 2\|\Lambda\|_{\text{op}}
. Finally, to bound the remaining buckets it suffices to set
M=\Omega(\text{polylog}(n,d,\frac{1}{\varepsilon},\log\frac{1}{\beta}))
, completing the argument.
∎

Proof of the Efficient Variant of
Theorem

5.3
.

We use a similar approach as our proof for the efficient case above. Analogous to before,

\alpha\leqslant\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{M}\alpha_{i}+2^{-M/2}\cdot d\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\Lambda^{2}}}\right\|_{\text{op}}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d+\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{n\varepsilon^{2/3}}}}\right)^{3/4}+\frac{d+\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{\varepsilon n}}}\right)\,.

Let’s ignore the second term for now (and set
M
large enough later to make it negligible). The first term, using the concavity of
f(x)=x^{3/4}
, is at most (up to constant factors)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{M}\alpha_{i}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{M}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sigma_{i}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((M+1)^{2/3}\frac{d_{i}+\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{n\varepsilon^{2/3}}}}\right)^{3/4}+\sigma_{i}(M+1)\cdot\frac{d_{i}+\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{\varepsilon n}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant M^{1/4}\sqrt{M+1}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{M}\sigma_{i}^{4/3}\cdot\frac{d_{i}+\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{n\varepsilon^{2/3}}}}\right)^{3/4}+(M+1)\sum_{i=1}^{M}\sigma_{i}\cdot\frac{d_{i}+\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{\varepsilon n}

\displaystyle\leqslant M^{1/4}\sqrt{M+1}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\text{tr}(\Lambda^{4/3})+\|\Lambda^{4/3}\|_{\text{op}}\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{n\varepsilon^{2/3}}}}\right)^{3/4}+(M+1)\cdot\frac{\text{tr}(\Lambda)+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\Lambda}}\right\|_{\text{op}}\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{\varepsilon n}

where we used the inequalities
\sum_{i=0}^{M}\sigma_{i}^{4/3}\leqslant 2\|\Lambda^{4/3}\|_{\text{op}}
and
\sum_{i=0}^{M}\sigma_{i}\leqslant 2\|\Lambda\|_{\text{op}}
. Finally, to bound the remaining buckets it suffices to set
M=\Omega(\text{polylog}(n,d,\frac{1}{\varepsilon},\log\frac{1}{\beta}))
, completing the argument.
∎

A.3
Proof of
Section

5.3.1
: resilience bound for feasibility

In this section we prove the resilience bound needed to show that the ground truth is feasible for the main program
\mathcal{A}
in
Section

5.3.1
:

Lemma

(Restatement of
Section

5.3.1
)
.

Let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
be i.i.d. samples from
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\textup{Id}_{d})
and
\bar{x}
be their empirical mean. Then with probability
1-\beta
, for all vectors
v\in\mdmathbb{S}^{d-1}
and
b\in[0,1]^{n}
such that
\E_{i}b_{i}\geqslant 1-\eta
, we have

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\E_{i}b_{i}\langle x_{i}-\bar{x},v\rangle}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leqslant O\Bigl(\sqrt{\frac{\eta(d+\log(1/\beta))}{n}}+\eta\sqrt{\log(1/\eta)}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\E_{i}b_{i}\langle x_{i}-\bar{x},v\rangle^{2}-1}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leqslant O\Bigl(\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}+\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}+\eta\log(1/\eta)\Bigr)

Proof.

Fix
b\in[0,1]^{n}
satisfying
\E_{i}b_{i}\geqslant 1-\eta
. Let
v\in\mdmathbb{S}^{d-1}
be arbitrary.
Using
\bar{x}=\E_{i}x_{i}
, we have

\displaystyle\E_{i}b_{i}\langle x_{i}-\bar{x},v\rangle

\displaystyle=\E_{i}b_{i}\langle x_{i},v\rangle-(\E_{i}b_{i})\,\E_{j}\langle x_{j},v\rangle

\displaystyle=\E_{i}\big(b_{i}-(\E_{j}b_{j})\big)\langle x_{i},v\rangle.

Decompose
x_{i}=\mu+(x_{i}-\mu)
. Since
\E_{i}\big(b_{i}-(\E_{j}b_{j})\big)=0
, the
\mu
term cancels and

\E_{i}b_{i}\langle x_{i}-\bar{x},v\rangle=\E_{i}\big(b_{i}-(\E_{j}b_{j})\big)\langle x_{i}-\mu,v\rangle.

Now note that
u=\E_{i}(b_{i}-(\E_{j}b_{j}))(x_{i}-\mu)
is distributed as a Gaussian centered at zero and variance
\E_{i}(b_{i}-(\E_{j}b_{j}))^{2}I_{d}
(in Loewner order). Now note that we can write

\displaystyle\E_{i}(b_{i}-\E_{j}b_{j})^{2}

\displaystyle=\E_{i}b_{i}^{2}-(\E_{i}b_{i})^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant 1-(1-\eta)^{2}

\displaystyle=2\eta-\eta^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\eta\,.

Therefore, from the concentration of the
\ell_{2}
norm of a Gaussian centered at
0
with variance at most
O(\eta)I_{d}
, namely
u
,we conclude that with probability at
1-\beta
, for all
v\in\mdmathbb{S}^{d-1}
we have that

\lvert\E_{i}b_{i}\langle x_{i}-\bar{x},v\rangle\rvert\leqslant O\Bigl(\sqrt{\frac{\eta(d+\log(1/\beta))}{n}}\Bigr)\,.

Now in order to extend this to all
b
, it suffices to take a union bound over all integral points
b\in\{0,1\}^{n}
such that
\E_{i}b_{i}\geqslant 1-\eta
, since those can be the extreme points. Taking a union bound over the
{n\choose\eta n}\leqslant(e/\eta)^{\eta n}\leqslant\exp(O(\eta\log(1/\eta)n))
many such
b
’s taking a union bound over them gives us that with probability
1-\beta
, for all vectors
v\in\mdmathbb{S}^{d-1}
and
b\in[0,1]^{n}
such that
\E_{i}b_{i}\geqslant 1-\eta
, we have

\lvert\E_{i}b_{i}\langle x_{i}-\bar{x},v\rangle\rvert\leqslant O\Bigl(\sqrt{\frac{\eta(d+\log(1/\beta))}{n}}+\eta\sqrt{\log(1/\eta)}\Bigr)\,.

as desired.

Now we prove the second part of the lemma. Fix
b\in\{0,1\}^{n}
satisfying
\E_{i}b_{i}\geqslant 1-\eta
. We can write

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\E_{i}b_{i}\langle x_{i}-\bar{x},v\rangle^{2}-1}}\right\rvert\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\E_{i}\langle x_{i}-\bar{x},v\rangle^{2}-1}}\right\rvert+\E_{i}(1-b_{i})\langle x_{i}-\bar{x},v\rangle^{2}\,.

We bound each term separately.
For the first term we can write

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\E_{i}\langle x_{i}-\bar{x},v\rangle^{2}-1}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\E_{i}\langle x_{i}-\mu,v\rangle^{2}-\langle\bar{x}-\mu,v\rangle^{2}-1}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\E_{i}\langle x_{i}-\mu,v\rangle^{2}-1}}\right\rvert+\langle\bar{x}-\mu,v\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\E_{i}(x_{i}-\mu){(x_{i}-\mu)}{}^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}-I_{d}}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\bar{x}-\mu}}\right\rVert^{2}_{2}\,.

From standard concentration bounds of a standard Gaussian we know that with probability
1-\beta
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\E_{i}(x_{i}-\mu){(x_{i}-\mu)}{}^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}-I_{d}}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant O\Bigl(\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}+\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}\Bigr),\quad\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\bar{x}-\mu}}\right\rVert^{2}_{2}\leqslant O\Bigl(\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}\Bigr)\,.

Therefore, with probability
1-\beta
, for all
v\in\mdmathbb{S}^{d-1}
we obtain

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\E_{i}\langle x_{i}-\bar{x},v\rangle^{2}-1}}\right\rvert\leqslant O\Bigl(\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}+\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}\Bigr)\,.

Now we bound the second term. Adding and subtracting
\mu
, we can write

\displaystyle\E_{i}(1-b_{i})\langle x_{i}-\bar{x},v\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\E_{i}(1-b_{i})\langle x_{i}-\mu,v\rangle^{2}+2\E_{i}(1-b_{i})\langle\mu-\bar{x},v\rangle^{2}\,.

The second term we can show is bounded from above by
O\Bigl(\frac{\eta(d+\log(1/\beta))}{n}\Bigr)
, with probability
1-\beta
, by applying standard bounds on concentration of a Gaussian and the assumption that
\E_{i}b_{i}\geqslant 1-\eta
. It remains to bound the first term. Again applying the standard concentration bound for the empirical covariance we can write that with probability
1-\beta

\E_{i}(1-b_{i})\langle x_{i}-\mu,v\rangle^{2}\leqslant\eta\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[1+O\Bigl(\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}+\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}\Bigr)}}\right]\,.

Overall for a fixed
b
we obtain that with probability
1-\beta
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lvert\E_{i}b_{i}\langle x_{i}-\bar{x},v\rangle^{2}-1}}\right\rvert\leqslant O\Bigl(\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}+\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}+\eta\Bigr)\,.

Taking a union bound over the choice of
\beta
gives us the desired bound.

∎

A.4
Isotropic estimation lemma

Here we will state and prove the following lemma, which is an adaption of Lemma 4.1 in
[
KMZ22
]
and which is a crucial ingredient in the proof in
Section

5.3.1
. As we will only require the lemma in the case where
V(\mu_{0},v)=1
, that’s the setting where we state and prove here as well. We will follow their strategy exactly, the only difference is that we adapt it to the setting where we do not necessarily have the resilience guarantees with
O(\eta)
error and instead might have weaker as is the case in the
n\ll d/\eta^{2}
regime for Gaussian mean estimation.

Lemma A.1
.

Let
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in\mdmathbb{R}^{d}
, and fix a reference mean
\mu_{0}\in\mdmathbb{R}^{d}
. Assume the following
robust moment bounds
: for every
v\in S
and every weight vector
a=(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})\in[0,1]^{n}
with
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\geqslant(1-\eta)n
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\langle x_{i}-\mu_{0},\ v\rangle}}\right|\ \leqslant\ \alpha_{1},\qquad\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\big(\langle x_{i}-\mu_{0},\ v\rangle^{2}-1\big)}}\right|\ \leqslant\ \alpha_{2}.

(42)

Let
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
be an
\eta
-corruption of
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
. Let
\tilde{\mdmathbb{E}}
be a pseudo-expectation of sufficiently high degree over formal variables
x^{\prime}_{1},\dots,x^{\prime}_{n}\in\mdmathbb{R}^{d}
and
w_{1},\dots,w_{n}
, and let
\mu^{\prime}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x^{\prime}_{i}
. Suppose:

1.

\tilde{\mdmathbb{E}}[w_{i}^{2}=w_{i}]
for all
i\in[n]
,

2.

\tilde{\mdmathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}}}\right]=(1-\eta)n
,

3.

\tilde{\mdmathbb{E}}[w_{i}x^{\prime}_{i}=w_{i}y_{i}]
for all
i\in[n]
,

4.

for every
v\in S
,
\displaystyle\tilde{\mdmathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle x^{\prime}_{i}-\mu^{\prime},\ v\rangle^{2}}}\right]\leqslant 1+\alpha_{0}
.

Define
w^{\prime}_{i}:=w_{i}\mathbf{1}\{x_{i}=y_{i}\}
and set
a_{i}:=\tilde{\mdmathbb{E}}[w^{\prime}_{i}]
for
i\in[n]
. Then
a_{i}\in[0,1]
and
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}a_{i}\geqslant 1-2\eta
. For every
v\in S
, writing
\widehat{\mu}:=\tilde{\mdmathbb{E}}[\mu^{\prime}]
, the following hold:

\displaystyle\tilde{\mdmathbb{E}}\big[\langle\mu^{\prime}-\mu_{0},\ v\rangle^{2}\big]

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ \frac{4\eta(\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{2})+2\alpha_{1}^{2}}{1-4\eta}\ \ \leqslant\ 8\eta(\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{2})+4\alpha_{1}^{2}\quad(\eta\leqslant\tfrac{1}{8}),

(43)

\displaystyle\Big|\langle\widehat{\mu}-\mu_{0},\ v\rangle\Big|

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ \alpha_{1}\ +\ \sqrt{2\eta\Big(\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{2}+\tilde{\mdmathbb{E}}\big[\langle\mu^{\prime}-\mu_{0},\ v\rangle^{2}\big]\Big)}.

(44)

In particular, combining (
43
) and (
44
) gives the bound

\Big|\langle\widehat{\mu}-\mu_{0},\ v\rangle\Big|\ \leqslant\ \alpha_{1}\ +\ \sqrt{C_{1}\eta(\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{2})\ +\ C_{2}\eta\alpha_{1}^{2}\ +\ C_{3}\eta^{2}(\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{2})}\ \leqslant\ C\Big(\alpha_{1}+\sqrt{\eta(\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{2})}+\eta\Big),

(45)

for universal constants
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3},C>0
. Thus the rate

\alpha_{1}\ +\ \sqrt{\eta(\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{2})\ +\ \eta^{2}}

is valid up to universal constant factors.

Proof of Lemma
A.4
.

Define
w^{\prime}_{i}:=w_{i}\mathbf{1}\{x_{i}=y_{i}\}
. Then
\tilde{\mdmathbb{E}}[{w^{\prime}_{i}}^{2}=w^{\prime}_{i}]
,
\tilde{\mdmathbb{E}}[w^{\prime}_{i}x^{\prime}_{i}=w^{\prime}_{i}x_{i}]
, and
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{\mdmathbb{E}}[1-w^{\prime}_{i}]\leqslant 2\eta
. Set
a_{i}:=\tilde{\mdmathbb{E}}[w^{\prime}_{i}]
; then
a_{i}\in[0,1]
and
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}a_{i}\geqslant 1-2\eta
.

Step 1: Decompose the estimation error.

For any
v\in S
,

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{\mu}-\mu_{0},\ v\rangle

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mdmathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle x^{\prime}_{i}-\mu_{0},\ v\rangle}}\right]

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mdmathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-w^{\prime}_{i})\langle x^{\prime}_{i}-\mu_{0},\ v\rangle}}\right]+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{\mdmathbb{E}}[w^{\prime}_{i}]\ \langle x_{i}-\mu_{0},\ v\rangle.

Hence,

\Big|\langle\widehat{\mu}-\mu_{0},\ v\rangle\Big|\ \leqslant\ \underbrace{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\tilde{\mdmathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-w^{\prime}_{i})\langle x^{\prime}_{i}-\mu_{0},\ v\rangle}}\right]}}\right|}_{\text{(A)}}+\underbrace{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\langle x_{i}-\mu_{0},\ v\rangle}}\right|}_{\leqslant\ \alpha_{1}\ \text{ by \eqref{eq:robust-assumptions-unit}}}.

(46)

Applying Cauchy–Schwarz to (A) and
{w^{\prime}_{i}}^{2}=w^{\prime}_{i}
, we get

\displaystyle\text{(A)}^{2}

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ \tilde{\mdmathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-w^{\prime}_{i})\langle x^{\prime}_{i}-\mu_{0},\ v\rangle}}\right)^{2}}}\right]\ \leqslant\ \tilde{\mdmathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-w^{\prime}_{i})}}\right]\cdot\tilde{\mdmathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-w^{\prime}_{i})\langle x^{\prime}_{i}-\mu_{0},\ v\rangle^{2}}}\right]

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ 2\eta\cdot\tilde{\mdmathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-w^{\prime}_{i})\langle x^{\prime}_{i}-\mu_{0},\ v\rangle^{2}}}\right].

(47)

Substituting
w^{\prime}_{i}x^{\prime}_{i}=w^{\prime}_{i}x_{i}
,

\displaystyle\tilde{\mdmathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-w^{\prime}_{i})\langle x^{\prime}_{i}-\mu_{0},\ v\rangle^{2}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mdmathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle x^{\prime}_{i}-\mu_{0},\ v\rangle^{2}}}\right]-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\langle x_{i}-\mu_{0},\ v\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\ \underbrace{\tilde{\mdmathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle x^{\prime}_{i}-\mu^{\prime},\ v\rangle^{2}}}\right]}_{\leqslant\ 1+\alpha_{0}}+\tilde{\mdmathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\langle\mu^{\prime}-\mu_{0},\ v\rangle^{2}}}\right]-\underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\langle x_{i}-\mu_{0},\ v\rangle^{2}}_{\geqslant\ 1-\alpha_{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\ \alpha_{0}+\alpha_{2}\ +\ \tilde{\mdmathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\langle\mu^{\prime}-\mu_{0},\ v\rangle^{2}}}\right].

(48)

Combining (
47
) and (
48
),

\text{(A)}^{2}\ \leqslant\ 2\eta\Big(\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{2}+\tilde{\mdmathbb{E}}\big[\langle\mu^{\prime}-\mu_{0},\ v\rangle^{2}\big]\Big).

(49)

Inserting (
49
) into (
46
) yields (
44
).

Now we bound
\tilde{\mdmathbb{E}}[\langle\mu^{\prime}-\mu_{0},\ v\rangle^{2}]
.
Decompose

\langle\mu^{\prime}-\mu_{0},\ v\rangle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-w^{\prime}_{i})\langle x^{\prime}_{i}-\mu_{0},\ v\rangle+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\langle x_{i}-\mu_{0},\ v\rangle.

By
(x+y)^{2}\leqslant 2x^{2}+2y^{2}
, (
49
), and (
42
),

\displaystyle\tilde{\mdmathbb{E}}\big[\langle\mu^{\prime}-\mu_{0},\ v\rangle^{2}\big]

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\text{(A)}^{2}+2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\langle x_{i}-\mu_{0},\ v\rangle}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant 4\eta\Big(\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{2}+\tilde{\mdmathbb{E}}\big[\langle\mu^{\prime}-\mu_{0},\ v\rangle^{2}\big]\Big)+2\alpha_{1}^{2}.

Rearranging gives (
43
). Substituting (
43
) into (
44
) and simplifying gives us (
45
).
∎

Appendix B
Frequentist Implications

In this section we prove the frequentist consequences of our main results, as stated in
Theorem

1.2
and
Theorem

1.5
.

B.1
Frequentist mean estimation

Proof of
Theorem

1.2
.

Except with probability
\frac{\beta}{3}
, the sample mean
\bar{x}
satisfies

\|\bar{x}\|_{2}\leqslant O\Bigl(R+\sqrt{\text{Tr}(\Lambda^{2})+\log 1/\beta}\Bigr).

Condition on the above. By
Theorem

5.3
, whenever

n\geqslant\tilde{\Omega}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\log\Bigl(\frac{R+\|\Lambda\|_{F}}{\alpha}\Bigr)}}\right)\,,

where the
\tilde{\Omega}
hides factors of
\log\log\frac{1}{\beta}
, except with probability
\frac{\beta}{3}
our inefficient estimator outputs
\hat{\mu}
such that

\|\bar{x}-\hat{\mu}\|\leqslant\tilde{O}\biggl(\frac{\tr(\Lambda)+\lVert\Lambda\rVert_{\sf op}\log(1/\beta)}{\varepsilon n}\biggr)\,,

and our efficient estimator outputs
\hat{\mu}
such that

\|\bar{x}-\hat{\mu}\|\leqslant\tilde{O}\biggl(\frac{\lVert\Lambda\rVert_{4/3}+\lVert\Lambda\rVert_{\sf op}\log^{3/4}(1/\beta)}{\varepsilon^{1/2}n^{3/4}}+\frac{\tr(\Lambda)+\lVert\Lambda\rVert_{\sf op}\log(1/\beta)}{\varepsilon n}\biggr)

Finally, except with probability
\frac{\beta}{3}
,

\|\bar{x}-\mu\|_{2}\leqslant\sqrt{\frac{\|\Lambda\|_{F}^{2}+\|\Lambda\|_{\text{op}}^{2}\log\frac{3}{\beta}}{n}}=(1+o(1))\sqrt{\frac{\|\Lambda\|_{F}^{2}+\|\Lambda\|_{\text{op}}^{2}\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{n}}.

where the
o(1)
holds in regimes where the effective dimension
\|\Lambda\|_{F}/\|\Lambda\|_{\text{op}}\to\infty
or
\beta\to 0
. The theorem follows by a union bound over all three events and a triangle inequality.
∎

B.2
Frequentist linear regression

In this part, we give the proof of
Theorem

1.5
.
We first give a lemma for the error rate achieved by ordinary least square estimator.

Lemma B.1
.

Let

X:=[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]\in\mdmathbb{R}^{d\times n}
,

y=X^{\intercal}w+\xi
,

\xi\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\textup{Id}_{n}).
,
and let

w_{\mathrm{OLS}}:=(XX^{\intercal})^{-1}Xy.

For any
\beta\in(0,1/2)
, define

r_{\beta}:=C\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(4/\beta)}{n}},

where
C>0
is a sufficiently large absolute constant. If
r_{\beta}\leqslant 1/2
, then with
probability at least
1-\beta
,

\|w_{\mathrm{OLS}}-w\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{(1-r_{\beta})n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(d+2\sqrt{d\log\frac{4}{\beta}}+2\log\frac{4}{\beta}}}\right).

Consequently, if
r_{\beta}=o(1)
then for some universal constant
c
,

\|w_{\mathrm{OLS}}-w\|_{2}\leqslant(1+o(1))\sqrt{\frac{d+c\log(1/\beta)}{n}}.

Proof.

Consider the random event that

\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{cov}}:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\frac{1}{n}XX^{\intercal}-\textup{Id}}}\right\|_{\sf op}\leqslant r_{\beta}}}\right\}.

By Theorem C.1, after enlarging
C
if necessary,

\ProbOp(\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{cov}})\geqslant 1-\beta/2.

On
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{cov}}
, in particular
XX^{\intercal}
is invertible and

(1-r_{\beta})n\textup{Id}\preceq XX^{\intercal}\preceq(1+r_{\beta})n\textup{Id}.

Also,

w_{\mathrm{OLS}}-w=(XX^{\intercal})^{-1}X\xi.

Conditional on
X
, this is a centered Gaussian in
\mdmathbb{R}^{d}
with covariance

\Sigma_{X}:=(XX^{\intercal})^{-1}.

Therefore, by the standard Gaussian tail bound, conditional on
X
, with
probability at least
1-\beta/2
,

\|w_{\mathrm{OLS}}-w\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant\tr(\Sigma_{X})+2\sqrt{\tr(\Sigma_{X}^{2})\log\frac{2}{\beta}}+2\|\Sigma_{X}\|_{\sf op}\log\frac{2}{\beta}.

On
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{cov}}
we have

\tr(\Sigma_{X})\leqslant\frac{d}{(1-r_{\beta})n},\qquad\tr(\Sigma_{X}^{2})\leqslant\frac{d}{(1-r_{\beta})^{2}n^{2}},\qquad\|\Sigma_{X}\|_{\sf op}\leqslant\frac{1}{(1-r_{\beta})n}.

Substituting these bounds yields that on
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{cov}}
, with conditional
probability at least
1-\delta/2
,

\|w_{\mathrm{OLS}}-w\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{(1-r_{\beta})n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(d+2\sqrt{d\log\frac{2}{\beta}}+2\log\frac{2}{\beta}}}\right).

A union bound proves the first claim, and the second follows immediately from

r_{\beta}=o(1)
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

1.5
.

Following similar proofs as
Theorem

6.15
and
Theorem

6.2
, with
\eta=0
and the radius bound
R
, whenever the assumptions of
Theorem

1.5
hold,
with probability at least
1-\frac{\beta}{3}
our inefficient estimator outputs
\hat{w}

such that

\|\hat{w}-w_{\mathrm{OLS}}\|_{2}\leqslant\widetilde{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d+\log(3/\beta)}{\varepsilon n}}}\right)=\widetilde{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{\varepsilon n}}}\right),

and except with probability
\frac{\beta}{3}
our efficient estimator outputs
\hat{w}

such that

\|\hat{w}-w_{\mathrm{OLS}}\|_{2}\leqslant\widetilde{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{(d+\log(3/\beta))^{3/4}}{\varepsilon^{1/2}n^{3/4}}+\frac{d+\log(3/\beta)}{\varepsilon n}}}\right)=\widetilde{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{(d+\log(1/\beta))^{3/4}}{\varepsilon^{1/2}n^{3/4}}+\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{\varepsilon n}}}\right).

Finally, applying
Section

B.2
with

\delta=\beta/3
, except with probability
\beta/3
,

\|w_{\mathrm{OLS}}-w\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{(1-r_{\beta})n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(d+2\sqrt{d\log\frac{12}{\beta}}+2\log\frac{12}{\beta}}}\right),

where

r_{\beta}:=C\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(12/\beta)}{n}}=o(1).

In particular, if
\log(1/\beta)=o(d)
, then

\|w_{\mathrm{OLS}}-w\|_{2}\leqslant(1+o(1))\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}.

The theorem follows by a union bound over these three events and the triangle inequality
between
w
,
w_{\mathrm{OLS}}
, and
\hat{w}
.
∎

Appendix C
Deferred Proofs From
Section

6

C.1
Concentration inequalities for feasibility

Here we prove concentration inequalities that were used in the proof that the ground truth is feasible for the program
\mathcal{A}
in
Algorithm

2
.

Theorem C.1
.

Let
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}
be i.i.d sampled from Gaussian distribution
N(0,\Sigma)
.
Then with probability at least
1-\beta
, we have

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in[n]}x_{i}x_{i}^{\intercal}-\Sigma}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant\sqrt{\frac{\Tr(\Sigma)+\log(1/\beta)\lVert\Sigma\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}}{n}}\,.

Theorem C.2

(Maximal subset of Gaussian coordinates)
.

Let
v=(v_{1},\dots,v_{d})\sim N(0,\textup{Id}_{d})
, let
\eta\in(0,1)
, and let
\beta\in(0,1)
.
Set
m:=\eta d
. Then with probability at least
1-\beta
(over the draw of
v
),

\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq[d]\\
|S|=m\end{subarray}}\;\sum_{i\in S}v_{i}^{2}\;\leqslant\;C\Bigl(\eta d\log\frac{e}{\eta}+\log\frac{1}{\beta}\Bigr),

(50)

where
C>0
is an absolute constant.
In words: simultaneously for every subset
S
of coordinates of size
\eta d
, the sum of squares of those coordinates is at most the right-hand side.

Proof.

Fix any subset
S\subseteq[d]
of size
m=\eta d
. Since
v\sim N(0,I_{d})
, the random variable

X_{S}:=\sum_{i\in S}v_{i}^{2}

has the
\chi^{2}
distribution with
m
degrees of freedom, i.e.
X_{S}\sim\chi^{2}_{m}
.
We will use the standard Laurent–Massart tail bound for the chi-square distribution: if
X\sim\chi^{2}_{m}
, then for every
x>0
,

\ProbOp\bigl(X\geqslant m+2\sqrt{mx}+2x\bigr)\leqslant e^{-x}.

(51)

Apply (
51
) to
X_{S}
for a parameter
x>0
to be chosen later:

\ProbOp\bigl(X_{S}\geqslant m+2\sqrt{mx}+2x\bigr)\leqslant e^{-x}.

(52)

We want the upper bound to hold
simultaneously
for every subset
S
of size
m
.
The total number of such subsets is

\binom{d}{m}\leqslant\Bigl(\frac{ed}{m}\Bigr)^{m}=\Bigl(\frac{e}{\eta}\Bigr)^{\eta d}.

(53)

By the union bound, combining (
52
) and (
53
), we obtain

\ProbOp\Bigl(\exists S\subseteq[d],|S|=m:X_{S}\geqslant m+2\sqrt{mx}+2x\Bigr)\;\leqslant\;\binom{d}{m}e^{-x}\;\leqslant\;\Bigl(\frac{e}{\eta}\Bigr)^{\eta d}e^{-x}.

Choose

x:=\eta d\log\frac{e}{\eta}+\log\frac{1}{\beta}.

(54)

Then

\Bigl(\frac{e}{\eta}\Bigr)^{\eta d}e^{-x}=\exp\bigl(\eta d\log(e/\eta)\bigr)\;\exp\bigl(-\eta d\log(e/\eta)-\log(1/\beta)\bigr)=\beta.

Hence, with probability at least
1-\beta
,

\forall S\subseteq[d],|S|=m:\quad\sum_{i\in S}v_{i}^{2}\;\leqslant\;m+2\sqrt{mx}+2x,

(55)

where
x
is given by (
54
).

It remains to simplify the right-hand side.
Recall that
m=\eta d
and

x=\eta d\log\frac{e}{\eta}+\log\frac{1}{\beta}.

Then

m+2x\;\leqslant\;3\eta d\log\frac{e}{\eta}+2\log\frac{1}{\beta},

and

2\sqrt{mx}=2\sqrt{\eta d\bigl(\eta d\log(e/\eta)+\log(1/\beta)\bigr)}\;\leqslant\;\eta d\log\frac{e}{\eta}+\log\frac{1}{\beta},

after enlarging constants (this is a standard inequality of the form
2\sqrt{ab}\leqslant a+b
).
Therefore, for a universal constant
C>0
,

m+2\sqrt{mx}+2x\;\leqslant\;C\Bigl(\eta d\log\frac{e}{\eta}+\log\frac{1}{\beta}\Bigr).

Plugging this back into (
55
) gives exactly (
50
).
∎

Theorem C.3

(Order statistic of a Gaussian vector with sharp
\beta
-dependence)
.

Let
v=(v_{1},\dots,v_{d})\sim N(0,\textup{Id}_{d})
, and let
\eta,\beta\in(0,1)
.
Order the squared coordinates in nonincreasing order:

v_{(1)}^{2}\geqslant v_{(2)}^{2}\geqslant\dots\geqslant v_{(d)}^{2}.

Then, with probability at least
1-\beta
,

v_{(\lceil\eta d\rceil)}^{2}\;\leqslant\;2\log\frac{e}{\eta}\;+\;\frac{2}{\eta d}\log\frac{1}{\beta}.

(56)

Equivalently: with probability at least
1-\beta
,
at most

\eta d
coordinates of
v
have squared value exceeding the right-hand side of (
56
).

Proof.

Fix
\eta\in(0,1)
and set
m:=\lceil\eta d\rceil
.
Let
T>0
be a threshold to be chosen.
We want to bound the probability that there exist
m
coordinates of
v
all having squared value at least
T
.

For a single coordinate
v_{i}\sim N(0,1)
we have

\ProbOp(v_{i}^{2}\geqslant T)=\ProbOp(|v_{i}|\geqslant\sqrt{T})\leqslant e^{-T/2}.

Now fix a subset
S\subseteq[d]
with
|S|=m
. By independence,

\ProbOp\bigl(v_{i}^{2}\geqslant T\text{ for all }i\in S\bigr)\;\leqslant\;\bigl(e^{-T/2}\bigr)^{m}=e^{-(T/2)m}.

There are
\binom{d}{m}
such subsets
S
. By the union bound,

\ProbOp\Bigl(\exists S\subseteq[d],|S|=m:v_{i}^{2}\geqslant T\ \forall i\in S\Bigr)\;\leqslant\;\binom{d}{m}\,e^{-(T/2)m}.

(57)

We will upper bound the binomial coefficient by the standard entropy-like bound

\binom{d}{m}\leqslant\Bigl(\frac{ed}{m}\Bigr)^{m}.

Since
m=\lceil\eta d\rceil\geqslant\eta d
, we have

\binom{d}{m}\leqslant\Bigl(\frac{ed}{\eta d}\Bigr)^{m}=\Bigl(\frac{e}{\eta}\Bigr)^{m}.

Plugging into (
57
) gives

\ProbOp\Bigl(\exists S:|S|=m,\ v_{i}^{2}\geqslant T\ \forall i\in S\Bigr)\;\leqslant\;\Bigl(\frac{e}{\eta}\Bigr)^{m}\,e^{-(T/2)m}=\exp\Bigl(m\bigl(\log\tfrac{e}{\eta}-\tfrac{T}{2}\bigr)\Bigr).

We want the right-hand side to be at most
\beta
. Since
m\geqslant\eta d
, it suffices to require

\exp\Bigl(\eta d\bigl(\log\tfrac{e}{\eta}-\tfrac{T}{2}\bigr)\Bigr)\leqslant\beta.

Taking logs,

\eta d\bigl(\log\tfrac{e}{\eta}-\tfrac{T}{2}\bigr)\leqslant\log\beta\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad-\tfrac{T}{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{\eta d}\log\beta-\log\tfrac{e}{\eta}.

Multiply by
-1
(and flip the inequality):

\frac{T}{2}\geqslant\log\tfrac{e}{\eta}+\frac{1}{\eta d}\log\frac{1}{\beta}.

So it is enough to choose

T:=2\log\frac{e}{\eta}+\frac{2}{\eta d}\log\frac{1}{\beta}.

With this choice of
T
, the probability in (
57
) is at most
\beta
.

But the event in (
57
) is exactly:
there exist
m
coordinates all
\geqslant T
.
Then the complement is given by at most
m-1\leqslant\eta d
coordinates exceed
T
.
Equivalently, in that complement event we have

v_{(m)}^{2}\leqslant T,

i.e.

v_{(\lceil\eta d\rceil)}^{2}\leqslant 2\log\frac{e}{\eta}+\frac{2}{\eta d}\log\frac{1}{\beta},

with probability at least
1-\beta
, which is exactly (
56
).
∎

Finally, we use the largest sparse singular values of the Gaussian submatrices.

Lemma C.4

(Sparse spectral norm of Gaussian submatrices)
.

Let
X\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{d\times n}
have i.i.d. columns
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
.
For an index set
S\subseteq[n]
, let
X_{S}\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{d\times|S|}
denote the submatrix formed by the
columns indexed by
S
.
For
k\in[n]
, recall the definition of
k
-sparse spectral norm(
Section

4.5
)

\|X\|_{\mathrm{sp}}(k)\;\coloneqq\;\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq[n]\\
|S|\leqslant k\end{subarray}}\ \|X_{S}\|_{\mathsf{op}}\,.

Then there exists a universal constant
C>0
such that for every
k\in[n]
and
\beta\in(0,1)
,
with probability at least
1-\beta
,

\|X\|_{\mathrm{sp}}(k)\;\leqslant\;C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sqrt{d}\;+\;\sqrt{k\log\frac{en}{k}}\;+\;\sqrt{\log\frac{1}{\beta}}}}\right).

Equivalently, on the same event, for all
S\subseteq[n]
with
|S|\leqslant k
,

\|X_{S}\|_{\mathsf{op}}^{2}\;\leqslant\;C^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(d\;+\;k\log\frac{en}{k}\;+\;\log\frac{1}{\beta}}}\right).

Proof.

Fix
s\in\{1,\dots,k\}
and a set
S\subseteq[n]
with
|S|=s
. By a standard operator-norm tail
bound for a
d\times s
Gaussian matrix, for all
t\geqslant 0
,

\ProbOp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\|X_{S}\|_{\mathsf{op}}\geqslant\sqrt{d}+\sqrt{s}+t}}\right]\leqslant 2e^{-t^{2}/2}.

(58)

There are at most
\binom{n}{s}\leqslant(en/s)^{s}
such subsets. Set

t_{s}\;\coloneqq\;\sqrt{2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(s\log\frac{en}{s}+\log\frac{2k}{\beta}}}\right)}.

By a union bound and (
58
),

\ProbOp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\max_{|S|=s}\|X_{S}\|_{\mathsf{op}}\geqslant\sqrt{d}+\sqrt{s}+t_{s}}}\right]\leqslant\binom{n}{s}\cdot 2e^{-t_{s}^{2}/2}\leqslant\frac{\beta}{k}.

Taking a union bound over
s=1,\dots,k
yields that with probability at least
1-\beta
, for all

S
with
|S|\leqslant k
,

\|X_{S}\|_{\mathsf{op}}\leqslant\sqrt{d}+\sqrt{|S|}+t_{|S|}\leqslant\sqrt{d}+\sqrt{k}+\sqrt{2k\log\frac{en}{k}}+\sqrt{2\log\frac{2k}{\beta}}.

Absorbing
\sqrt{k}
and
\sqrt{\log(2k/\beta)}
into the displayed expression (by enlarging the
universal constant
C
) gives the stated bound.
∎

C.2
Proof of
Theorem

6.2
: Inefficient algorithm for robust and private Bayesian regression

In this section, we first give an inefficient robust algorithm for Bayesian regression.
For simplicity, we base our algorithm on our previous robust and efficient linear regression estimator.
As in empirical mean estimation, we can exploit better resilience bound for approximating the posterior mean estimator(or equivalently ridge regression estimator) robustly.
Finally we use the black-box robustness to privacy reduction
[
HKMN23
,
AUZ23
]
for developing a differentially private algorithm with optimal error guarantees.

C.2.1
Inefficient algorithm for robust regression

Theorem C.5
.

Let
\Sigma=\sigma^{2}\textup{Id}_{d}
for known parameter
\sigma>0
.
Let
\beta\in[0,1]
.
Then there is a computationally inefficient algorithm that, given
\eta
-corrupted samples
(X_{\mathrm{input}},y_{\mathrm{input}})
generated as in
Section

6
, outputs an estimator
\hat{w}
such that with probability at least
1-\beta
,

\lVert\hat{w}-\E[w^{*}\mid{X^{\circ}},{y^{\circ}}]\rVert_{2}\leqslant O\biggl(\eta\log(1/\eta)\biggr)\,,

where
({X^{\circ}},{y^{\circ}})
are the uncorrupted samples, under the condition that
n\geqslant(d+\log(1/\beta))/\eta
..

Throughout,
({X^{\circ}},{y^{\circ}})
denotes the uncorrupted sample, and
(X_{\mathrm{input}},y_{\mathrm{input}})
denotes the

\eta
-corrupted sample. Let
I^{\star}\subseteq[n]
be the (unknown) set of uncorrupted indices, so

|I^{\star}|\geqslant(1-\eta)n
.

Assume
\Sigma=\sigma^{2}\textup{Id}_{d}
and define

\lambda\coloneqq\frac{1}{n\sigma^{2}},\qquad A^{\star}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}{X^{\circ}}{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal},\qquad b^{\star}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}{X^{\circ}}{y^{\circ}}.

Then the posterior mean is

w_{\rm post}\coloneqq\E[w\mid{X^{\circ}},{y^{\circ}}]=(A^{\star}+\lambda\textup{Id}_{d})^{-1}b^{\star}.

For any
u\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{d}
define the (clean) residual vector

r^{\star}(u)\coloneqq{y^{\circ}}-{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}u\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{n},\qquad g^{\star}(u)\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}{X^{\circ}}\,r^{\star}(u)\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{d}.

Note
g^{\star}(u)=b^{\star}-A^{\star}u
, and hence the following identity holds for every
u
:

w_{\rm post}=u+(A^{\star}+\lambda\textup{Id}_{d})^{-1}\bigl(g^{\star}(u)-\lambda u\bigr).

(59)

Define failure probabilities
\beta_{1}=\beta_{2}=\beta_{3}=\beta/3
.

For a sequence
\{z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\subset{\mdmathbb{R}}^{d}
and
\eta\in(0,1)
define the
resilience

\mathrm{Res}_{\eta}(z)\coloneqq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq[n]\\
|S|\leqslant\eta n\end{subarray}}\Bigl\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in S}z_{i}\Bigr\|_{2}.

We define our algorithm as
Algorithm

4
.

Algorithm 4

(Two-stage robust posterior mean estimation)
.

Input:

\eta
-corrupted design matrix
X_{\mathrm{input}}\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{d\times n}
(columns
x_{i}
),
responses
y_{\mathrm{input}}\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{n}
, corruption fraction
\eta\in(0,1/2)
, failure probability
\beta\in(0,1)
,
prior variance
\sigma>0
(so
\Sigma=\sigma^{2}I_{d}
).

Output:
An estimator
\hat{w}\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{d}
of the posterior mean
w_{\rm post}=\E[w\mid{X^{\circ}},{y^{\circ}}]
.

1.

Assume
n\gtrsim(d+\log(1/\beta))/\eta
.
Set
\beta_{1}=\beta_{2}=\beta_{3}=\beta/3
and
\lambda\leftarrow(n\sigma^{2})^{-1}
.

2.

(
Stage 1: rough robust regression
)
Run the polynomial time robust regression algorithm from
Theorem

6.3
to obtain an estimator
\tilde{w}
.

3.

(
Stage 2: residualization
)
Compute residuals
r\leftarrow y_{\mathrm{input}}-X_{\mathrm{input}}^{\intercal}\tilde{w}
and vectors

v\leftarrow X_{\mathrm{input}}r
.

4.

Set

\delta_{0}\leftarrow C_{1}\Big(\sqrt{\tfrac{d+\log(1/\beta_{1})}{n}}+\eta\Big),

and

\tau\leftarrow 2\eta\,\delta_{0}+C_{2}(1+\delta_{0})\Big(\sqrt{\eta\log\tfrac{1}{\eta}}\sqrt{\tfrac{d+\log(1/\beta_{2})}{n}}+\eta\log\tfrac{1}{\eta}\Big).

5.

Run
Algorithm

5
on
(\{v_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n},\eta,\tau)
to obtain
\hat{g}
.

6.

Return

\hat{w}\leftarrow\tilde{w}+\frac{1}{1+\lambda}\big(\hat{g}-\lambda\tilde{w}\big).

Algorithm 5

(Robust mean estimation by replacement)
.

Input:
Vectors
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{d}
, corruption fraction
\eta\in(0,1/2)
,
resilience bound
\tau>0
.

Output:
A robust estimate
\hat{g}
of the clean mean
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}v_{i}^{\star}
.

1.

Find vectors
v^{\prime}_{1},\dots,v^{\prime}_{n}\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{d}
and an index set
J\subseteq[n]
with
|J|\leqslant\eta n

such that:

(a)

v^{\prime}_{i}=v_{i}
for all
i\notin J
;

(b)

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq[n]\\
|S|\leqslant\eta n\end{subarray}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in S}v^{\prime}_{i}}}\right\|_{2}\leqslant\tau.

2.

Output

\hat{g}\leftarrow\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}v^{\prime}_{i}.

Lemma C.6

(Certificate
\Rightarrow
closeness to the clean mean)
.

Let
\{z_{i}\}
be
\eta
-corrupted from an unknown clean sequence
\{z_{i}^{\star}\}
, i.e. they differ on
at most
\eta n
indices. Suppose there exists
\tau
such that

\mathrm{Res}_{2\eta}(z^{\star})\leqslant\tau
and

Algorithm

5
outputs a replacement
z^{\prime}
satisfying

\mathrm{Res}_{2\eta}(z^{\prime})\leqslant\tau
. Then

\Bigl\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}z^{\prime}_{i}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}^{\star}\Bigr\|_{2}\leqslant 2\tau.

Proof.

Let
\Delta\coloneqq\{i:z^{\prime}_{i}\neq z_{i}^{\star}\}
. Since
z
differs from
z^{\star}
on at most
\eta n

indices and
z^{\prime}
differs from
z
on at most
\eta n
indices, we have
|\Delta|\leqslant 2\eta n
.
Then

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(z^{\prime}_{i}-z_{i}^{\star})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in\Delta}(z^{\prime}_{i}-z_{i}^{\star}),

so by triangle inequality,

\Bigl\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(z^{\prime}_{i}-z_{i}^{\star})\Bigr\|_{2}\leqslant\Bigl\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in\Delta}z^{\prime}_{i}\Bigr\|_{2}+\Bigl\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in\Delta}z_{i}^{\star}\Bigr\|_{2}\leqslant\mathrm{Res}_{2\eta}(z^{\prime})+\mathrm{Res}_{2\eta}(z^{\star})\leqslant 2\tau.

∎

Now we provide the resilience bound.
Define the (clean) residuals at
\tilde{w}
:

r_{i}^{\star}\coloneqq{y^{\circ}}(i)-\langle{X^{\circ}}(\cdot,i),\tilde{w}\rangle=\langle{X^{\circ}}(\cdot,i),w-\tilde{w}\rangle+\xi_{i},\qquad\xi_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1),

and the clean vectors

v_{i}^{\star}\coloneqq{X^{\circ}}(\cdot,i)\,r_{i}^{\star}\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{d}.

Write
\Delta\coloneqq w-\tilde{w}
and note
\E[v_{i}^{\star}\mid\Delta]=\Delta
.

Define the clean residuals at
\tilde{w}
by

r_{i}^{\star}\coloneqq{y^{\circ}}(i)-\langle{X^{\circ}}(\cdot,i),\tilde{w}\rangle=\langle{X^{\circ}}(\cdot,i),w-\tilde{w}\rangle+\xi_{i},\qquad\xi_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1),

and the clean vectors

v_{i}^{\star}\coloneqq{X^{\circ}}(\cdot,i)\,r_{i}^{\star}\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{d}.

Write
\Delta\coloneqq w-\tilde{w}
.
Note that for every
i
,

v_{i}^{\star}={X^{\circ}}(\cdot,i){X^{\circ}}(\cdot,i)^{\intercal}\Delta+{X^{\circ}}(\cdot,i)\,\xi_{i}.

Lemma C.7

(Small-set resilience bound for
\{v_{i}^{\star}\}
)
.

Assume
\|\Delta\|_{2}\leqslant\delta
, and let
k\coloneqq\lceil 2\eta n\rceil
.
Under the assumption that

n\gtrsim\frac{d+\log(1/\beta_{2})}{\eta},

there exists an absolute constant
C>0
such that with probability at least
1-\beta_{2}
over the clean draw
({X^{\circ}},{y^{\circ}})
,

\mathrm{Res}_{2\eta}(v^{\star})\leqslant C(1+\delta)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sqrt{\eta\log\frac{e}{\eta}}\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta_{2})}{n}}+\eta\log\frac{e}{\eta}}}\right).

Proof.

Let
x_{i}\coloneqq{X^{\circ}}(\cdot,i)
, and for
S\subseteq[n]
let
{X^{\circ}}_{S}
be the submatrix formed by the
columns indexed by
S
. Also let
\xi_{S}\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{|S|}
denote the restriction of
(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n})
to
S
.

For every
S\subseteq[n]
with
|S|\leqslant k
, using

v_{i}^{\star}=x_{i}x_{i}^{\intercal}\Delta+x_{i}\xi_{i},

we have

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in S}v_{i}^{\star}=\frac{1}{n}\,{X^{\circ}}_{S}{X^{\circ}}_{S}^{\intercal}\Delta+\frac{1}{n}\,{X^{\circ}}_{S}\xi_{S}.

Therefore,

\Bigl\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in S}v_{i}^{\star}\Bigr\|_{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{n}\|{X^{\circ}}_{S}\|_{\mathsf{op}}^{2}\,\|\Delta\|_{2}+\frac{1}{n}\|{X^{\circ}}_{S}\|_{\mathsf{op}}\,\|\xi_{S}\|_{2}.

Taking the supremum over all
|S|\leqslant k
yields

\mathrm{Res}_{2\eta}(v^{\star})\leqslant\frac{\delta}{n}\,\|{X^{\circ}}\|_{\mathrm{sp}}(k)^{2}+\frac{1}{n}\,\|{X^{\circ}}\|_{\mathrm{sp}}(k)\,\max_{|S|\leqslant k}\|\xi_{S}\|_{2}.

(60)

We now bound the two factors on the right-hand side.

First, by Lemma
C.1
, with probability at least
1-\beta_{2}/2
,

\|{X^{\circ}}\|_{\mathrm{sp}}(k)\leqslant C_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sqrt{d}+\sqrt{k\log\frac{en}{k}}+\sqrt{\log\frac{2}{\beta_{2}}}}}\right).

Hence on this event,

\frac{\delta}{n}\,\|{X^{\circ}}\|_{\mathrm{sp}}(k)^{2}\leqslant C\delta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d+\log(1/\beta_{2})}{n}+\frac{k}{n}\log\frac{en}{k}}}\right).

(61)

Second, since
(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n})\sim N(0,I_{n})
and the coordinates are nonnegative after squaring,

\max_{|S|\leqslant k}\|\xi_{S}\|_{2}^{2}=\max_{|S|=k}\sum_{i\in S}\xi_{i}^{2}.

Applying Theorem
C.2
in dimension
n
with subset size
k
,
we get that with probability at least
1-\beta_{2}/2
,

\max_{|S|\leqslant k}\|\xi_{S}\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant C_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(k\log\frac{en}{k}+\log\frac{2}{\beta_{2}}}}\right).

(62)

On the intersection of the events (
61
) and (
62
), which has probability at least
1-\beta_{2}
, the second term in (
60
) is bounded by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\,\|{X^{\circ}}\|_{\mathrm{sp}}(k)\,\max_{|S|\leqslant k}\|\xi_{S}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{C}{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sqrt{d}+\sqrt{k\log\frac{en}{k}}+\sqrt{\log\frac{1}{\beta_{2}}}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sqrt{k\log\frac{en}{k}}+\sqrt{\log\frac{1}{\beta_{2}}}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta_{2})}{n}}\,\sqrt{\frac{k\log(en/k)+\log(1/\beta_{2})}{n}}+\frac{k\log(en/k)+\log(1/\beta_{2})}{n}}}\right).

Since
k=\lceil 2\eta n\rceil
, we have
k/n\asymp\eta
and

\log\frac{en}{k}\asymp\log\frac{e}{\eta}.

Substituting these into the two bounds above and combining with (
60
) proves

\mathrm{Res}_{2\eta}(v^{\star})\leqslant C\delta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d+\log(1/\beta_{2})}{n}+\eta\log\frac{e}{\eta}}}\right)+C\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sqrt{\eta\log\frac{e}{\eta}}\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta_{2})}{n}}+\eta\log\frac{e}{\eta}+\frac{\log(1/\beta_{2})}{n}}}\right).

Finally, under the assumption that

n\gtrsim\frac{d+\log(1/\beta_{2})}{\eta},

the terms
\delta(d+\log(1/\beta_{2}))/n
and
\log(1/\beta_{2})/n
are absorbed into

C(1+\delta)\,\eta\log\frac{e}{\eta},

which gives the final error bound.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

C.5
.

Conditioned on
({X^{\circ}},{y^{\circ}})
, the posterior is Gaussian with covariance

\mathrm{Cov}(w^{*}\mid{X^{\circ}},{y^{\circ}})=({X^{\circ}}{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}+\sigma^{-2}\textup{Id}_{d})^{-1}=\frac{1}{n}(A^{\star}+\lambda\textup{Id}_{d})^{-1}.

On the event
\lambda_{\min}(A^{\star})\geqslant 1/2
, we have

(A^{\star}+\lambda\textup{Id}_{d})^{-1}\preceq 2\textup{Id}_{d}
, hence

\mathrm{Cov}(w\mid{X^{\circ}},{y^{\circ}})\preceq\frac{2}{n}\textup{Id}_{d}
.
Therefore, with probability at least
1-\beta_{3}
(possibly after adjusting constants),

\|{w^{*}}-w_{\rm post}\|_{2}\leqslant C_{5}\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta_{3})}{n}}.

(63)

Apply Lemma
C.2.1
with
\delta\coloneqq\|\Delta\|_{2}
(given the rough estimator we have
