\delta\leqslant C_{1}(\sqrt{(d+\log(1/\beta_{1}))/n}+\eta)
).
Let
\tau
be the RHS of Lemma
C.2.1
. The observed vectors

v_{i}=x_{i}r_{i}
are
\eta
-corrupted from
v_{i}^{\star}
, hence the completion-by-certificate procedure
(Definition
5
) produces
\hat{g}
satisfying

\|\hat{g}-g^{\star}(\tilde{w})\|_{2}\leqslant 2\tau

(64)

by Lemma
C.2.1
, with probability at least
1-\beta_{2}
.

Now we relate
\hat{w}
to
w_{\rm post}
and control the error from
A^{\star}-\textup{Id}_{d}
.
Using (
59
) with
u=\tilde{w}
,

w_{\rm post}=\tilde{w}+(A^{\star}+\lambda\textup{Id}_{d})^{-1}\bigl(g^{\star}(\tilde{w})-\lambda\tilde{w}\bigr).

Recall
\hat{w}=\tilde{w}+\frac{1}{1+\lambda}(\hat{g}-\lambda\tilde{w})
.
Subtracting yields

\displaystyle\hat{w}-w_{\rm post}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1+\lambda}(\hat{g}-g^{\star}(\tilde{w}))+\Big(\frac{1}{1+\lambda}\textup{Id}_{d}-(A^{\star}+\lambda\textup{Id}_{d})^{-1}\Big)\bigl(g^{\star}(\tilde{w})-\lambda\tilde{w}\bigr).

Using the resolvent identity

(A^{\star}+\lambda\textup{Id}_{d})^{-1}-\frac{1}{1+\lambda}\textup{Id}_{d}=(A^{\star}+\lambda\textup{Id}_{d})^{-1}(\textup{Id}_{d}-A^{\star})\frac{1}{1+\lambda}\textup{Id}_{d}

and
\frac{1}{1+\lambda}\leqslant 1
, we get

\|\hat{w}-w_{\rm post}\|_{2}\leqslant\|\hat{g}-g^{\star}(\tilde{w})\|_{2}+\|A^{\star}-\textup{Id}_{d}\|_{\rm op}\cdot\|w_{\rm post}-\tilde{w}\|_{2},

(65)

where we used that

(A^{\star}+\lambda\textup{Id}_{d})^{-1}(g^{\star}(\tilde{w})-\lambda\tilde{w})=w_{\rm post}-\tilde{w}

by (
59
).

Next,

\|w_{\rm post}-\tilde{w}\|_{2}\leqslant\|w_{\rm post}-w\|_{2}+\|w-\tilde{w}\|_{2}.

On the intersection of events of rough estimation error guarantee and (
63
),

\|w_{\rm post}-\tilde{w}\|_{2}\leqslant C_{6}\Big(\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}+\eta\Big).

(66)

By the concentration of Wishart matrix, we have

\|A^{\star}-\textup{Id}_{d}\|_{\rm op}\leqslant C_{4}\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}.

(67)

Combining (
66
) and (
67
),

\|A^{\star}-\textup{Id}_{d}\|_{\rm op}\cdot\|w_{\rm post}-\tilde{w}\|_{2}\leqslant C_{7}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}+\eta\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}}}\right).

Under the assumed regime
n\gtrsim(d+\log(1/\beta))/\eta
, we have

\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}\lesssim\eta
and
\eta\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}\lesssim\eta
.
Hence,

\|A^{\star}-\textup{Id}_{d}\|_{\rm op}\cdot\|w_{\rm post}-\tilde{w}\|_{2}\lesssim\eta.

(68)

Finally,
plugging (
64
) and (
68
) into (
65
),

\|\hat{w}-w_{\rm post}\|_{2}\leqslant 2\tau+O(\eta).

Finally, using
\delta=\|w^{*}-\tilde{w}\|_{2}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}+\eta
inside
Lemma
C.2.1
and absorbing the lower-order
O(\eta)
term into

\eta\log(1/\eta)
(for
\eta\leqslant 1/2
), we obtain

\|\hat{w}-w_{\rm post}\|_{2}\leqslant O\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sqrt{\eta\log\frac{1}{\eta}}\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}+\eta\log\frac{1}{\eta}}}\right)

with probability at least
1-\beta
after a union bound over the events used above.
∎

C.2.2
Robustness-to-privacy reduction

Now using the reduction from privacy to robustness for exponential time algorithms
[
HKMN23
]
, we obtain
Theorem

6.2
, restated here for convenience:

Theorem

(Restatement of
Theorem

6.2
)
.

Let
\Sigma=\sigma^{2}\textup{Id}_{d}
for known parameter
\sigma>0
.
Let
\beta\in[0,1],\varepsilon\leqslant O(1)
.
Suppose
n\geqslant\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{d\log(\sigma\sqrt{d})/\varepsilon,(d+\log(1/\beta))/\eta}}\right\}
.
Then there is a computationally inefficient algorithm that, given
\eta
-corrupted samples
(X_{\mathrm{input}},y_{\mathrm{input}})
generated as in
Section

6
, outputs an
\varepsilon
-DP estimator
\hat{w}
such that with probability at least
1-\beta
,

\lVert\hat{w}-\E[{w^{*}}\mid{X^{\circ}},{y^{\circ}}]\rVert_{2}\leqslant\tilde{O}(\eta+\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n\varepsilon})\,,

where
({X^{\circ}},{y^{\circ}})
are the uncorrupted samples.

Proof.

By
Theorem

C.5
, we have an inefficient algorithm which achieves error rate
\eta\log(1/\eta)
when
n\geqslant\tilde{\Omega}(d/\eta)
.
Using the blackbox privacy-robustness reduction (
[
HKMN23
, Lemma 2.1]
), we have a private algorithm which achieves error
\alpha
when

n\geqslant\tilde{\Omega}\Bigl(\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{\alpha\varepsilon}+\frac{d\log(\sigma\sqrt{d})}{\varepsilon}+\frac{(d+\log(1/\beta))\eta}{\alpha^{2}}\Bigr)\,.

under the condition that
\alpha\geqslant\eta\log(1/\eta)
.
Now taking
\alpha=\eta\log(1/\eta)+\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n\varepsilon}\log\Bigl(\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n\varepsilon}\Bigr)
, we have the claim of
Theorem

6.2
.
∎

C.3
Proof of
Section

6.2.2
: Robust regression with constant error rate

Here we provide the details of the rough estimation procedure for robust regression to constant error in the weak prior regime. The result is stated below for convenience:

Lemma

(Restatement of
Section

6.2.2
)
.

Let
\Sigma=\sigma^{2}\textup{Id}_{d}
for known parameter
\sigma=\omega(1/\sqrt{n})
. There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given
\eta
-corrupted samples
(X,y)
generated as in
Section

6
, outputs
\hat{w}
such that with probability at least
1-\beta
, we have
\lVert\hat{w}-{w^{*}}\rVert_{2}\leqslant O(1)
provided that
n\geqslant\Omega((d+\log(1/\beta))/\eta)
.

Recall the definition of the program
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{\rm corr}\cup\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{res}}^{\prime}
, where
\mathcal{A}_{\rm corr}
is defined in
Eq.

23
and
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{res}}^{\prime}
is defined in
Eq.

26
. Formally, our algorithm is described in
Algorithm

6
.

Algorithm 6

(Robust algorithm for regression with constant error)
.

Input:

\eta
corrupted matrix
X_{\text{input}}\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{n\times m},y_{\text{input}}\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{n}

Output:
a polynomial time estimator
\hat{w}\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{d}

1.

Solve the degree-
O(1)
SoS relaxation of the constraints
\mathcal{A}(X,y,\xi,w;X_{\mathrm{input}},y_{\mathrm{input}})=\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{corr}}(X,y,\xi)\cup\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{res}}^{\prime}(X,y,w)
, where
t=O(1)
,
obtaining a pseudo-expectation operator.

2.

Round the pseudo-distribution using
\tilde{\E}[w]
.

Proof of
Section

6.2.2
.

First, as proved by
Theorem

C.3
, the constraints in the sum-of-squares program are satisfied with probability at least
1-\beta
.
Next, we give the sum-of-squares identifiability proof.
Let
r\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{0,1}}\right\}^{n}
be the set of corruptions. Let
v=1-(1-\xi)\odot(1-r)
denote the indicator for the points which were corrupted and/or were marked by
\xi
as corrupted.
Then we have

(y-X^{\intercal}w)\odot(1-v)=({y^{\circ}}-{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}w)\odot(1-v)\,,

so

({y^{\circ}}-{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}w)-(y-X^{\intercal}w)=({y^{\circ}}-{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}w)\odot v-(y-X^{\intercal}w)\odot v\,.

We can bound the second term on the right-hand side using the short-flat decomposition of
y-X^{\intercal}w
:

\lVert(y-X^{\intercal}w)\odot v\rVert^{2}\leqslant\lVert z_{1}\rVert^{2}+2\eta n\lVert z_{2}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\leqslant\eta n\log(1/\eta)+\log(1/\beta)\,.

On the other hand, we have

{y^{\circ}}-{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}w={y^{\circ}}-{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}{w^{*}}+{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}({w^{*}}-w)

As the ground truth is feasible,
{y^{\circ}}-{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}w^{*}
also admits a short-flat decomposition, so

\lVert({y^{\circ}}-{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}{w^{*}})\odot v\rVert^{2}\leqslant\eta n\log(1/\eta)+\log(1/\beta)\,.

Therefore it remains to bound

\lVert({X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}(w-{w^{*}}))\odot v\rVert^{2}
.
We have the sum-of-squares upper bound

\lVert({X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}(w-{w^{*}}))\odot v\rVert^{2}=\sum_{i\in[n]}v_{i}\langle{X^{\circ}}(\cdot,i),w-{w^{*}}\rangle^{2}\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert w-{w^{*}}}}\right\rVert^{2}\,,

where the second step follows by the selector lemma (
Section

4.3.2
).
By triangle inequality, we then have

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert{y^{\circ}}-{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}w}}\right\rVert^{2}\leqslant 2\eta n\log(1/\eta)\lVert w-{w^{*}}\rVert^{2}+2n\,.

We then have

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}w-{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}{w^{*}}}}\right\rVert^{2}\leqslant 2\eta n\log(1/\eta)\lVert w-{w^{*}}\rVert^{2}+4n\,,

Since we have
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\frac{1}{n}{X^{\circ}}{X^{\circ}}^{\intercal}-\textup{Id}}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}}
, the pseudo-distribution satisfies

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert w-{w^{*}}}}\right\rVert^{2}\leqslant O(1)\,.

By standard rounding, we obtain an estimator
\tilde{\E}[w]
which achieves constant error rate.
∎

C.4
Privatizing robust regression algorithms under weak prior

C.4.1
Privatizing the rough estimator from Stage 1

In this part, we privatize our algorithms for
Algorithm

6
.
We prove the following theorem.

Theorem C.8
.

There is a polynomial-time
\varepsilon
-DP algorithm that, given an
\eta
-corrupted sample
(X_{\mathrm{in}},y_{\mathrm{in}})
, outputs
\widehat{w}
with

\E\bigl[\|\widehat{w}-{w^{*}}\|_{2}^{2}\bigr]\ \leqslant\ O(1)

as long as

n\ \geqslant\ \widetilde{\Omega}\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d\,\log(\sigma\sqrt{d})}{\varepsilon}}}\right).

Moreover, via concentration of
w_{\mathrm{out}}
around the true Bayesian posterior mean
\E[{w^{*}}\mid{X^{\circ}},{y^{\circ}}]
, the same bound holds (up to lower-order terms) for
\E\|\widehat{w}-\E[{w^{*}}\mid{X^{\circ}},{y^{\circ}}]\|_{2}^{2}
.

Definition C.9

((
\alpha,\tau,T
)-certifiable regressor )
.

Fix
R>0
and
\alpha,\tau>0
. For an input
(X_{\mathrm{in}},y_{\mathrm{in}})
, a vector
\widetilde{w}\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{d}

is
(\alpha,\tau,T)
-certifiable
if there exists a degree-
O(1)
linear functional
L
(a pseudoexpectation) over indeterminates
(X,y,\xi,w)
such that:

1.

Closeness/boundedness:

\lVert L[w]-\widetilde{w}\rVert_{2}\leqslant\alpha
and
\lVert L[w]\rVert_{2}\leqslant 2R+\tau T
.

2.

Approximate feasibility (degree-6):

L

\tau
-approximately satisfies the union of constraint families

\mathcal{A}\;=\;\underbrace{\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{corr}}}_{\text{corruption}}\ \cup\ \underbrace{\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{res}}}_{\text{design/residual stability}}

concretely:

•

\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{corr}}
:
\xi\odot\xi=\xi
,
\|\xi\|_{1}\leqslant\eta n
and
(1-\xi(i))X(i,\cdot)=(1-\xi(i))X_{\mathrm{in}}(i,\cdot)
,
(1-\xi(i))y(i)=(1-\xi(i))y_{\mathrm{in}}(i)
for all
i
.

•

\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{res}}
: degree-6 SoS surrogates for

\big\|\frac{1}{n}XX^{\intercal}-I\big\|_{\mathsf{op}}\lesssim\sqrt{(d+\log(1/\beta))/n}
and a decomposition
y-X^{\intercal}w=z_{1}+z_{2}
with

\|z_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim\eta n\log(1/\eta)+\log(1/\beta)
and
\|z_{2}\|_{\infty}\lesssim\log(1/\eta)
.

We choose
R=\Theta(\sigma\sqrt{d})
.

Lemma C.10

(Low-score sets localize near
w
)
.

Let
\eta_{\star}=R
.
Let

\alpha(\eta)^{2}\ \lesssim\ \eta^{2}\log\!\tfrac{1}{\eta}\ +\ \eta\cdot\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}\,.

Then the following hold simultaneously.

1.

Completeness (inner ball).
If
\lVert\widetilde{w}-w_{\mathrm{out}}\rVert_{2}\leqslant\alpha(\eta)
then
\mathcal{S}(\widetilde{w};\cdot)\leqslant\eta n
.

2.

Soundness (outer ball).
If
\mathcal{S}(\widetilde{w};\cdot)\leqslant\eta_{\star}n
then
\lVert\widetilde{w}-w_{\mathrm{out}}\rVert_{2}\lesssim\alpha(\eta_{\star})
.

Proof of
Theorem

C.8
.

It is easy to verify the quasi-convexity of the score function.
To show that the score function is well-defined for
\tilde{w}\in\mdmathbb{B}_{2}^{d}(2R+n\tau+\alpha)
, we note that we can set
y=0
and
X=0
.
Then we can set
w
be the projection of
\tilde{w}

into the ball of radius
2R+\tau T
.
Now we apply the reduction in
Theorem

4.19
.
Particularly, this leads to sample complexity

n\ \gtrsim\ \max_{\eta\leqslant\eta^{\prime}\leqslant 1}\frac{d\log\!\big((R+n\tau)/\alpha(\eta)\big)+\log(1/\beta_{\eta})}{\varepsilon}.

which concludes the proof.
∎

C.4.2
Privatizing the refined estimator from Stage 2

Finally we prove the following theorem for privately approximating the least square estimator.

Theorem C.11
.

There is a polynomial-time
\varepsilon
-DP algorithm that, given an
\eta
-corrupted sample
(X_{\mathrm{in}},y_{\mathrm{in}})
, outputs
\widehat{w}
with

\E\bigl[\|\widehat{w}-{w^{*}}\|_{2}^{2}\bigr]\ \leqslant\ O(\alpha^{2})

as long as

n\ \geqslant\ \widetilde{\Omega}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d\,\log(\sigma\sqrt{d})}{\varepsilon}}}\right).

Moreover, via concentration of
w_{\mathrm{out}}
around the true Bayesian posterior mean
\E[{w^{*}}\mid{X^{\circ}},{y^{\circ}}]
, the same bound holds (up to lower-order terms) for
\E\|\widehat{w}-\E[{w^{*}}\mid{X^{\circ}},{y^{\circ}}]\|_{2}^{2}
.

In this section, we privatize our
Algorithm

2
.
We make the following definition for certifiable posterior mean for regression.

Definition C.12

((
\alpha,\tau,T
)-certifiable posterior mean for regression)
.

Fix
R>0
and
\alpha,\tau>0
. For an input
(X_{\mathrm{in}},y_{\mathrm{in}})
, a vector
\widetilde{w}\in{\mdmathbb{R}}^{d}

is
(\alpha,\tau,T)
-certifiable
if there exists a degree-
O(1)
linear functional
L
(a pseudoexpectation) over indeterminates
(X,y,\xi,w,w_{\mathrm{out}},M)
such that:

1.

Closeness/boundedness:

\lVert L[w_{\mathrm{out}}]-\widetilde{w}\rVert_{2}\leqslant\alpha
and
\lVert L[w_{\mathrm{out}}]\rVert_{2}\leqslant 2R+\tau T
.

2.

Approximate feasibility (degree-6):

L

\tau
-approximately satisfies the union of constraint families

\mathcal{A}\;=\;\underbrace{\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{corr}}}_{\text{corruption}}\ \cup\ \underbrace{\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{res}}^{\prime\prime}}_{\text{design/residual stability}}\ \cup\ \underbrace{\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{LS}}}_{w_{\text{LS}}=\frac{1}{n}Xy}\!,

concretely:

•

\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{corr}}
:
\xi\odot\xi=\xi
,
\|\xi\|_{1}\leqslant\eta n
and
(1-\xi(i))X(i,\cdot)=(1-\xi(i))X_{\mathrm{in}}(i,\cdot)
,
(1-\xi(i))y(i)=(1-\xi(i))y_{\mathrm{in}}(i)
for all
i
.

•

\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{res}}^{\prime\prime}
: degree-6 SoS surrogates for

\big\|\frac{1}{n}XX^{\intercal}-I\big\|_{\mathsf{op}}\lesssim\sqrt{(d+\log(1/\beta))/n}
and a decomposition
y-X^{\intercal}w=z_{1}+z_{2}
with

\|z_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim\eta n\log(1/\eta)+\log(1/\beta)
and
\|z_{2}\|_{\infty}^{2}\lesssim\log(1/\eta)+\log(1/\beta)/\eta n
, also we have
X^{\intercal}(w^{*}-w_{\text{init}})=b_{1}+b_{2}
such that
\|b_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim\eta n\log(1/\eta)+\log(1/\beta)
and
\|b_{2}\|_{\infty}^{2}\lesssim\log(1/\eta)+\log(1/\beta)/\eta n
.

•

\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{LS}}
:
w_{\text{LS}}=\frac{1}{n}Xy
.

We choose
R=\Theta(\sigma\sqrt{d})
.

Theorem

(Restatement of
Theorem

6.15
)
.

Let
\Sigma=\sigma^{2}\textup{Id}_{d}
for known parameter
\sigma=\omega(1/\sqrt{n})
. Let
\beta\in[0,1]
,
\varepsilon\leqslant O(1)
. There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given
\eta
-corrupted samples
(X,y)
generated as in
Section

6
, outputs an
\varepsilon
-DP estimator
\hat{w}
such that with probability at least
1-\beta
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\hat{w}-\E[{w^{*}}\mid{X^{\circ}},{y^{\circ}}]}}\right\rVert^{2}\leqslant\alpha^{2}\,,

where
({X^{\circ}},{y^{\circ}})
are the uncorrupted samples, under the conditions that
\alpha\geqslant\eta\log(1/\eta)
and

n\geqslant\tilde{\Omega}\,\Bigl(\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{\alpha^{4/3}\varepsilon^{2/3}}+\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{\alpha\varepsilon}+\frac{d\log(\sigma\sqrt{d})}{\varepsilon}+\frac{d\eta^{2}}{\alpha^{4}}\Bigr)\,.

Proof.

Apply the robustness-to-privacy reduction to the score
\mathcal{S}
on the domain
\mdmathbb{B}_{2}^{d}(\,2R+n\tau+\alpha\,)
.
Let
V_{\eta}
denote the volume of
\{\widetilde{w}:\ \mathcal{S}(\widetilde{w})\leqslant\eta n\}
inside this domain.
By
Section

6.1.2
:

•

Local regime

\eta\leqslant\eta^{\prime}\leqslant\eta_{\star}
: low-score sets are sandwiched between
\ell_{2}
-balls around
w_{\mathrm{out}}
with radii
\alpha(\eta)
and
\alpha(\eta^{\prime})
, hence

\log\!\frac{V_{\eta^{\prime}}}{V_{\eta}}\ \lesssim\ d\cdot\log\!\frac{\alpha(\eta^{\prime})}{\alpha(\eta)}.

•

Global regime

\eta_{\star}\leqslant\eta^{\prime}\leqslant 1
: the score-
\leqslant\eta_{\star}n
set lies in an
O(\alpha(\eta_{\star}))
-ball while the ambient radius is
O(R+n\tau)
, so

\log\!\frac{V_{\eta^{\prime}}}{V_{\eta}}\ \lesssim\ d\cdot\log\!\frac{R+n\tau}{\alpha(\eta_{\star})}.

Finally it is easy to verify that the score function is quasi-convex and can be efficiently evaluated.

The reduction in
Theorem

4.19
then yields a sufficient condition

n\ \gtrsim\ \max_{\eta\leqslant\eta^{\prime}\leqslant\eta_{\star}}\frac{d\log(\alpha(\eta^{\prime})/\alpha(\eta))+\log(1/\beta_{\eta})}{\varepsilon\eta^{\prime}}\;+\;\max_{\eta_{\star}\leqslant\eta^{\prime}\leqslant 1}\frac{d\log\!\big((R+n\tau)/\alpha(\eta_{\star})\big)+\log(1/\beta_{\eta})}{\varepsilon}.

Optimizing over
\eta
using
\alpha(\eta)^{2}\asymp\eta\cdot\frac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{n}
in the small-
\eta
regime yields the two local terms

\tfrac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{\alpha^{4/3}\varepsilon^{2/3}}
and
\tfrac{d+\log(1/\beta)}{\alpha\varepsilon}
.
For the global term, take
R=\Theta(\sigma\sqrt{d})
so that the contribution is
\tfrac{d\log(\sigma\sqrt{d})}{\varepsilon}
(polylog factors suppressed).
∎
